Exponential mixing and enhanced dissipation on the unit sphere with Rossby-Haurwitz flows
Augusto Del Zotto, Marc Nualart

Abstract
. We exhibit a family of smooth incompressible velocity fields on the two-dimensional unit sphere such that the time evolution of any mean-free initial data passively advected by any of them is mixed exponentially fast. In the presence of molecular diffusivity, we show that the solution to the associated advection-diffusion equation experiences enhanced dissipation with optimal decay rates. Each member of this family is an alternating combination of two Rossby-Haurwitz flows with random amplitudes and constitutes a spherical analogue to the sine shear-alternating example of Pierrehumbert.



1.
Introduction

The evolution of a passive scalar
\rho:[0,\infty)\times{\mathrm{M}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
on a compact boundary-less surface
{\mathrm{M}}
, advected by a smooth divergence-free velocity field
u\in\mathfrak{X}({\mathrm{M}})
, and undergoing molecular diffusion with diffusivity coefficient
\kappa\geq 0
, is governed by

(1.1)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+u\cdot\nabla\rho=\kappa{\Delta}_{\mathrm{M}}\rho&{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathrm{M}},\quad t>0,\\
\rho(0,{\mathbf{x}})=\rho_{0}({\mathbf{x}}),&{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathrm{M}}.\end{cases}

Here,
\Delta_{\mathrm{M}}
denotes the Laplace-Beltrami operator on
{\mathrm{M}}
.
In this manuscript, we are interested in describing the long-time dynamics of arbitrary mean-free initial data
\rho_{0}
in terms of the incompressible vector field
u
.

The transport nature of (
1.1
) for
\kappa=0
ensures that all
L^{p}
norms of the solution are preserved, for all
p\in[1,\infty]
, where no property of
u
is used other than its incompressibility.
However, we do observe in everyday phenomena that some stirring velocity fields are more effective than others in mixing initial passive scalars, one of the most instructive examples being the mixing of milk in the morning’s cup of coffee.
This stretching and folding of
\rho
at time
t
can be measured in terms of homogeneous negative Sobolev norms,

(1.2)

\displaystyle\|\rho\|_{\dot{H}^{-s}}^{2}=\sum_{k>0}|\lambda_{k}|^{-2s}|\rho_{k}(t)|^{2},

where
(\lambda_{k})_{k\geq 0}
are the eigenvalues of
(-\Delta_{{\mathrm{M}}})
and
\rho_{k}
denote the Fourier coefficients of
\rho
in the basis given by the eigenfunctions of
(-\Delta_{{\mathrm{M}}})
.
By duality and due to the conservation of
L^{2}
norm, any decay of (
1.2
) yields the transfer of
L^{2}
mass of
\rho(t)
to higher and higher frequencies, a testament of mixing of the passive scalar.
In this work the exponential mixing is understood as the exponential decay in time of the negative Sobolev norm (
1.2
).

In parallel, when
\kappa>0
, combining the classical energy equality

(1.3)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\mathrm{M}}|\rho(t,{\mathbf{x}})|^{2}\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{x}}=-\kappa\int_{{\mathrm{M}}}|\nabla\rho(t,{\mathbf{x}})|^{2}\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{x}}

together with the Poincaré inequality shows that

(1.4)

\displaystyle\|\rho(t,\cdot)-\overline{\rho}\|_{L^{2}}\leq e^{-\lambda_{1}\kappa t}\|\rho_{0}-\overline{\rho}\|_{L^{2}},

namely the solution to (
1.1
) converges exponentially fast to the average
\overline{\rho}=\int_{\mathrm{M}}\rho_{0}({\mathbf{x}})\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{x}}
of its initial datum
1
1

1

We remark that
\overline{\rho}
is a stationary solution of (
1.1
).
, with exponent
-\lambda_{1}\kappa
where
\lambda_{1}>0
is the first non-zero eigenvalue of
-\Delta_{\mathrm{M}}
.

However, this simple bound does not take into account any possible mixing of the solution
\rho(t)
: the creation of large gradients of
\rho
due to the stretching and folding produced by
u
should transfer most of the energy of
\rho
to large frequencies, where the diffusive operator
\kappa\Delta_{\mathrm{M}}
is most effective and significantly improves the rate of convergence of the solution to its average.
To quantify this improvement, we say that
u
enhances dissipation if there exists
\mathsf{d}:[0,1)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
with

(1.5)

\displaystyle\lim_{\kappa\rightarrow 0}\frac{\kappa}{\mathsf{d}(\kappa)}=0

and a constant
C>0
such that the unique solution
\rho(t,x)
to (
1.1
) with initial data
\rho(0,{\mathbf{x}})=\rho_{0}({\mathbf{x}})
satisfies

(1.6)

\displaystyle\|\rho(t,\cdot)-\overline{\rho}\|_{L^{2}}\leq Ce^{-\mathsf{d}(\kappa)t}\|\rho_{0}-\overline{\rho}\|_{L^{2}},

for all
t>0
.

In this work we consider
\mathrm{M}={{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, the two-dimensional unit sphere, and we exhibit an incompressible velocity field on
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
that is a uniform-in-
\kappa
exponential mixer and enhances dissipation.
To the best of our knowledge, this is the first explicit construction of such a velocity field on the two-dimensional unit sphere.
Inspired by the example of Pierrehumbert
[
38
]
on
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
, we define a vector field on
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
that consists of alternating zonal Rossby-Haurwitz waves of degree 2 with randomized strengths.
These zonal flows are the spherical analogue of the sine shearing profile on
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
(see Figure
1
and Figure
2
), and their chaotic transport properties were already investigated by Pierrehumbert in the
\beta
-plane approximation
[
39
,
38
]
, see also
[
29
]
.

We show that for almost all noise realizations, such a time-dependent vector field exponentially mixes and enhances dissipation of any passive tracer
\rho
solving (
1.1
) for small enough diffusivities
\kappa\geq 0
.
See Figures
3
and
4
for numerical simulations that illustrate the two phenomena analysed in this paper.

1.1.
Lagrangian dynamics and construction of vector field

The solution to the advection-diffusion equation (
1.1
) is studied in terms of the Lagrangian dynamics generated by the convective velocity field
u\in\mathfrak{X}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
and the Laplace-Beltrami operator
\Delta_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
.
Let
\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})
denote the flow map associated to the infinitesimal generator of
u\cdot\nabla-\kappa\Delta_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
on
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, which satisfies the stochastic differential equation

(1.7)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})=u(t,\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}))\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2\kappa}\Pi_{\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})}\circ\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{B}}_{t},&\\
\phi_{0}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})={\mathbf{x}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}},\end{cases}

in the Stratonovich sense, where
\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{B}}_{t}=(\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{1},\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{2},\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{3})
denotes a standard Brownian motion on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
and
\Pi_{\mathbf{x}}=I-{\mathbf{x}}\otimes{\mathbf{x}}
stands for the usual orthogonal projection from
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
to
T_{\mathbf{x}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, for all
{\mathbf{x}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
.
The associated Itô formulation reads

(1.8)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})=v_{\kappa}(t,\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}))\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2\kappa}\Pi_{\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})}\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{B}}_{t},&\\
\phi_{0}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})={\mathbf{x}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}},\end{cases}

where
v_{\kappa}(t,{\mathbf{x}})=u(t,{\mathbf{x}})-2\kappa{\mathbf{x}}
encodes the Itô correction.
Then, for any
\rho_{0}:{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
, the solution
\rho(t,{\mathbf{x}})
to the advection-diffusion equation (
1.1
) is given in terms of the Feynman-Kac formula by

(1.9)

\displaystyle\rho(t,{\mathbf{x}}):={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left[(\phi_{t}^{\kappa})_{\#}\rho_{0}({\mathbf{x}})\right],

where
(\phi_{t}^{\kappa})_{\#}g({\mathbf{x}})=g\circ(\phi_{t}^{\kappa})^{-1}({\mathbf{x}})
, for all measurable functions
g
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
denote a unit direction axis on
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
and
\omega\in{\mathbb{R}}
. We define
f_{\omega}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\mathbf{x}})
, for all
{\mathbf{x}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, as the time-
1
flow map of (
1.8
) for
\kappa=0
given by the vector field

(1.10)

u_{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{a}}({\mathbf{x}}):=\omega u^{\boldsymbol{a}}({\mathbf{x}}),\quad u^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\mathbf{x}}):=({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})({\boldsymbol{a}}\times{\mathbf{x}}).

Figure 1
.
Visual representation of
u^{e_{3}}
(on the left) and
u^{e_{2}}
(on the right). Black arrows encode direction and strength of vector fields, blue continuous line and dots (equator and poles) indicate zero-level sets, and the red oblique line represents the velocity profile (omitted on the right).

The vector field
u^{\boldsymbol{a}}
is, up to a constant, the velocity field associated with the Rossby-Haurwitz wave
Y_{2}^{0}({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})
, where
Y_{2}^{0}
denotes the zonal spherical harmonic of degree 2.
As such,
u^{e_{3}}
is the most direct analogue on the sphere of the sine shear flow
(\sin(y),0)
on
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
.
The velocity field
u_{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{a}}
is the infinitesimal generator on
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
of the rotation of angle
\omega({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})
around the
{\boldsymbol{a}}
axis, see Figure
2
.

Figure 2
.
Visual representation of the shearing effect of the flow map
f^{e_{3}}_{\omega}
on red and blue strips for amplitudes
\omega=0,1,6
respectively.

To obtain exponential mixing, in this manuscript we consider time-dependent velocity fields that consist of alternating between two distinct autonomous fields with randomly chosen amplitudes.
More precisely, for a fixed sufficiently large real number
N>1
, we consider two independent and identically distributed random variables
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\sim\text{Unif}([-N,N])
.
The probability space where the random variables take values is defined to be

(1.11)

\displaystyle\Omega_{0}=[-N,N]^{2},\quad\mathcal{F}_{0}=\mathcal{B}([-N,N]^{2}),\quad{\mathbb{P}}_{0}=\text{Unif}([-N,N]^{2}).

Then, for two fixed non-parallel rotation axes
{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, we consider the composition flow map

(1.12)

\displaystyle f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}}):=f_{\omega_{2}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\circ f_{\omega_{1}}^{{\boldsymbol{b}}}({\mathbf{x}})

for
\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in[-N,N]^{2}
.
At the
j
-th iteration, for
\underline{\omega}_{j}=(\underline{\omega}_{j,1},\underline{\omega}_{j,2})\in\Omega_{0}
we obtain the composition

(1.13)

f_{\underline{\omega}_{j}}({\mathbf{x}})=f_{{\underline{\omega}_{j,2}}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\circ f_{{\underline{\omega}_{j,1}}}^{{\boldsymbol{b}}}\circ f_{\underline{\omega}_{j-1}}({\mathbf{x}}).

The corresponding velocity field
u
depends on the noise path
\underline{\omega}=(\underline{\omega}_{1},\underline{\omega}_{2},...)\in\underline{\Omega}=\Omega_{0}^{\mathbb{N}}
and is given by

(1.14)

\displaystyle u(t,{\mathbf{x}},\underline{\omega};{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}}):=\sum_{i\geq 0}u^{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\underline{\omega}_{i,1}}({\mathbf{x}})\mathbf{1}_{[2i,2i+1)}(t)+\sum_{i\geq 0}u^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\underline{\omega}_{i,2}}({\mathbf{x}})\mathbf{1}_{[2i+1,2i+2)}(t).

In particular,
u(t,{\mathbf{x}},\underline{\omega};{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})
is a time-
2
vector field alternating between the two Rossby-Haurwitz velocity fields
u^{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
u^{\boldsymbol{b}}
with random amplitudes
\underline{\omega}
and the solution
\phi_{t}:=\phi_{t}^{0}
to (
1.8
) for
\kappa=0
is such that
\phi_{t}({\mathbf{x}})=f_{\underline{\omega}}^{n}({\mathbf{x}})
for all
t=2n
with
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Figure 3
.
Inviscid evolution for the alternating Rossby-Haurwitz flow on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
with
{\boldsymbol{a}}=e_{2}
,
{\boldsymbol{b}}=e_{3}
,
\kappa=0
,
N=4
, and
40000
particles. Snapshots are at times
t=0,2,4,8
from left to right. The sampled phase pairs by period are
(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=(+3.292,-1.490)
,
(+1.204,+2.502)
,
(-3.164,+0.079)
, and
(-0.361,-3.023)
.

When
\kappa>0
, to define the process
\phi_{t}^{\kappa}
we introduce a probability space
(\Omega_{\mathbf{B}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbf{B}})
and we let
{\mathbf{B}}_{t}=(B_{t}^{1},B_{t}^{2},B_{t}^{3})
be an
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
-valued Brownian motion on this space.
The probability space for the process
\phi_{t}^{\kappa}
solving (
1.8
) with vector field
u(t,\cdot,\underline{\omega};{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})
is taken to be
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},{\mathbb{P}})
, where
\Omega
denotes the product space
\Omega=\Omega_{\mathbf{B}}\times\underline{\Omega}
with
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{B}}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\mathbb{N}}
and probability measure
{\mathbb{P}}:={\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbf{B}}\otimes{\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{\mathbb{N}}
.
We shall treat random variables on each coordinate space
\Omega_{0}^{\mathbb{N}}
,
\Omega_{\mathbf{B}}
as random variables on the product space
\Omega
, and we may treat
\underline{\omega}
and
{\mathbf{B}}_{t}
as independent processes on
\Omega
.
Just as for the deterministic setting, for
\kappa>0
and for
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
and
\omega\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we define
f^{{\boldsymbol{a}},\kappa}_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}})
as the time-1 flow map of (
1.8
) given by the vector field
u^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\omega}
.
We then consider the stochastic composition

(1.15)

\displaystyle f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}):=f_{\omega_{2}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}},\kappa}\circ f_{\omega_{1}}^{{\boldsymbol{b}},\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})

for
\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in[-N,N]^{2}
and
{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
.
Similarly, at the
j
-th iteration, for
\underline{\omega}_{j}=(\underline{\omega}_{j,1},\underline{\omega}_{j,2})\in\Omega_{0}
we have

(1.16)

f_{\underline{\omega}_{j}}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})=f_{{\underline{\omega}_{j,2}}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}},\kappa}\circ f_{{\underline{\omega}_{j,1}}}^{{\boldsymbol{b}},\kappa}\circ f_{\underline{\omega}_{j-1}}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}).

Figure 4
.
Viscous evolution for the alternating Rossby-Haurwitz flow on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
with
{\boldsymbol{a}}=e_{2}
,
{\boldsymbol{b}}=e_{3}
,
\kappa=0.0005
,
N=4
, and
40000
particles. Snapshots are at times
t=0,2,4,8
from left to right. The same sampled phase pairs as in the inviscid figure are used:
(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=(+3.292,-1.490)
,
(+1.204,+2.502)
,
(-3.164,+0.079)
, and
(-0.361,-3.023)
.

1.2.
Main results

The primary result of this work is an almost-sure exponential decay of correlations for the stochastic Lagrangian dynamics generated by the amplitude-randomized alternating Rossby-Haurwitz flows.
More precisely, for every pair of non-parallel unit axes
{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, we show that the flow associated with
u(t,{\mathbf{x}},\underline{\omega};{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})
exhibits exponential decay of correlations between arbitrary regular mean-free observables.
The estimate is uniform for all sufficiently small diffusivities
\kappa>0
, and the random prefactor has moments bounded independently of
\kappa
.
The precise statement is as follows.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
be two non-parallel unit axes. Let
\phi_{t}^{\kappa}
be the flow defined by the Itô SDE (
1.8
), with
\kappa\geq 0
and
u(t,\cdot,\underline{\omega};{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})
the Pierrehumbert amplitude-randomized alternating vector field given by (
1.14
).
For any
q,s>0
, there exists
\kappa_{0}>0
, a random constant
\mathrm{C}_{\underline{\omega},\kappa}\geq 1
independent of
{\mathbf{B}}
and a deterministic
\kappa
-independent
\lambda_{s}>0
such that for all mean-free functions
\varphi,\psi\in H^{s}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
, we have almost surely

(1.17)

\left|\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}\varphi({\mathbf{x}})\psi(\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})){\rm d}{\mathbf{x}}\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{\underline{\omega},\kappa}e^{-\lambda_{s}t}\|\varphi\|_{H^{s}}\|\psi\|_{H^{s}},

for all
t>0
and all
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0})
.
Moreover, there exists a
\kappa
-independent constant
\overline{\mathrm{C}}_{q}>0
such that
\mathbb{E}_{\omega}|\mathrm{C}_{\underline{\omega},\kappa}|^{q}\leq\overline{\mathrm{C}}_{q}
.

The first consequence of Theorem
1.1
is the corresponding quantitative mixing estimate for solutions of the passive scalar equation, which follows by the standard duality between decay of correlations and decay of homogeneous negative Sobolev norms.

Corollary 1.2
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
be two non-parallel unit axes. Let
\rho_{0}\in H^{s}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
be mean-free and let
\rho(t,x)
be the solution of (
1.1
).
Then, there exists
\kappa_{0}>0
such that for all
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0})
and all
s>0
there holds

(1.18)

\displaystyle\|\rho(t,\cdot)\|_{\dot{H}^{-s}}\leq\mathrm{C}_{\underline{\omega},\kappa}e^{-\lambda_{s}t}\|\rho_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}

almost surely for all
t\geq 0
.

The second consequence concerns the viscous problem.
When
\kappa>0
, the uniform-in-
\kappa
mixing estimate of Corollary
1.2
yields an enhanced dissipation estimate for the advection-diffusion equation.
The mechanism is recalled in Remark
1.4
below.

Corollary 1.3
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
be two non-parallel unit axes. Let
\rho_{0}\in H^{1}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
be mean-free and
\rho(t,{\mathbf{x}})
solves (
1.1
).
Then, there exists
\lambda>0
and
\kappa_{0}>0
such that for all
\kappa\in(0,\kappa_{0})
there exists a random constant
\mathrm{C}_{\underline{\omega},\kappa}^{\prime}\geq 1
independent of
{\mathbf{B}}
and an almost surely finite random constant
\mu(\kappa)>0
such that
{\mathbb{E}}_{\omega}[|\mathrm{C}_{\underline{\omega},\kappa}^{\prime}|^{2}]\leq\mathrm{C}^{\prime}_{2}
for some
\mathrm{C}_{2}^{\prime}\geq 1
uniformly in
\kappa
and there holds

(1.19)

\displaystyle\|\rho(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}\leq\min\left\{\frac{\mathrm{C}^{\prime}_{\underline{\omega},\kappa}}{\sqrt{\kappa}}e^{-\lambda t},e^{-\mu(\kappa)t}\right\}\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{2}}

almost surely for all
t\geq 0
. Moreover,

(1.20)

\displaystyle\lim_{\kappa\rightarrow 0}\mu(\kappa)\log\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}\right)\in\left(\frac{2\lambda}{1+M},2\lambda\right)

with probability at least
1-e^{2}\kappa_{0}^{M}{\mathrm{C}}^{\prime}_{2}
, for all
M\geq 0
.

Theorem
1.1
and Corollaries
1.2
and
1.3
give, to the best of our knowledge, the first example of a smooth universal exponentially mixing and enhanced-dissipating velocity field on a two-dimensional compact surface without boundary different from
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
.
The qualification universal refers to the fact that the same velocity field mixes every mean-free initial datum in the stated class.

Remark 1.4
.

We record a few comments clarifying the role of the random amplitudes and the scope of the main statements for the passive scalar equation.

(i)

The estimate in Theorem
1.1
is pointwise with respect to the random amplitudes. The random prefactor
\mathrm{C}_{\underline{\omega},\kappa}
may depend on the realization of the amplitude sequence and on
\kappa
, but it is independent of the Brownian motion
{\mathbf{B}}
, and its moments with respect to
\underline{\omega}
are bounded uniformly as
\kappa\to 0
.
The exponential rate
\lambda_{s}
is deterministic and independent of
\kappa
. The proof follows the random dynamical systems strategy of
[
5
]
in the inviscid case and its uniform-in-diffusivity extension in
[
10
]
. In Section
2
, we recall the abstract framework and explain how it applies to the present spherical model.

(ii)

As in
[
5
]
, the piecewise-in-time vector field
u(t,\cdot,\underline{\omega};{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})
can be made smooth in time by a suitable time reparametrization, for instance by using the construction of
[
43
]
*page 1914. This smoothing does not make the flow time-periodic: the use of independent random amplitudes at each step is essential to the random dynamical systems framework used below.

(iii)

For the inviscid dynamics underlying Theorem
1.1
, multiplying
u^{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
u^{\boldsymbol{b}}
by the random amplitudes is equivalent to alternating between the two deterministic Rossby-Haurwitz vector fields for random signed times (with the convention that negative times correspond to flowing with
-u^{\boldsymbol{a}}
or
-u^{\boldsymbol{b}}
forwards in time). Thus the randomness enters through the signed duration of each elementary shearing motion.

(iv)

Theorem
1.1
holds for every pair of non-parallel axes
{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
. In particular, the two families of streamlines generated by the elementary flows
u^{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
u^{\boldsymbol{b}}
need not be orthogonal. Whether deterministically alternating between two non-parallel
and non-orthogonal
smooth shearing flows generically produces exponential mixing remains an outstanding open problem.

(v)

The mixing estimate in Corollary
1.2
has the optimal exponential time decay among velocity fields with uniformly bounded Lipschitz norm, see
[
18
,
28
,
40
]
. Such optimal decay rates cannot be achieved by smooth two-dimensional autonomous divergence-free velocity fields, since they mix only at algebraic rates, see
[
6
]
.

(vi)

Corollary
1.3
follows from the uniform-in-
\kappa
stochastic decay of correlations, rather than from a deterministic inviscid mixing estimate alone. More precisely, Proposition
2.9
gives the pointwise estimate (
2.37
) with a random prefactor whose moments are bounded independently of
\kappa
. This is the key ingredient used, following
[
10
]
, to obtain the enhanced dissipation estimate for the advection-diffusion equation. The underlying intuition is that mixing transfers information/energy to high frequencies where the diffusive term
\kappa\Delta_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}
is most effective.

(vii)

The enhanced dissipation estimate in Corollary
1.3
has the optimal logarithmic scale for uniformly-in-time Lipschitz velocity fields; see
[
41
]
. More precisely, the global-in-time enhanced dissipation rate is of order
|\log\kappa|^{-1}
as
\kappa\to 0
, while the first term in the estimate of Corollary
1.3
shows that, after a logarithmic transient time, the decay rate becomes independent of
\kappa
, see
[
37
]
for more details.

1.3.
Context and contributions

The study of mixing properties of divergence-free vector fields on solutions to the advection-diffusion equation (
1.1
) has seen a huge development over the last two decades. Quantifying mixing in terms of negative Sobolev norms (also called functional mixing) as in (
1.2
) was first introduced in
[
35
]
, and later revisited in
[
33
]
. While there are other, more geometrical, notions of mixing, see
[
42
]
, that are equivalent to the decay in time of homogeneous negative Sobolev norms in special cases, in this manuscript we consider functional mixing.

It is by now a classical result that for sufficiently regular vector fields mixing cannot be faster than exponential, see
[
18
]
, and also
[
28
,
40
]
. This exponential decay rate was first proved to be sharp in
[
43
,
2
]
, where exponentially mixing velocity fields
u
in
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
specifically tailored to the initial data
\rho_{0}
were exhibited. The question of universal mixers (a velocity field that mixes all mean-free initial data) was subsequently first resolved in
[
22
]
, see also
[
21
]
where deterministic alternating Lipschitz vector fields on
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
are shown to mix exponentially fast. Introducing stochasticity in the system may enhance the chaotic dynamics generated by
u
. Indeed, universal exponential mixers can also be obtained from almost every solution to the forced stochastic Navier-Stokes equations, see
[
4
,
11
]
.
It has also been recently shown in
[
37
]
that a cellular flow in
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
with a randomly moving centre is a further instance of an exponentially mixing vector field.

The examples above differ in several aspects: while some are tailored to a prescribed initial datum, some are not smooth in space, some are not uniformly bounded in time, and others rely on stochastic forcing. To address these caveats, in
[
5
]
the authors established the existence of space-time smooth and uniformly bounded universal exponential mixers on
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
by developing a robust random dynamical systems framework.
It was shown there that the Pierrehumbert model
[
38
]
, which consists of alternating periodic sine shear flows with randomized phases, is an almost-surely exponential mixer.
This approach was later applied in
[
16
]
to show that the ABC flows with randomized phases and amplitudes in
{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
are exponentially mixing. It was also used in
[
12
]
to show that the Pierrehumbert example in
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
with randomized time-intervals is exponentially mixing as well.

The present work reproduces this Pierrehumbert-type mechanism on the sphere.
The elementary building blocks are zonal Rossby-Haurwitz vector fields. They are natural spherical analogues of shear flows that arise in large-scale atmospheric dynamics, including stratospheric flows (see, for instance,
[
7
,
8
,
20
]
), and, in this sense, the amplitude-randomized alternating vector field
u(t,{\mathbf{x}},\underline{\omega};{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})
defined in (
1.14
) should be viewed as the spherical counterpart of the random-time alternating sine-shear models studied in
[
38
,
5
,
12
]
.

In the presence of molecular diffusivity
\kappa>0
, enhanced dissipation for time-independent velocity fields
u
takes place if
u\cdot\nabla
has no eigenfunction in
H^{1}
, see
[
9
,
45
,
31
]
. We refer the interested reader to
[
13
,
14
,
17
]
and references therein for the relation between mixing and enhanced dissipation. While a heuristic argument suggests that exponentially mixing flows
u
should produce a rate
\mathsf{d}(\kappa)\geq C|\log(\kappa)|^{-1}
, it has only been rigorously shown in
[
14
,
25
]
that exponentially mixing flows
u
produce at least a rate
\mathsf{d}(\kappa)\geq C|\log(\kappa)|^{-2}
. The optimal rate
\mathsf{d}(\kappa)\geq C|\log(\kappa)|^{-1}
is recovered if one further assumes that
u
is Lipschitz in space uniformly in time, see
[
41
]
. The flows constructed in
[
3
,
16
,
21
,
10
]
are uniform-in-
\kappa
exponentially mixing and obtain the optimal enhanced dissipation rate
O(|\log\kappa|^{-1})
in view of
[
41
]
. Another instance of stochastic vector fields that mix exponentially fast uniformly in diffusivity and enhance dissipation is provided by the Kraichnan model, see
[
26
,
34
,
15
]
.

We now comment on the specific difficulties that arise in the spherical setting and are not present in the previous works
[
38
,
5
,
12
]
.
From a technical point of view, proving exponential mixing and enhanced dissipation on
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
requires new arguments tailored both to the geometry of the sphere and to the specific structure of the Rossby-Haurwitz vector fields
u^{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
u^{\boldsymbol{b}}
.
Indeed, the passage from
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
to
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
is not merely geometric: the presence and nature of stagnation points, global symmetries, and the non-parallelizability of the tangent bundle changes the structure of the one-point, projective, and two-point dynamics.
Accordingly, the proof cannot be obtained by directly adapting the torus arguments.
The main new technical points are the following:

(i)

The existence of a set of fixed points
F
on
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
for almost every realization of
u(t,\cdot,\underline{\omega};{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})
. The one-point process is thus not uniformly geometrically ergodic as there is no unique stationary measure on
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
. Hence, a Lyapunov-Foster drift condition must be satisfied for a Lyapunov function specifically designed to capture the dynamics of
u(t,\cdot,\underline{\omega};{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})
near the fixed points. These dynamics are, in some cases, not uniformly hyperbolic, and they must be stable enough to be persistent in the presence of diffusivity
\kappa>0
.

(ii)

The existence of other invariant sets for the two-point process other than the diagonal due to symmetries of the sphere. Just as for the one-point process, these sets have to be determined to construct a Lyapunov-Foster drift function for the two-point process.

(iii)

The open small set condition for non-orthogonal axes. The open small set condition for the one-point, two-point and projective processes is ensured once these processes are shown to be submersions for specific choices of noise parameters, see Lemma
2.4
below. In previous constructions
[
5
,
16
,
12
]
the authors exhibited specific sequences of noise parameters for which the relevant processes were submersions. In the spherical setting we consider, and especially when the axes
{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
{\boldsymbol{b}}
are non-orthogonal, it is remarkably more difficult to find specific noise parameters with the submersion property due to the complexity of the processes arising from
u(t,\cdot,\underline{\omega};{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})
.
Instead, we propose a distinct approach based on the Lie Algebra Rank Condition. In short, we derive a framework that ensures the submersion property of the relevant processes if the associated vector fields and their nested commutators have full rank in the associated tangent spaces. Such framework is fundamental when proving the positivity of the top Lyapunov exponent. See Section
4
for more details.

(iv)

The presence of diffusivity breaks the Lagrangian deterministic dynamics. To show the uniform-in-
\kappa
decay of correlations in Theorem
1.1
by means of a quantitative Harris theorem, we need to devise a drift function for the stochastic two-point process that satisfies a Lyapunov-Foster condition with
\kappa
-independent constants. Such function must take into account the dynamics of the stochastic one-point process near the fixed points, where there is a competition between the Brownian noise and the inviscid dynamics. We put forward a careful analysis near the fixed points and distinguish several regimes according to whether the processes are driven by either the deterministic dynamics or by the Brownian motion. We refer the reader to Section
11
for more details.

Organization of the paper

We begin in Section
2
by introducing the one-point, projective, and two-point Markov transition kernels that are the primary object of study in this manuscript. We also provide there the main theoretical background on Random Dynamical Systems for establishing geometric ergodicity of the three Markov chains and the positivity of the top Lyapunov exponent by means of an abstract Harris Theorem and Furstenberg’s criterion. These abstract results require the respective Markov chains to satisfy specific conditions closely related to the chaotic behaviour of the associated dynamical systems. These conditions may be verified by means of intermediate results that are presented in Section
2
as well.

These intermediate results need a precise understanding of the differential geometric structures of the two-dimensional sphere, which is carried out in Section
3
. In Section
4
we set up a novel scheme to verify the existence of an open small set (one of the conditions for the abstract Harris Theorem) and the positivity of the top Lyapunov exponent. Next, Sections
5
,
6
and
8
show that the one-point, projective, and two-point processes satisfy the conditions of the abstract Harris Theorem and are hence geometrically ergodic. For the geometric ergodicity of the two-point process, the positivity of the top Lyapunov exponent is also necessary, and it is achieved in Section
7
.

The two-point process with non-zero Brownian noise is treated in Section
9
, where we set the main stochastic stability estimates to prove the uniform-in-
\kappa
decay of correlations. Among other things, the scheme needs the stochastic two-point process to admit a Lyapunov-Foster drift function, which is established in Sections
10
and
11
. We finish with Section
12
, where we verify that the stochastic process
f_{\omega}^{\kappa}
satisfies the required conditions to be uniformly geometrically ergodic.

2.
Random Dynamical Systems and a Harris Theorem

In this section we introduce the random dynamical system framework that we use to prove Theorem
1.1
. First, we observe that
u^{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
u^{\boldsymbol{b}}
share, at least, two fixed points (see Proposition
5.1
below), which constitute an invariant set for the flow map
f_{\omega}
. Let
F
denote the set of such fixed points of the random-amplitude Pierrehumbert velocity field
u(t,\cdot,\underline{\omega};{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})
, and consider the phase space
\mathsf{X}:={{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}\setminus F
.
We define on
\mathsf{X}
the three main Markov transition kernels that describe the dynamics for the inviscid (
\kappa=0
) flow map
f_{\omega}
arising from
u(t,\cdot,\underline{\omega};{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})
on
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
be non-parallel axes and let
f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}}):=f^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{\omega_{2}}\circ f^{{\boldsymbol{b}}}_{\omega_{1}}({\mathbf{x}})
be the associated time-1 flow map, for
\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\sim\text{Unif}([-N,N]^{2})
, for some
N>1
fixed.

(1)

The one-point chain
P
in
\mathsf{X}
associated to the one-point process
f_{\omega}
is given by

(2.1)

P({\mathbf{x}},U):={\mathbb{P}}_{0}(f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}})\in U)

for any Borel set
U
in
\mathsf{X}
.

(2)

The projective chain associated to the projective process
\hat{f}_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}},v)
on the unit tangent bundle

(2.2)

\mathsf{Y}:=\{({\mathbf{x}},v):{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathsf{X},v\in T_{\mathbf{x}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}},|v|=1\}

is defined via
\hat{f}_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}},v):=(f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}}),\frac{D_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}})v}{|D_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}})v|})
as

(2.3)

\hat{P}(({\mathbf{x}},v),\hat{U})={\mathbb{P}}_{0}(\hat{f}_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}},v)\in\hat{U})

for any Borel set
\hat{U}
in
\mathsf{Y}
.

(3)

The two-point chain
P^{(2)}
associated to the two-point process
f^{(2)}_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=(f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}({\mathbf{y}}))
in the phase space
\mathsf{X}^{(2)}:=\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}
is given by

(2.4)

P^{(2)}(({\mathbf{x}}_{1},{\mathbf{x}}_{2}),U^{(2)})={\mathbb{P}}_{0}(f_{\omega}^{(2)}({\mathbf{x}}_{1},{\mathbf{x}}_{2})\in U^{(2)})

for any Borel set
U^{(2)}
in
\mathsf{X}^{(2)}
. Here,
{\mathcal{S}}
denotes the set of symmetries on the product space
\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}
for which
f^{(2)}_{\omega}
is invariant, see Proposition
8.1
, and includes the diagonal
{\Delta}=\{({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\in\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}:{\mathbf{x}}={\mathbf{y}}\}
.

The analysis leading to Theorem
1.1
relies on showing ergodic properties for the Markov chains introduced above. This section is devoted to presenting a primer of the abstract general setting and results on Markov chains and random dynamical systems developed in
[
5
,
10
]
that are applicable to the one-point, projective and two-point chains.

2.1.
Markov transition kernels and Harris Theorem

Let
\mathrm{X}
be a complete metric space (not necessarily compact), let
\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{X})
denote the set of Borel measurable sets on
\mathrm{X}
and let
\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{X})
be the set of Borel probability measures on
\mathrm{X}
. We say that
\mathrm{P}
is a Markov transition kernel on
\mathrm{X}
if for each
\mathrm{x}\in\mathrm{X}
we have that
{\mathrm{P}}(\mathrm{x},\cdot)
is a Borel probability on
\mathrm{X}
. Iterates of the Markov transition kernel
{\mathrm{P}}
are defined through Chapman-Kolmogorov by

(2.5)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{P}}^{n+1}(\mathrm{x},A)=\int_{\mathrm{X}}{\mathrm{P}}^{n}(\mathrm{z},A){\mathrm{P}}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{d}\mathrm{z}),

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and all Borel measurable sets
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{X})
. The transition kernel
{\mathrm{P}}
acts on bounded Borel measurable functions
\varphi:\mathrm{X}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
via

(2.6)

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}\varphi(\mathrm{x})=\int_{\mathrm{X}}\varphi(\mathrm{z}){\mathrm{P}}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{d}\mathrm{z}),

and if
\mathrm{P}g
is a continuous and bounded function on
\mathrm{X}
for all continuous and bounded functions
g
, we say that
{\mathrm{P}}
has the
Feller
property. Finally,
{\mathrm{P}}
also acts on probability measures
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{X})
on
\mathrm{X}
through

(2.7)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{P}}\nu(A)=\int_{\mathrm{X}}{\mathrm{P}}(\mathrm{x},A)\mathrm{d}\nu(\mathrm{x}),

for any Borel measurable
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{X})
.
Hence, we say that a Borel probability measure
\pi\in\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{X})
is
stationary
for
{\mathrm{P}}
if it is a fixed point of
{\mathrm{P}}
, that is,

(2.8)

\displaystyle\pi(A)=\int_{\mathrm{X}}{\mathrm{P}}(\mathrm{x},A)\mathrm{d}\pi(\mathrm{x}),

for all
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{X})
. A stationary measure
\pi
is called
ergodic
if all
({\mathrm{P}},\pi)
invariant sets have
\pi
-measure either zero or one.
In this setting, it is a standard fact that if
\pi
is the unique stationary measure for a transition kernel
{\mathrm{P}}
then it is ergodic, see
[
30
]
.

Given any function
\mathrm{V}:\mathrm{X}\rightarrow[1,\infty)
, we say that
\varphi\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{V}}(\mathrm{X})
if
\varphi
is measurable and

(2.9)

\displaystyle\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{V}}(\mathrm{X})}:=\sup_{\mathrm{x}\in\mathrm{X}}\frac{|\varphi(\mathrm{x})|}{\mathrm{V}(\mathrm{x})}<\infty.

In this paper, we are interested not only in the existence of an ergodic stationary measure for a Markov transition kernel
{\mathrm{P}}
but also in deriving a geometric rate of convergence in
L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{V}}(\mathrm{X})
towards it. More precisely, we say that
{\mathrm{P}}
is
\mathrm{V}
-
uniformly geometrically ergodic
if
{\mathrm{P}}
admits a unique stationary measure
\pi
and there exists
C>0
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
such that

(2.10)

\displaystyle\left|{\mathrm{P}}^{n}\varphi(\mathrm{x})-\int_{\mathrm{X}}\varphi(\mathrm{x})\mathrm{d}\pi(\mathrm{x})\right|\leq C\mathrm{V}(\mathrm{x})\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{V}}(\mathrm{X})}\gamma^{n},

for all
\mathrm{x}\in\mathrm{X}
, all
\varphi\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{V}}
and all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. The main tool to show that
{\mathrm{P}}
is
\mathrm{V}
-uniformly geometrically ergodic is Harris Theorem.

Theorem 2.2

(Harris Theorem)
.

Let
{\mathrm{P}}
be a Markov transition kernel with the Feller property. Assume that

(1)

{\mathrm{P}}
admits an open small set: there exists a set
A\subset\mathrm{X}
, a positive measure
\nu
on
\mathrm{X}
and a natural number
n=n(A)\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for all
{\mathbf{x}}\in A
, we have

(2.11)

{\mathrm{P}}^{n}({\mathbf{x}},B)\geq\nu(B),

for all measurable subsets
B\subset\mathrm{X}
. Such set
A
is said to be small.

(2)

{\mathrm{P}}
is topologically irreducible: for every point
{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathrm{X}
and every open non-empty set
A\subset\mathrm{X}
, there exists a natural number
n=n({\mathbf{x}},A)\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(2.12)

{\mathrm{P}}^{n}({\mathbf{x}},A)>0.

(3)

{\mathrm{P}}
is strongly aperiodic: there exists some
{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathrm{X}
such that for all open neighbourhoods
A
of
{\mathbf{x}}
, we have

(2.13)

{\mathrm{P}}({\mathbf{x}},A)>0.

(4)

There exists a function
\mathrm{V}:\mathrm{X}\rightarrow[1,\infty)
that meets the Lyapunov-Foster drift condition: there exist some constants
\gamma\in(0,1)
,
\beta>0
, and a compact set
K\subset\mathrm{X}
such that

(2.14)

\mathrm{P}\mathrm{V}\leq\gamma\mathrm{V}+\beta\boldsymbol{1}_{K}.

Then,
{\mathrm{P}}
is
\mathrm{V}
-uniformly geometrically ergodic.

The proof of this version of Harris Theorem is found in
[
5
]
. See also
[
27
,
36
]
for more general versions of the theorem.

2.2.
Random dynamical systems and sufficient conditions for Harris Theorem

Most of the effort in this paper is dedicated to showing that the one-point, two-point and projective processes are Markov transition kernels that satisfy all the hypotheses of Harris theorem. We do so following
[
5
]
, where the authors state a set of conditions for a random dynamical system with absolutely continuous noise that ensure its associated Markov transition kernel admit an open small set, is topologically irreducible, and is strongly aperiodic.

Let
(\Omega_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathbb{P}_{0})
be a fixed probability space. A continuous Random Dynamical System (RDS) with independent increments on
\mathrm{X}
is an assignment to each
\omega\in\Omega_{0}
of a continuous mapping
\mathrm{f}_{\omega}:\mathrm{X}\rightarrow\mathrm{X}
such that the set
\{\omega\in\Omega_{0}:\mathrm{f}_{\omega}(\mathrm{x})\in A\}
is
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-measurable, for all
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{X})
and all
\mathrm{x}\in\mathrm{X}
. Setting
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})=(\Omega_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathbb{P}_{0})^{\mathbb{N}}
and writing
\underline{\omega}=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},...)
for
\underline{\omega}\in\Omega
, we next consider the random composition of functions

(2.15)

\displaystyle\mathrm{f}_{\underline{\omega}}^{n}=\mathrm{f}_{\omega_{n}}\circ\dots\circ\mathrm{f}_{\omega_{1}}

for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and with the convention that
\mathrm{f}_{\underline{\omega}}^{0}
is the identity map in
\mathrm{X}
. Let
\theta:\Omega\rightarrow\Omega
denote the leftward shift on
\Omega
, that is,
\theta\underline{\omega}=(\omega_{2},\omega_{3},...)
for
\underline{\omega}=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},...)
. Note that
\theta
is a measure-preserving transformation on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
and that
\mathrm{f}_{\underline{\omega}}^{n}
satisfies the cocycle property

(2.16)

\displaystyle\mathrm{f}_{\underline{\omega}}^{n+m}=\mathrm{f}_{\theta^{m}\underline{\omega}}^{n}\circ\mathrm{f}^{m}_{\underline{\omega}},

for all
m,n\geq 0
. In this framework, continuous RDS define Markov chains with transition kernels

(2.17)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{P}}(\mathrm{x},A):=\mathbb{P}_{0}(\mathrm{f}_{\omega}(\mathrm{x})\in A),

and continuity of
\mathrm{f}_{\omega}
directly yields the Feller property for the transition kernel
{\mathrm{P}}
. Further specializing to the Markov chains derived from the one-point, two-point and projective processes, we assume the following:

(A1)

\Omega_{0}
is a smooth complete Riemannian manifold, the law
\mathbb{P}_{0}
on
\Omega_{0}
admits a density
\varrho_{0}
with respect to the Lebesgue measure
\mathrm{d}\omega
on
\Omega_{0}
and the mapping
\Omega_{0}\times\mathrm{X}\rightarrow\mathrm{X}
given by
(\omega,\mathrm{x})\mapsto\mathrm{f}_{\omega}(\mathrm{x})
is
C^{2}
.

(A2)

There exists a constant
C_{0}>0
such that
\mathbb{P}_{0}
-a.s.,

(2.18)

\displaystyle\|(D_{\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{f}_{\omega})^{-1}\|+\|D_{\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{f}_{\omega}\|+\|\mathrm{f}_{\omega}\|_{C^{2}}\leq C_{0}.

(A3)

The RDS
\mathrm{f}_{\omega}
preserves,
\mathbb{P}_{0}
-a.s., the Lebesgue measure on
\mathrm{X}
.

Given finitely many coordinates
\omega_{i}
of the random sequence
\underline{\omega}=\{\omega_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
, we adopt the notation
\underline{\omega}^{n}=(\omega_{1},...,\omega_{n})\in\Omega_{0}^{n}
, the Cartesian product of
n
copies of
\Omega_{0}
. Similarly, we abuse notation to denote
\varrho_{0}
the density of the product law
\mathbb{P}_{0}^{n}
on
\Omega_{0}^{n}
given by

(2.19)

\displaystyle\varrho_{0}(\underline{\omega}^{n})=\varrho_{0}(\omega_{1})\dots\varrho_{0}(\omega_{n}).

We begin by giving a simple condition for a Markov kernel
{\mathrm{P}}
to be strongly aperiodic in the sense of Theorem
2.2
. Its proof is given in
[
5
]
*Lemma 3.2.

Lemma 2.3
.

Assume there exists
\omega_{*}\in\text{Supp}(\mathbb{P}_{0})
and
\mathrm{x}_{*}\in\mathrm{X}
such that
\mathrm{f}_{\omega_{*}}(\mathrm{x}_{*})=\mathrm{x}_{*}
. Then,
{\mathrm{P}}
is strongly aperiodic.

Next, to derive a sufficient condition for the existence of open small sets, fix
\mathrm{x}\in\mathrm{X}
and define
\Psi_{\mathrm{x}}:\Omega_{0}^{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{X}
by

(2.20)

\displaystyle\Psi_{\mathrm{x}}(\underline{\omega}^{n}):=\mathrm{f}_{\omega_{n}}\circ\dots\circ\mathrm{f}_{\omega_{1}}(\mathrm{x}),\quad\underline{\omega}^{n}=(\omega_{1},...,\omega_{n}).

The following Lemma is drawn from
[
5
]
*Proposition 3.1.

Lemma 2.4
.

Suppose the RDS
\mathrm{f}_{\omega}
satisfies (A1) and there exists
n\geq 1
and
(\underline{\omega}_{*}^{n},\mathrm{x}_{*})\in\text{Supp}(\mathbb{P}_{0}^{n})\times\text{Supp}(\pi)
such that

(1)

There exist some
c>0
and
\varepsilon>0
such that
\varrho_{0}(\underline{\omega}^{n})\geq c>0
if
|\underline{\omega}^{n}-\underline{\omega}_{*}^{n}|<\varepsilon
.

(2)

The map
\Psi_{\mathrm{x}_{*}}
is a submersion at
\underline{\omega}^{n}=\underline{\omega}_{n}^{*}
.

Then,
{\mathrm{P}}
admits an open,
{\mathrm{P}}^{n}
-small set with corresponding measure
\nu_{n}\ll\mathrm{Leb}_{\mathrm{X}}
.

Lemma 10 in
[
12
]
gives a sufficient condition for
{\mathrm{P}}
to be topologically irreducible. It reads:

Lemma 2.5
.

Suppose the RDS
\mathrm{f}_{\omega}
satisfies (A1). Assume that for all
\mathrm{x}\in\mathrm{X}
and all open sets
\mathrm{U}\subset\mathrm{X}
there exists
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
{\underline{\omega}}_{*}^{n}\in\text{Supp}(\mathbb{P}_{0}^{n})\subset\Omega_{0}^{n}
such that

(1)

There exist some
c>0
and
\varepsilon>0
such that
\varrho_{0}(\underline{\omega}^{n})\geq c>0
if
|\underline{\omega}^{n}-\underline{\omega}_{*}^{n}|<\varepsilon
.

(2)

\Psi_{\mathrm{x}}({\underline{\omega}}_{*}^{n})\in\mathrm{U}
.

Then,
{\mathrm{P}}
is topologically irreducible.

If
\mathrm{X}
is a compact phase space, irreducibility, small sets and aperiodicity are sufficient conditions for exponential ergodicity of the Markov chain. However, the one-point, projective and two-point processes given in Definition
2.1
are defined in non-compact phase spaces (because of the fixed point of the dynamics in the one-point and projective processes and because of the diagonal in the two-point process). Hence, we must find a Lyapunov-drift function for each of these processes. While a Lyapunov-drift function for the one-point process can be constructed after understanding the behaviour of the flow maps
f_{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
f_{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{b}}
near the fixed points of
f_{\underline{\omega}}
, a Lyapunov-drift function for the two-point process must address, among others, the dynamics near the diagonal
\Delta
. This is achieved by introducing the
top Lyapunov exponent
, which is defined by

(2.21)

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}:=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log|D_{\mathrm{x}}\mathrm{f}_{\underline{\omega}^{n}}|

and measures the maximum rate of separation of two arbitrarily close points by the RDS
\mathrm{f}_{\omega}
. If the Markov transition kernel
{\mathrm{P}}
has a stationary ergodic measure and the map
\mathrm{f}_{\omega}
is almost surely bounded in
C^{1}
, then the Multiplicative Ergodic Theorem, see
[
30
]
*Part II, ensures that
\lambda_{1}
is well-defined and is almost surely a deterministic constant. In
[
5
]
*Proposition 4.5 it is shown that the positivity of the top Lyapunov exponent is enough to construct a Lyapunov-drift function for the two-point process:

Proposition 2.6
.

Assume that the one-point and projective chains are geometrically ergodic. If the top Lyapunov exponent is positive, then there exists a function
\mathrm{V}^{(2)}\in L^{1}(\mathrm{X}^{2}\setminus\Delta)
of the form

(2.22)

\displaystyle\mathrm{V}^{(2)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})=d_{X}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})^{-\xi}\psi_{\xi}(\mathrm{x},\hat{\mathrm{w}}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})),\quad\hat{\mathrm{w}}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})=\frac{\mathrm{exp}_{\mathrm{x}}^{-1}(\mathrm{y})}{|\mathrm{exp}_{\mathrm{x}}^{-1}(\mathrm{y})|}

for some
\xi\in(0,1)
and some continuous and strictly positive
\psi_{\xi}
such that
\mathrm{V}^{(2)}\geq 1
, and there exists
\gamma<1
and
s_{*}>0
with

(2.23)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{P}}^{(2)}\mathrm{V}^{(2)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})<\gamma\mathrm{V}^{(2)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y}),

for all
(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\in\Delta(s_{*})
, where
\Delta(s)=\{(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\in\mathrm{X}^{2}:0<d_{X}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})<s\}
.

It then remains to check whether the top Lyapunov exponent is positive. For this, let
d
be the dimension of
\mathrm{X}
and consider the fiber bundle
SL(\mathrm{X})
over
\mathrm{X}
with fiber

(2.24)

\displaystyle SL_{\mathrm{p}}(\mathrm{X})=\{A:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow T_{\mathrm{p}}\mathrm{X}\text{ linear map such that det}(A)=1\}

understood as a principal bundle over
\mathrm{X}
with structure group
SL_{d}({\mathbb{R}})
. For a fixed
\mathrm{x}\in\mathrm{X}
and a determinant 1 isomorphism
E:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow T_{\mathrm{x}}\mathrm{X}
, the fiber
SL_{\mathrm{p}}(\mathrm{X})
over
\mathrm{p}\in\mathrm{X}
can now be viewed as the space of determinant 1 mappings
A:T_{\mathrm{x}}\mathrm{X}\rightarrow T_{\mathrm{p}}\mathrm{X}
and we define
\widehat{\Psi}_{\mathrm{x}}:\Omega_{0}^{n}\rightarrow SL(\mathrm{X})
given by

(2.25)

\displaystyle\widehat{\Psi}_{\mathrm{x}}(\underline{\omega}^{n}):=\left(\Psi_{\mathrm{x}}(\underline{\omega}^{n}),D_{\mathrm{x}}\Psi_{\mathrm{x}}(\underline{\omega}^{n})\right).

The following result corresponds to
[
5
]
*Proposition 3.3 and gives conditions on
\Psi_{\mathrm{x}}
and
\widehat{\Psi}_{\mathrm{x}}
for which the top Lyapunov exponent associated to
\Psi_{\mathrm{x}}
is positive.

Proposition 2.7
.

Assume that the RDS
\mathrm{f}_{\omega}
on
\mathrm{X}
is such that

(1)

the law
{\mathbb{P}}_{0}
admits a density
\rho_{0}
with respect to the Lebesgue measure
{\rm d}\omega
on
\Omega_{0}
(smooth complete Riemannian manifold) and the mapping
(\omega,\mathrm{x})\mapsto\mathrm{f}_{\omega}(\mathrm{x})
is
C^{2}
;

(2)

for a.e.
\omega\in\Omega_{0}
,
\mathrm{f}_{\omega}
is a local diffeomorphism and admits an ergodic stationary measure
\pi
on
\mathrm{X}
with the property

(2.26)

\int(\log^{+}|D_{\mathrm{x}}\mathrm{f}_{\omega}|+\log^{+}|(D_{\mathrm{x}}\mathrm{f}_{\omega})^{-1}|){\rm d}\pi(\mathrm{x}){\rm d}{\mathbb{P}}_{0}(\omega)<\infty.

Moreover, assume that the associated Markov kernel
{\mathrm{P}}
is
\mathrm{V}
-uniformly geometrically ergodic with stationary measure
\pi
.
If there exist
n\geq 1
and a point
(\omega_{*}^{(n)},{\mathbf{x}}_{*})\in{\text{supp}}(\rho_{0})\times{\text{supp}}(\pi)
which satisfies

(i)

There exists
c,{\varepsilon}>0
such that
\varrho(\underline{\omega}^{n})\geq c>0
if
|\underline{\omega}^{n}-\underline{\omega}_{*}^{n}|<{\varepsilon}
;

(ii)

The mapping
\Psi_{\mathrm{x}_{*}}
is a submersion at
\underline{\omega}^{n}=\underline{\omega}_{*}^{n}
.

(iii)

Let
\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{x},\underline{\omega}_{*}^{n}}:=\text{ker}D_{\underline{\omega}^{n}}\Psi_{\mathrm{x}}\subset T_{\underline{\omega}_{*}^{n}}\Omega_{0}^{n}
. The restriction of
D_{\underline{\omega}_{*}^{n}}\widehat{\Psi}_{\mathrm{x}_{*}}
to
\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{x},\underline{\omega}_{*}^{n}}
is surjective as a linear operator onto
T_{\widehat{\Psi}_{\mathrm{x}_{*}}(\underline{\omega}_{*}^{n})}SL(\mathrm{X})
.

Then
\lambda_{1}>0
.

It was observed in
[
12
]
that while
[
5
]
*Proposition 3.3 is stated for a compact Riemannian manifold
\mathrm{X}
, the result holds under the weaker assumption of
\mathrm{X}
being
\sigma
-compact, which is our case here, as
\mathrm{X}
is compact up to a finite number of points.

2.3.
Almost-sure exponential mixing

Assume next that

(S)

The continuous RDS
\mathrm{f}_{\omega}
admits a stationary measure
\pi
which is almost-surely invariant, that is,
(\mathrm{f}_{\omega})_{*}\pi=\pi
for
\mathbb{P}_{0}
-almost every
\omega\in\Omega_{0}
.

By a duality argument, almost-sure mixing follows from decay of the correlations

(2.27)

\displaystyle\text{Cor}_{n}(g,h):=\left|\int_{\mathrm{X}}g(\mathrm{x})h\circ\mathrm{f}_{\underline{\omega}}^{n}(\mathrm{x})\mathrm{d}\pi(\mathrm{x})\right|

with probability 1 as
n\rightarrow\infty
, for sufficiently smooth observables
g,h
with
\pi
-mean zero. As
\text{Cor}_{n}(g,h)
is a random variable on
\underline{\omega}
, Chebyshev’s inequality shows that

(2.28)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{0}\left(\{\text{Cor}_{n}(g,h)>\varepsilon\}\right)\leq\varepsilon^{-2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}\left|\int_{\mathrm{X}}g(\mathrm{x})h\circ\mathrm{f}_{\underline{\omega}}^{n}(\mathrm{x})\mathrm{d}\pi(\mathrm{x})\right|^{2}.

In turn, we can write

(2.29)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}\left|\int_{\mathrm{X}}g(\mathrm{x})h\circ\mathrm{f}_{\underline{\omega}}^{n}(\mathrm{x})\mathrm{d}\pi(\mathrm{x})\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}\int_{\mathrm{X}\times\mathrm{X}}g(\mathrm{x})g(\mathrm{y})h\circ\mathrm{f}_{\underline{\omega}}^{n}(\mathrm{x})h\circ\mathrm{f}_{\underline{\omega}}^{n}(\mathrm{y})\mathrm{d}\pi(\mathrm{x})\mathrm{d}\pi(\mathrm{y})

(2.30)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{X}^{2}}g^{(2)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})({\mathrm{P}}^{(2)})^{n}h^{(2)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\mathrm{d}\pi^{(2)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y}),

where
g^{(2)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})=g(\mathrm{x})g(\mathrm{y})
,
h^{(2)}
is defined analogously,
\mathrm{d}\pi^{(2)}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})=\mathrm{d}\pi(\mathrm{x})\mathrm{d}\pi(\mathrm{y})
and
{\mathrm{P}}^{(2)}
denotes the Markov semigroup for the two-point process
(\mathrm{x}_{n},\mathrm{y}_{n})=(\mathrm{f}_{\underline{\omega}}^{n}(\mathrm{x}),\mathrm{f}_{\underline{\omega}}^{n}(\mathrm{y}))
. Hence, geometric ergodicity of the two-point process implies almost-surely exponential mixing for
\mathrm{f}_{\omega}
. This principle and the argument presented above are well known in the field of random dynamical systems, see
[
4
,
19
]
. However,
{\mathrm{P}}^{(2)}
cannot be uniformly geometrically ergodic with respect to
\pi^{(2)}
because the diagonal set

(2.31)

\displaystyle\Delta:=\{(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{x}):\mathrm{x}\in\mathrm{X}\}\subset\mathrm{X}\times\mathrm{X}

is almost-surely invariant for the two-point process and thus it supports at least one additional stationary measure for
{\mathrm{P}}^{(2)}
distinct from
\pi^{(2)}
. For
\mathrm{X}^{(2)}=\mathrm{X}\times\mathrm{X}\setminus\Delta
, the above principle can be made precise if there exists a Lyapunov-drift function for
{\mathrm{P}}^{(2)}
on
\mathrm{X}^{(2)}
, since it is now a non-compact phase space, that ensures positive recurrence away from
\Delta
. The next result can be found in
[
5
]
*Proposition 4.6.

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
\mathrm{f}_{\omega}
be a continuous RDS on a compact, orientable Riemannian manifold
\mathrm{X}
without boundary satisfying condition
(S)
. Assume that the two-point process with kernel
{\mathrm{P}}^{(2)}
on
\mathrm{X}^{(2)}
is
\mathrm{V}^{(2)}
-geometrically ergodic, for some
\mathrm{V}^{(2)}:\mathrm{X}^{(2)}\rightarrow[1,\infty)
that is integrable with respect to
\pi^{(2)}
. Then, for all
q,s>0
there exist a function
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}_{q,s}:\Omega_{0}\rightarrow[1,\infty)
and a constant
\lambda=\lambda_{q,s}>0
such that

(2.32)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathrm{X}}g(\mathrm{x})h\circ\mathrm{f}_{\underline{\omega}}^{n}(\mathrm{x})\mathrm{d}\pi(\mathrm{x})\right|\leq\mathrm{C}(\underline{\omega})e^{-\lambda n}\|g\|_{H^{s}}\|h\|_{H^{s}},

for all
\pi
-mean free functions
g,h\in H^{s}=H^{s}(\mathrm{X})
with
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[\mathrm{C}^{q}]<\infty
.

An inspection of the proof shows that the result remains true if
\mathrm{X}
admits a finite cover by smooth charts.

2.4.
Summary of sufficient conditions for exponential mixing

Theorem
1.1
, and also Corollary
1.2
after a standard duality argument, is ensured for
\kappa=0
by Proposition
2.8
if the one-point process
f_{\omega}
given by (
2.1
) satisfies (S) and its associated two-point process
f_{\omega}^{(2)}
is
\mathrm{V}^{(2)}
-uniformly geometrically ergodic. Throughout the manuscript, we define

(2.33)

\displaystyle\pi:=\mathrm{Leb}_{\mathsf{X}},\quad\hat{\pi}:=\mathrm{Leb}_{\mathsf{Y}},\quad\pi^{(2)}:=\pi\otimes\pi.

Since
u(t,\cdot,{\omega};{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})
is divergence-free, the measure
\pi
is invariant under
f_{\omega}
and thus stationary. Hence,
\hat{\pi}
is stationary for
\hat{f}_{\omega}
on
\mathsf{Y}
and
\pi^{(2)}
is stationary for
f_{\omega}^{(2)}
on
\mathsf{X}^{(2)}
as well so that (S) is satisfied for all three processes.

The geometric ergodicity of
f_{\omega}^{(2)}
is, in turn, a consequence of Theorem
2.2
if
f_{\omega}^{(2)}
admits an open small set, is topologically irreducible, aperiodic and admits a Lyapunov-drift function
\mathrm{V}^{(2)}
. The existence of such
\mathrm{V}^{(2)}
is a consequence of Proposition
2.6
, which requires the one-point process
f_{\omega}
and the projective process
\hat{f}_{\omega}
to be themselves geometrically ergodic, and for the top Lyapunov exponent of
f_{\omega}
to be positive.

The geometric ergodicity of
f_{\omega}
and
\hat{f}_{\omega}
is also obtained by means of the Harris Theorem
2.2
after showing these two processes are also topologically irreducible, aperiodic and admit open small sets and Lyapunov drift functions
\mathrm{V}
and
\hat{\mathrm{V}}
. For these three processes, the existence of open small sets is obtained via Lemma
2.4
; their aperiodicity is automatic from Lemma
2.3
as they are the identity for
\omega=0
and they are continuous maps, while the topological irreducibility is a consequence of Lemma
2.5
. Lastly, the positivity of the top Lyapunov exponent is deduced by the combination of Propositions
2.7
and
7.1
.

In Section
5
,
6
and Section
8
we show that the Markov transition kernels
{P}
,
\hat{P}
and
{P}^{(2)}
are topologically irreducible, admit an open small set (via Lemma
2.4
) and admit Lyapunov-drift function
{\mathrm{V}}
,
\hat{\mathrm{V}}
and
{\mathrm{V}}^{(2)}
, respectively. In Section
7
we investigate the positivity of the top Lyapunov exponent by means of Proposition
7.1
and Proposition
2.7
.

2.5.
Uniform-in-
\kappa
exponential mixing and enhanced dissipation

In the presence of molecular diffusivity the composition flow map
f_{\omega}^{\kappa}
is no longer deterministic but instead satisfies the stochastic differential equation (
1.8
) with convective vector field
v_{\kappa}(t,{\mathbf{x}})=u(t,{\mathbf{x}},\underline{\omega};{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})-2\kappa{\mathbf{x}}
and the deterministic RDS framework of
[
5
]
does not apply directly any more. However, the recent contribution
[
10
]
shows that the exponential decay of correlations of Proposition
2.8
remains true for
f_{\omega}^{\kappa}
with uniform-in-
\kappa
constants if the inviscid dynamics are robust enough. To obtain a uniform-in-
\kappa
version of Proposition
2.8
, we follow the framework derived in
[
10
]
and we introduce the stochastic two-point Markov transition kernel
P^{(2)}_{\kappa}
defined by

(2.34)

\displaystyle P^{(2)}_{\kappa}(({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}),U^{(2)})={\mathbb{P}}(f_{\omega}^{(2),\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\in U^{(2)})

where
f_{\omega}^{(2),\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=(f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}}))
denotes the stochastic two-point process for
({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\in\mathsf{X}^{(2)}
. We consider the following assumption
P^{(2)}_{\kappa}
.

(
H_{\kappa}
)

Assume there exists
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\gamma\in(0,1)
,
\beta>0
,
R>\frac{2\beta}{1-\gamma}
,
\upsilon>0
and a probability measure
\nu_{0}
such that for all
\kappa>0
sufficiently small there holds

(2.35)

\displaystyle P^{(2),\ell}_{\kappa}\mathrm{V}^{(2)}_{\kappa}\leq\gamma\mathrm{V}^{(2)}_{\kappa}+\beta,

and

(2.36)

\displaystyle\inf_{(p,q)\in\{\mathrm{V}^{(2)}_{\kappa}\leq R\}}P^{(2),\ell}_{\kappa}((p,q),\cdot)\geq\upsilon\nu_{0}(\cdot),

for some
\mathrm{V}^{(2)}_{\kappa}:\mathsf{X}^{(2)}\rightarrow[1,\infty)
.

If
(H_{\kappa})
is satisfied, a quantitative version of Harris Theorem, see
[
10
,
27
,
36
]
, shows that
P^{(2)}_{\kappa}
is
\mathrm{V}_{\kappa}^{(2)}
-uniformly geometrically ergodic with geometric constant
\lambda>0
independent of
\kappa\in(0,\kappa_{0})
for some
\kappa_{0}>0
small. As a result, almost sure uniform-in-
\kappa
exponential decay of correlations follows.

Proposition 2.9

(Lemma 3.3 in
[
10
]
)
.

Assume that
P^{(2)}_{\kappa}
is
\mathrm{V}_{\kappa}^{(2)}
-geometrically ergodic. Then, for all
s>0
and all
0<q<\infty
there exists a random variable
\mathrm{C}_{\kappa}\geq 1
and a deterministic
\lambda>0
, both independent of
{\mathbf{B}}
, such that for every pair of mean-zero test functions
g,h\in\dot{H}^{s}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
and every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there holds

(2.37)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}g({\mathbf{x}})h(f_{\underline{\omega}_{n}}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}))\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{x}}\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{\kappa}e^{-\lambda n}\|g\|_{H^{s}}\|h\|_{H^{s}},

almost surely. Moreover, there exists
\overline{\mathrm{C}}_{q}>0
independent of
\kappa
such that
{\mathbb{E}}|\mathrm{C}_{\kappa}|^{q}\leq\overline{\mathrm{C}}_{q}
, for all
\kappa\in(0,\kappa_{0})
, for some
\kappa_{0}>0
small enough.

Once Proposition
2.9
is established, the continuous-in-time statement of Theorem
1.1
follows as in
[
16
]
. A standard duality argument completes Corollary
1.2
, while Corollary
1.3
is a consequence of (
2.37
) and an inspection of the proof of
[
37
]
*Proposition 2.2, see Section
12
.

To obtain Proposition
2.9
we shall see that the two-point chain
P_{\kappa}^{(2)}
satisfies
(H_{\kappa})
.
This is achieved in Proposition
12.6
in Section
12
which requires, among other things, the construction of some
\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}_{\kappa}
that satisfies a Lyapunov-Foster drift-condition for the stochastic two-point process
f_{\omega}^{(2),\kappa}
with constants independent of
\kappa\in(0,\kappa_{0})
, for some
\kappa_{0}>0
small enough.
In Section
11
, we define such
\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}_{\kappa}
in terms of suitable modifications
\mathrm{V}_{\kappa}
and
\mathrm{W}_{\kappa}
of the Lyapunov-drift functions
\mathrm{V}
and
\mathrm{W}
for the inviscid one-point and two-point processes respectively and we show the Lyapunov-Foster drift estimate in Proposition
11.2
.

3.
Differential geometry on the sphere

To understand the projective process and the positivity of the top Lyapunov exponent a precise analysis of the differentials
D_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
D_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{b}}
of the two flow maps and their composition is required.
Compared to the
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
setting considered in
[
5
,
12
]
and the
{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
setting of
[
16
]
, one of the main difficulties when considering flows on the sphere is that
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
is not parallelizable, that is, the tangent bundle
T{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
cannot be identified with the product space
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
. Consequently, the differential of the flow map cannot be interpreted as a linear endomorphism, since in general it does not map the tangent space to a point to itself. This obstruction can be circumvented by defining a local reference frame and a map that encodes how the reference frame on
T_{\mathbf{x}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
changes whenever
{\mathbf{x}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
moves with
f_{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
f_{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{b}}
.

In this section we present the main geometric tools used throughout the paper.
We equip
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
with the standard metric induced by
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
in view of the canonical embedding
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. For all
{\mathbf{x}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, the tangent space
T_{\mathbf{x}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
is given by

(3.1)

\displaystyle T_{\mathbf{x}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}=\{v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}:{\mathbf{x}}\cdot v=0\},

and the orthogonal projection of a vector
v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
to
T_{\mathbf{x}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
is defined by

(3.2)

\Pi_{\mathbf{x}}v:=v-(v\cdot{\mathbf{x}}){\mathbf{x}},

Then, for a smooth vector field
w
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
such that
w|_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}\in T{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, the Levi-Civita covariant derivative with respect to
v\in T_{\mathbf{x}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
on
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
is given by

(3.3)

\nabla_{v}w({\mathbf{x}})=\Pi_{\mathbf{x}}(D_{\mathbf{x}}w({\mathbf{x}})v),\quad{\mathbf{x}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}},

where
D_{\mathbf{x}}w
denotes the ambient space
3\times 3
Jacobian matrix of
{w}
. The commutator of two vector fields
v_{1},v_{2}\in T{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, using smooth extensions to
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
denoted by the same symbols, is the vector field

(3.4)

\displaystyle[v_{1},v_{2}]({\mathbf{x}})=\nabla_{v_{1}}v_{2}({\mathbf{x}})-\nabla_{v_{2}}v_{1}({\mathbf{x}})=[v_{1},v_{2}]_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}({\mathbf{x}}),

where
[\cdot,\cdot]_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}
denotes the commutator of vector fields on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. The equality holds because the commutator of vector fields tangent to the sphere is itself tangent to the sphere.

3.1.
Frames on
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}

Let
{\mathcal{U}}\subset{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
be an open set of
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
. A smooth local frame
E
on
{\mathcal{U}}
is an ordered pair of smooth vector fields
(e_{1},e_{2})
defined on
{\mathcal{U}}
that are linearly independent and span the tangent bundle, namely the pair
(e_{1}({\mathbf{x}}),e_{2}({\mathbf{x}}))
forms a basis of
T_{\mathbf{x}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
for all
{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathcal{U}}
. We say that a smooth frame
E
is orthonormal if
(e_{1},e_{2})
are smooth orthonormal vector fields with respect to the ambient
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
scalar product. If we identify the space of frames on
{\mathcal{U}}
with the space of real
3\times 2
matrices, which we denote by
M^{3\times 2}({\mathbb{R}})
, a left inverse for
E({\mathbf{x}})
is given
2
2

2

In general, if
E({\mathbf{x}})
is a
3\times 2
matrix of rank 2, there exists a (not necessarily unique) left-inverse
E({\mathbf{x}})^{-1}
. If the column vectors of
E({\mathbf{x}})
are orthonormal, one can choose
E({\mathbf{x}})^{-1}=E({\mathbf{x}})^{T}
.
by
E({\mathbf{x}})^{T}\in M^{2\times 3}({\mathbb{R}})
, i.e.,
E({\mathbf{x}})^{T}E({\mathbf{x}})=I\in M^{2\times 2}({\mathbb{R}})
. In particular, for any
{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathcal{U}}
and any vector
v\in T_{\mathbf{x}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, the vector
w=E({\mathbf{x}})^{T}v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
denotes the local coordinates of
v\in T_{\mathbf{x}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
in the frame
E({\mathbf{x}})
. With this identification, we have

(3.5)

\displaystyle T{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}|_{\mathcal{U}}\cong{\mathcal{U}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}.

If
E
is a smooth orthonormal frame on
{\mathcal{U}}
, for all
{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathcal{U}}
the projection of a vector
v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
to
T_{\mathbf{x}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
can now be written as

(3.6)

\Pi_{\mathbf{x}}v=\sum_{i=1}^{2}(e_{i}({\mathbf{x}})\cdot v)e_{i}({\mathbf{x}})=E({\mathbf{x}})E({\mathbf{x}})^{T}v

and we can find the local coordinates of the covariant derivative
\nabla_{v}{w}({\mathbf{x}})
in the orthonormal frame
E
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
E({\mathbf{x}})
be a local smooth orthonormal frame on
{\mathcal{U}}\subset{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
. Let
{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathcal{U}}
and
v_{1}\in T_{\mathbf{x}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
. Then,

(3.7)

\displaystyle E({\mathbf{x}})^{T}\nabla_{v_{1}}v_{2}({\mathbf{x}})=E({\mathbf{x}})^{T}D_{\mathbf{x}}v_{2}({\mathbf{x}})v_{1}({\mathbf{x}})

for all vector fields
v_{2}
defined on open subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
that contain
{\mathbf{x}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
and such that
v_{2}|_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
is tangent to
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
.

Proof.

The proof follows directly from (
3.3
) and (
3.6
), namely

(3.8)

E({\mathbf{x}})^{T}\nabla_{v}{w}({\mathbf{x}})=E({\mathbf{x}})^{T}\Pi_{\mathbf{x}}(D_{\mathbf{x}}{w}({\mathbf{x}})v({\mathbf{x}}))=(E({\mathbf{x}})^{T}E({\mathbf{x}}))E({\mathbf{x}})^{T}D_{\mathbf{x}}{w}({\mathbf{x}})v({\mathbf{x}})=E({\mathbf{x}})^{T}D_{\mathbf{x}}{w}({\mathbf{x}})v({\mathbf{x}}).

∎

We further note that if
E
is a smooth orthonormal frame on
{\mathcal{U}}
, then for all
{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathcal{U}}
and all
v\in UT_{\mathbf{x}}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
, namely
|v|^{2}:=v\cdot v=1
, there exists a unique
w\in\mathbb{S}^{1}=\{(w_{1},w_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}:w_{1}^{2}+w_{2}^{2}=1\}
such that
v=E({\mathbf{x}})w
. Such
w\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
is indeed given by
w=E({\mathbf{x}})^{T}v
and we thus see that

(3.9)

\displaystyle UT{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}|_{\mathcal{U}}\cong{\mathcal{U}}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}.

We say that a smooth frame
E=(e_{1},e_{2})
on
{\mathcal{U}}
has unit area if
\mu_{\mathbf{x}}(e_{1}({\mathbf{x}}),e_{2}({\mathbf{x}}))=1
, for all
{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathcal{U}}
. Here,

(3.10)

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbf{x}}(e_{1}({\mathbf{x}}),e_{2}({\mathbf{x}})):={\mathbf{x}}\cdot(e_{1}({\mathbf{x}})\times e_{2}({\mathbf{x}}))

denotes the standard area form on
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
inherited from
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. Fixing now a smooth frame
E_{0}
on
{\mathcal{U}}
with unit area we observe that any other unit area and smooth frame
E
is given by
E({\mathbf{x}})=E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})\mathrm{g}({\mathbf{x}})
, for some
g({\mathbf{x}})\in SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
. With this identification, if
\mathcal{F}^{SL}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
denotes the principal
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
-bundle of unit area frames, we have

(3.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{SL}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})|_{\mathcal{U}}\cong{\mathcal{U}}\times SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})

and
g({\mathbf{x}})\in SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
is given by
E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})^{T}E({\mathbf{x}})
, if
E_{0}
is an orthonormal frame.

For all
{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathcal{U}}
and
u\in T_{\mathbf{x}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, the Levi-Civita covariant derivative of
E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})
in the direction
u
is given by

(3.12)

(\nabla_{u}E_{0})({\mathbf{x}}):=(\nabla_{u}e_{1}({\mathbf{x}}),\nabla_{u}e_{2}({\mathbf{x}})),

and represents the rate of change of the reference frame
E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})
in the direction
u\in T_{\mathbf{x}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
. To compute the covariant derivative, we radially extend
e_{1}({\mathbf{x}})
and
e_{2}({\mathbf{x}})
from
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
and still denote them by
e_{1}({\mathbf{x}})
and
e_{2}({\mathbf{x}})
to simplify notation. Since
\{e_{1}({\mathbf{x}}),e_{2}({\mathbf{x}})\}
is an orthonormal basis of
T_{\mathbf{x}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
for all
{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathcal{U}}
, there exists a one-form
\gamma_{\mathbf{x}}
such that

(3.13)

\displaystyle\nabla_{u}e_{1}({\mathbf{x}})=\gamma_{\mathbf{x}}(u)e_{2}({\mathbf{x}}),\quad\nabla_{u}e_{2}({\mathbf{x}})=-\gamma_{\mathbf{x}}(u)e_{1}({\mathbf{x}}),

for all
u\in T_{\mathbf{x}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, so that for
u=u^{1}e_{1}({\mathbf{x}})+u^{2}e_{2}({\mathbf{x}})
, we have

(3.14)

\gamma_{\mathbf{x}}(u)=u^{1}\gamma_{\mathbf{x}}(e_{1}({\mathbf{x}}))+u^{2}\gamma_{\mathbf{x}}(e_{2}({\mathbf{x}})).

3.2.
A convenient reference frame

We now fix a local smooth orthonormal and unit-area reference frame which will be employed in Section
6
.
Let
\mathbf{e}_{3}=(0,0,1)\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
and set

(3.15)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{3}:=\{{\mathbf{x}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}:\Pi_{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{e}_{3}\neq 0\}={{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}\setminus\{\pm\mathbf{e}_{3}\}.

For all
{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{3}
, we define the orthonormal tangent vector fields

(3.16)

\displaystyle e_{1}({\mathbf{x}}):=\frac{\Pi_{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{e}_{3}}{\|\Pi_{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{e}_{3}\|}\in T_{\mathbf{x}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}},\quad e_{2}({\mathbf{x}}):={\mathbf{x}}\times e_{1}({\mathbf{x}})\in T_{\mathbf{x}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}.

Then,
E_{0}({\mathbf{x}}):=(e_{1}({\mathbf{x}}),e_{2}({\mathbf{x}}))\in(T_{\mathbf{x}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})^{2}
is a smooth orthonormal local frame over
{\mathcal{U}}_{3}
. Moreover,
E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})
has also unit area, since for all
{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{3}
there holds

(3.17)

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbf{x}}(e_{1}({\mathbf{x}}),e_{2}({\mathbf{x}}))={\mathbf{x}}\cdot(e_{1}({\mathbf{x}})\times({\mathbf{x}}\times e_{1}({\mathbf{x}})))={\mathbf{x}}\cdot((e_{1}({\mathbf{x}})\cdot e_{1}({\mathbf{x}})){\mathbf{x}}-(e_{1}({\mathbf{x}})\cdot{\mathbf{x}})e_{1}({\mathbf{x}}))={\mathbf{x}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}=1.

In coordinates,
E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})
is given by

(3.18)

\displaystyle e_{1}({\mathbf{x}})=\frac{1}{r}(-zx,-zy,r^{2}),\quad e_{2}({\mathbf{x}})=\frac{1}{r}(y,-x,0),

for
r=\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}
and
{\mathbf{x}}=(x,y,z)\in{\mathcal{U}}_{3}
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})=(e_{1}({\mathbf{x}}),e_{2}({\mathbf{x}}))
be given by (
3.18
). For
{\mathbf{x}}_{0}=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},0\right)
we have

(3.19)

\displaystyle e_{1}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=(0,0,1),\quad e_{2}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},0\right)

and there holds
(\nabla_{u}E_{0})({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=0
for all
u\in T_{{\mathbf{x}}_{0}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
.

Proof.

A simple computation shows that for all
{\mathbf{x}}=(x,y,z)\in{\mathcal{U}}_{3}
,

(3.20)

\gamma_{\mathbf{x}}(e_{1}({\mathbf{x}}))=0,\quad\gamma_{\mathbf{x}}(e_{2}({\mathbf{x}}))=\frac{z}{\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}}.

Hence, at
{\mathbf{x}}_{0}=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},0\right)
we have

(3.21)

\displaystyle\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}_{0}}(e_{1}({\mathbf{x}}_{0}))=0,\quad\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}_{0}}(e_{2}({\mathbf{x}}_{0}))=0

and thus
(\nabla_{u}E_{0})({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=0
for all
u\in T_{{\mathbf{x}}_{0}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
due to (
3.13
) and (
3.14
).
∎

4.
The Lie algebra rank condition

In this section we present the new approach based on the Lie algebra rank condition (LARC), introducing the necessary definitions and showing how it connects to the deterministic Harris Theorem framework developed in
[
5
]
.
More precisely, we show that global LARC plays a central role when proving topological irreducibility for the one-point process while local LARC enters the picture when discussing the existence of open small sets for the processes defined in Section
2.1
, and more importantly, when proving the positivity of the top Lyapunov exponent. The main results of this section are Theorem
4.4
and Corollary
4.8
which connect the LARC with topological irreducibility and a local submersion property.

4.1.
The Lie algebra rank condition on manifolds

We introduce the discussion on a smooth
d
-dimensional manifold
\mathrm{M}
, since processes arising from the Rossby-Haurwitz model take place on open subsets of
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, unit tangent bundles and in product spaces.
Nevertheless, because all statements are local, a reduction to coordinate charts reduces the discussion to the standard Euclidean definition of vector fields on open subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.

To begin with, let
\mathrm{M}
be a smooth connected
d
-dimensional manifold together with its Lie algebra of smooth vector fields
\mathfrak{X}(\mathrm{M})
.
Consider a finite family of smooth vector fields
\mathcal{V}=\{V_{1},\dots,V_{m}\}\subset\mathfrak{X}(\mathrm{M})
.

Definition 4.1
.

The Lie algebra
{\text{Lie}}({\mathcal{V}})
generated by
{\mathcal{V}}
is the smallest Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{X}(\mathrm{M})
that contains
V_{1},\dots,V_{m}
.
Equivalently,
{\text{Lie}}({\mathcal{V}})
is the linear span of all iterated Lie brackets, which are defined via induction as

(4.1)

V_{(i)}:=V_{i},\qquad V_{(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})}:=[V_{i_{1}},V_{(i_{2},\dots,i_{k})}],

for all multi-indices
(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})\in\{1,\dots,m\}^{k}
and all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
For a point
x\in\mathrm{M}
, the pointwise Lie algebra is the pointwise evaluation

(4.2)

{\text{Lie}}_{x}(\mathcal{V}):=\{V(x)\,:\,V\in{\text{Lie}}(\mathcal{V})\}\subset T_{x}\mathrm{M}.

We now present the main tool introduced in this section.

Definition 4.2
.

We say that the family
\mathcal{V}
satisfies the Lie algebra rank condition (LARC) at
x\in\mathrm{M}
if

(4.3)

{\text{Lie}}_{x}(\mathcal{V})=T_{x}\mathrm{M}.

In addition, if the Lie algebra rank condition is satisfied for all
x\in\mathrm{M}
, we say that the family
\mathcal{V}
is bracket-generating on
\mathrm{M}
.

For each
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, the vector field
V_{i}
generates a local smooth flow
\Phi_{t}^{i}
that satisfies

(4.4)

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\Phi_{t}^{i}(x)=V_{i}(\Phi_{t}^{i}(x)),\qquad\Phi_{0}^{i}(x)=x.

For a choice of parameters
\omega=(\omega_{1},\dots\omega_{m})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
we define the composed flow

(4.5)

\Phi_{\omega}:=\Phi_{\omega_{m}}^{m}\circ\cdots\circ\Phi_{\omega_{1}}^{1},

where the order in (
4.5
) is fixed as follows: the flow of
V_{1}
acts first, then the flow of
V_{2}
, and so on.
We denote by a word of length
q
the tuple

(4.6)

\varpi=(\omega^{1},\dots,\omega^{q})\in({\mathbb{R}}^{m})^{q},\qquad\omega^{j}=(\omega^{j}_{1},\dots,\omega^{j}_{m}),

and, respectively, the flow
\Phi_{\varpi}
associated with
\varpi
and the endpoint map
\Psi_{x}^{q}
for the point
x
as

(4.7)

\Phi_{\varpi}(x):=\Phi_{\omega^{q}}\circ\cdots\circ\Phi_{\omega^{1}}(x),\qquad\Psi_{x}^{q}(\varpi):=\Phi_{\varpi}(x).

Remark 4.3
.

We shall use the following elementary facts throughout the section.

(i)

If
(\mathcal{U},\chi)
is a chart on
\mathrm{M}
and
\widetilde{V}=D\chi\circ V\circ\chi^{-1}
denotes
V
in local coordinates, then coordinate push-forward preserves Lie brackets. Hence the condition
{\text{Lie}}_{x}({\mathcal{V}})=T_{x}\mathrm{M}
is equivalent to the corresponding Euclidean rank condition in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.

(ii)

If
\mathrm{M}
is embedded in some
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, the rank condition is imposed in the intrinsic tangent space
T_{x}\mathrm{M}
, not in the ambient space. For example, on
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
the brackets must span
T_{x}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, equivalently
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
after choosing a local chart, not
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
.

(iii)

All flow constructions are local in time and the parameters are chosen sufficiently small so that the relevant compositions are well-defined.

4.2.
Topological irreducibility

The first consequence of the bracket-generating property is topological irreducibility. To translate the LARC condition into a checkable condition for topological irreducibility we use the Rashevsky-Chow theorem in the form stated by Agrachev and Sachkov
[
1
]
*Chapter 5, Theorem 5.9.

Theorem 4.4

(Rashevsky–Chow)
.

Let
\mathrm{M}
be a smooth connected manifold and let
{\mathcal{V}}=\{V_{1},\dots,V_{m}\}\subset\mathfrak{X}(\mathrm{M})
be bracket-generating on
\mathrm{M}
.
Then, for every
x\in\mathrm{M}
, the orbit generated by
{\mathcal{V}}
is the whole manifold:

(4.8)

\mathcal{O}_{x}({\mathcal{V}}):=\left\{\Phi_{t_{n}}^{i_{n}}\circ\cdots\circ\Phi_{t_{1}}^{i_{1}}(x):n\in\mathbb{N},\;i_{j}\in\{1,\dots,m\},\;t_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}\right\}=\mathrm{M}.

Theorem
4.4
gives an equivalent condition to verify the irreducibility criterion from Lemma
2.5
.
Indeed, any composition appearing in (
4.8
) can be written as a composition of elementary blocks
\Phi_{\omega}
by choosing vectors
\omega
with only one non-zero coordinate.
Consequently, Theorem
4.4
implies that, whenever
{\mathcal{V}}
is bracket-generating on
\mathrm{M}
, for every
x\in\mathrm{M}
and every non-empty open set
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathrm{M}
there exists a length
q\in\mathbb{N}
and a parameter
\varpi\in({\mathbb{R}}^{m})^{q}
such that
\Psi_{x}^{q}(\varpi)\in\mathcal{O}
.

4.3.
Commutator loops and small sets

In contrast to topological irreducibility, the existence of open small sets is a local non-degeneracy property.
By Lemma
2.4
, it is enough to exhibit a point
x_{*}
and a word
\varpi_{*}
such that the density of the law is bounded from below in a neighbourhood of
\varpi_{*}
and the endpoint map
\Psi^{q}_{x_{*}}
is a submersion at
\varpi_{*}
.
In the present setting, the density condition follows from the fact that the random amplitudes have a uniform law on a box, therefore, the substantive point is to construct a word with the required submersion property, and this is precisely what the LARC provides.

We now specialize to
\mathrm{M}={\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. It is immediate to note that, whenever the vector fields
V_{1}(x),\dots,V_{m}(x)
already span
T_{x}\mathrm{M}
, the map
\Psi_{x}^{q}(\varpi)
is a submersion at the trivial word
\varpi=0
. Indeed, if a single small parameter turns on the flow of
V_{i}
, then the first-order variation of the endpoint-map along
\varpi_{i}
at
\varpi=0
is in the direction
V_{i}
.
However, if they do not generate
T_{x}\mathrm{M}
, the remaining directions are generally spanned by Lie brackets, which appear as the first non-trivial leading order contribution of loops of flows.
