We develop this idea to understand suitably chosen compositions of flow maps and how their leading order in time expansion connects with the Lie algebra generated by
{\mathcal{V}}
at
x
.

To that purpose, we introduce commutator loops of flow maps associated to the iterated Lie brackets defined in (
4.1
) by induction.
For simplicity, write
I:=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
and
I^{\prime}:=(i_{2},\dots,i_{k})
with all
i_{j}\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, and define the associated loops inductively as

(4.9)

\Gamma_{t}^{(i)}:=\Phi_{t}^{i},\qquad\Gamma_{t}^{I}:=\Phi_{-t}^{i_{1}}\circ(\Gamma_{t}^{I^{\prime}})^{-1}\circ\Phi_{t}^{i_{1}}\circ\Gamma_{t}^{I^{\prime}}.

The following lemma connects flow map loops (
4.9
) with their associated iterated Lie brackets (
4.1
) showing that the non-trivial leading order of the Taylor expansion in time is exactly the iterated bracket.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
I
be a multi-index of length
k
and let
K\Subset\mathrm{M}
be contained in a coordinate chart.
Then there exist
t_{0}>0
and a smooth map
{\mathcal{E}}_{I}:(-t_{0},t_{0})\times K\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
in local coordinates such that, for
|t|<t_{0}
and
x\in K
,

(4.10)

\Gamma_{t}^{I}(x)=x+t^{k}V_{I}(x)+t^{k+1}{\mathcal{E}}_{I}(t,x),

in the chosen coordinate chart. Moreover, the expansion holds in
C^{1}(K)
.

The lemma follows from an induction argument on
|I|
and is presented in Appendix
B
together with some preliminary standard results on flow maps in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.

We focus now on showing how
n
linearly independent vector fields of the Lie Algebra generated by
{\mathcal{V}}=\{V_{1},...,V_{k}\}
for some
k\geq 2
give rise to flow maps obtained as suitable combinations of
\Phi_{t}^{j}
whose time-differentials have rank
n
.
For
i=1,...,k
, we define
h_{i}:{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
by
h_{i}(a)=a\mathbf{e}_{i}
, where
\mathbf{e}_{i}
is the usual Cartesian basis of
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
, so that we have
\Phi_{h_{i}(a)}=\Phi_{a}^{i}
.

The next proposition shows how to construct words
\varpi
that recover information on the Lie algebra generated by
{\mathcal{V}}
.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}=\{V_{1},\dots,V_{m}\}
be a family of smooth vector fields on a smooth
d
-dimensional manifold
\mathrm{M}
and let
x_{0}\in\mathrm{M}
.
Assume there exist multi-indices
I_{1},\dots,I_{r}
such that
V_{I_{1}}(x_{0}),\dots,V_{I_{r}}(x_{0})
are linearly independent in
T_{x_{0}}\mathrm{M}
. Then there exist
q\in\mathbb{N}
,
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, and a word
\varpi_{*}\in([-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0}]^{m})^{q}\setminus\{0\}
such that

(4.11)

\mathrm{rank}D_{\varpi}\Phi_{\varpi_{*}}(x_{0})\geq r.

Proof.

Let
d=\dim\mathrm{M}
and let
\{V_{I_{j}}:j=1,\dots,r\}
be the set of vector fields generated by repeated nested commutators that are linearly independent at
x=x_{0}
. We work locally around
x_{0}\in\mathrm{M}
and we give a recursive procedure that determines the words
\varpi_{I_{j}}
associated to each
V_{I_{j}}
. We already know that
\varpi_{i}=h_{i}(a)
is the word associated to
V_{i}
by means of
\Phi_{\varpi_{i}}=\Phi_{a}^{i}
. Next, let
V_{i_{1}},V_{i_{2}}\in{\mathcal{V}}
and note that the flow map

(4.12)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{a}^{[V_{i_{1}},V_{i_{2}}]}=\Phi_{a}^{i_{1}}\circ\Phi_{a}^{i_{2}}\circ(\Phi_{a}^{i_{1}})^{-1}\circ(\Phi_{a}^{i_{2}})^{-1}

gives rise to the commutator
[V_{i_{1}},V_{i_{2}}]
for any two distinct
i_{1},i_{2}\in\{1,\dots,m\}
. Since
\Phi_{a}^{i}
is the flow map generated by the autonomous vector field
V_{i}
, we have that
(\Phi_{a}^{i})^{-1}=\Phi_{-a}^{i}
for all
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
. In particular, we observe that

(4.13)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{a}^{[V_{i_{1}},V_{i_{2}}]}=\Phi_{\varpi_{i_{1},i_{2}}(a)},\quad\varpi_{i_{1},i_{2}}(a):=(-h_{i_{2}}(a),-h_{i_{1}}(a),h_{i_{2}}(a),h_{i_{1}}(a))\in({\mathbb{R}}^{m})^{4}

and

(4.14)

\displaystyle\left(\Gamma_{a}^{[V_{i_{1}},V_{i_{2}}]}\right)^{-1}=\Phi_{a}^{i_{2}}\circ\Phi_{a}^{i_{1}}\circ\Phi_{-a}^{i_{2}}\circ\Phi_{-a}^{i_{1}}=\Phi_{\varpi^{-1}_{i_{1},i_{2}}(a)},\hskip 8.00003pt\varpi^{-1}_{i_{1},i_{2}}(a):=(h_{i_{1}}(a),h_{i_{2}}(a),-h_{i_{1}}(a),-h_{i_{2}}(a))\in({\mathbb{R}}^{m})^{4}.

By induction, we are able to find words
\varpi_{I_{j}}
that give rise to the commutators
V_{I_{j}}
as follows: Assume that
V_{I_{j}}
is a vector field generated by
\ell_{j}>1
commutators, that is,
I_{j}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell_{j}})
with
i_{s}\in\{1,\dots,m\}
for all
s=1,\dots,\ell_{j}
and
V_{I_{j}}=[V_{i_{1}},[V_{i_{2}},\dots,[V_{i_{\ell_{j}-1}},V_{i_{\ell_{j}}}]\dots]]=[V_{i_{1}},V_{\tilde{I}_{j}}]
for
{\tilde{I}_{j}}=(i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell_{j}})
. Let
\varpi_{\tilde{I}_{j}}(a)
and
\varpi_{\tilde{I}_{j}}^{-1}(a)
be the words for
\Gamma_{a}^{V_{\tilde{I}_{j}}}
and
\left(\Gamma_{a}^{V_{\tilde{I}_{j}}}\right)^{-1}
. Then,

(4.15)

\displaystyle\varpi_{I_{j}}(a):=(\varpi^{-1}_{\tilde{I}_{j}}(a),-h_{i_{1}}(a),\varpi_{\tilde{I}_{j}}(a),h_{i_{1}}(a)),\quad\varpi_{I_{j}}^{-1}(a):=(-h_{i_{1}}(a),\varpi_{\tilde{I}_{j}}^{-1}(a),h_{i_{1}}(a),\varpi_{\tilde{I}_{j}}(a))

are such that

(4.16)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{a}^{V_{{I}_{j}}}=\Phi_{\varpi_{{I}_{j}}(a)},\quad\left(\Gamma_{a}^{V_{{I}_{j}}}\right)^{-1}=\Phi_{\varpi^{-1}_{{I}_{j}}(a)},

respectively, for all
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Once we have determined the words
\varpi_{I_{j}}
, for
a_{1},\dots,a_{r}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we now define

(4.17)

\displaystyle h(a_{1},\dots,a_{r}):=(\varpi_{I_{1}}(a_{1}),\dots,\varpi_{I_{r}}(a_{r}))\in({\mathbb{R}}^{m})^{q},

for some
q\in{\mathbb{N}}
and consider the composition map
F:{\mathbb{R}}^{r}\times\mathrm{M}\rightarrow\mathrm{M}
given by
F(a_{1},\dots,a_{r};x):=\Phi_{h(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})}(x)
. Then,

(4.18)

\displaystyle D_{a_{1},\dots,a_{r}}F(a_{1}^{*},\dots,a_{r}^{*};x)=D_{\varpi}\Phi_{\varpi_{*}}(x)D_{a_{1},\dots,a_{r}}h(a_{1}^{*},\dots,a_{r}^{*}),

where
\varpi_{*}=h(a_{1}^{*},\dots,a_{r}^{*})=(\varpi_{I_{1}}(a_{1}^{*}),\dots,\varpi_{I_{r}}(a_{r}^{*}))\in({\mathbb{R}}^{m})^{q}
. It is immediate to see that
D_{a_{1},\dots,a_{r}}h(a_{1}^{*},\dots,a_{r}^{*})
has full rank. Since the
\{V_{I_{j}}(x_{0})\}_{j=1}^{r}
are linearly independent, we now show that
D_{a_{1},\dots,a_{r}}F(a_{1}^{*},\dots,a_{r}^{*};x_{0})
has rank
r
, for some
a_{*}:=(a_{1}^{*},\dots,a_{r}^{*})
with
\|a_{*}\|
sufficiently small. To do this, we first note that

(4.19)

\displaystyle F(a_{1},\dots,a_{r};x)=\Phi_{\varpi_{I_{r}}(a_{r})}\circ\dots\circ\Phi_{\varpi_{I_{1}}(a_{1})}(x),

with further

(4.20)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\varpi_{I_{j}}(a)}(x)=x+a^{\ell_{j}}V_{I_{j}}(x)+a^{\ell_{j}+1}\mathcal{E}_{I_{j}}(a,x)

for all
j=1,\dots,r
, and all
a\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
and
x\in U
due to Lemma
4.5
, where we recall that
\ell_{j}\geq 1
is the length of the multi-index
I_{j}
, with the convention that
\ell_{j}=1
whenever
V_{I_{j}}=V_{i_{j}}
, for some
i_{j}\in\{1,\dots,m\}
. Then,

(4.21)

\displaystyle F(a_{1},\dots,a_{r};x)

\displaystyle=\left(Id+a_{r}^{\ell_{r}}V_{I_{r}}(\cdot)+a_{r}^{\ell_{r}+1}\mathcal{E}_{I_{r}}(a_{r},\cdot)\right)\circ\dots\circ\left(Id+a_{1}^{\ell_{1}}V_{I_{1}}(\cdot)+a_{1}^{\ell_{1}+1}\mathcal{E}_{I_{1}}(a_{1},\cdot)\right)(x)

(4.22)

\displaystyle=x+\sum_{j=1}^{r}\left(a_{j}^{\ell_{j}}V_{I_{j}}(x)+a_{j}^{\ell_{j}+1}\mathcal{E}_{I_{j}}(a_{j},x)\right)+\sum_{1\leq j_{1}<j_{2}\leq r}a_{j_{1}}^{\ell_{j_{1}}}a_{j_{2}}^{\ell_{j_{2}}}\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r};x)

for some smooth error functions
\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r};x)
such that
\|\partial_{j_{i}}\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r};x)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 1
. In particular,

(4.23)

\displaystyle\partial_{a_{j}}F(a_{1},\dots,a_{r};x)

\displaystyle=\ell_{j}a_{j}^{\ell_{j}-1}V_{I_{j}}(x)+a_{j}^{\ell_{j}}\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{j}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r};x)

(4.24)

\displaystyle\quad+\ell_{j}a_{j}^{\ell_{j}-1}\left(\sum_{j<\ell\leq r}a_{\ell}^{\ell_{\ell}}\mathcal{E}_{j,\ell}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r};x)+\sum_{1\leq\ell<j}a_{\ell}^{\ell_{\ell}}\mathcal{E}_{\ell,j}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r};x)\right)

(4.25)

\displaystyle=\ell_{j}a_{j}^{\ell_{j}-1}\left(V_{I_{j}}(x)+\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq r}a_{\ell}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{j,\ell}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r};x)\right)

with
\|{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}_{j,\ell}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r};x)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 1
for
\|a\|\leq 1
. Since
\{V_{I_{j}}(x_{0})\}_{j=1}^{r}
are linearly independent, we have that

(4.26)

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{r}c_{j}V_{I_{j}}(x_{0})\right\|\gtrsim\sum_{j=1}^{r}|c_{j}|>0,

for all
(c_{1},\dots,c_{r})\neq 0
.
Let
\widetilde{c_{j}}=\prod_{\ell\neq j}\ell_{\ell}a_{\ell}^{\ell_{\ell}-1}
, as
\{\partial_{a_{j}}F(a_{1},\dots,a_{r};x)\}_{j=1}^{r}
are linearly independent if and only if
\{\widetilde{c_{j}}\partial_{a_{j}}F(a_{1},\dots,a_{r};x)\}_{j=1}^{r}
are, we check the linear independence of

(4.27)

V_{I_{j}}(x_{0})+\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq r}a_{\ell}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{j,\ell}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r};x_{0}),\qquad j=1,\dots,r.

These are indeed linearly independent in view of (
4.26
) if
\sum_{j=1}^{r}|a_{j}|
is small enough.
Consequently, we see that
\{\partial_{a_{j}}F(a_{1},\dots,a_{r};x_{0})\}_{j=1}^{r}
are linearly independent and thus
D_{a_{1},\dots,a_{r}}F(a_{1}^{*},\dots,a_{r}^{*};x_{0})
has rank
r
.
In view of (
4.18
), we conclude that
D_{\varpi}\Phi_{\varpi_{*}}(x_{0})
has rank at least
r
.
∎

Remark 4.7
.

The use of both positive and negative times in commutator loops is compatible with the random dynamical systems studied in this manuscript because the deterministic flow map is generated by smooth vector fields whose amplitudes are supported in a neighbourhood of the origin.
Since the parameter
\varpi_{*}
in Proposition
4.6
can be chosen arbitrarily close to the origin, all the positive and negative times used in the construction belong to the admissible support of the random amplitudes after choosing
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small.

To conclude, we state as a general fact the following corollary connecting the LARC condition with the submersion property used in Lemma
2.4
.
However, in the subsequent sections, we tailor the analysis to the process under consideration and use directly Proposition
4.6
.

Corollary 4.8
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
4.6
, if the LARC condition holds for
{\mathcal{V}}
at
x_{0}\in\mathrm{M}
then there exists
q\in\mathbb{N}
and a word
\varpi_{*}\in({\mathbb{R}}^{m})^{q}
such that
\Psi_{x_{0}}^{q}
is a submersion at
\varpi_{*}
.

Proof.

If the LARC holds at
x_{0}
, then there exist bracket words
I_{1},\dots,I_{d}
such that
V_{I_{1}}(x_{0}),\dots,V_{I_{d}}(x_{0})
form a basis of
T_{x_{0}}\mathrm{M}
.
Applying Proposition
4.6
with
r=d=\dim\mathrm{M}
gives the existence of some
\varpi_{*}\in({\mathbb{R}}^{m})^{q}
such that
\operatorname{rank}D_{\varpi}\Psi_{x_{0}}^{q}(\varpi_{*})=d
, which is precisely the submersion property.
∎

5.
The one-point process

Let
{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
and
f_{\omega}=f_{\omega_{2}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}\circ f_{\omega_{1}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}
for
\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in[-N,N]^{2}
denote the one-point process associated to the vector field
u(t,\cdot,\omega;{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})
. In this section we use Theorem
2.2
to show that
f_{\omega}
is
\mathrm{V}
-uniformly geometrically ergodic. To do so, we first describe the motion induced by the vector field
u(t,\cdot,\omega;{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})
and we record several formulas that will prove useful for the remainder of the manuscript. We show in Proposition
5.5
that the process is topologically irreducible and admits an open small set. Since the phase space
\mathsf{X}={{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}\setminus F
is not compact due to the presence of the fixed points
F
, in Proposition
5.10
we construct a function
\mathrm{V}:\mathsf{X}\rightarrow[1,\infty)
that satisfies a Lyapunov-Foster drift condition.

5.1.
Fixed points and relevant identities

We begin by characterizing the set of fixed points
F
for
f_{\omega}
.

Proposition 5.1
.

For non-parallel and non-orthogonal axes
{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, the set of fixed points for the random dynamics is
F:=\{\pm{\boldsymbol{c}}\}
, where
{\boldsymbol{c}}:=\frac{{\boldsymbol{a}}\times{\boldsymbol{b}}}{|{\boldsymbol{a}}\times{\boldsymbol{b}}|}
.
If
{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
{\boldsymbol{b}}
are mutually orthogonal, we further have
F:=\{\pm{\boldsymbol{c}},\pm{\boldsymbol{a}},\pm{\boldsymbol{b}}\}
.

Proof.

We look for the points where both vector fields vanish simultaneously, deducing that either
{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}=0
or
{\mathbf{x}}=\pm{\boldsymbol{a}}
and analogously for
{\boldsymbol{b}}
.
If
{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
{\boldsymbol{b}}
are not orthogonal to each other, the only points invariant are the intersection of the two great circles
{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}=0
and
{\boldsymbol{b}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}=0
.
Solving for
{\mathbf{x}}
gives the first part of the statement.
However, if
{\boldsymbol{a}}\perp{\boldsymbol{b}}
the four poles
\pm{\boldsymbol{a}},\pm{\boldsymbol{b}}
lie on the great circle of the other vector field, implying that they are fixed points for the dynamics.
∎

The next lemma describes the motion generated by the vector field
u^{\boldsymbol{a}}
.

Lemma 5.2
.

The time-1 flow map
f_{\omega}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})
associated to
u_{\omega}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}
is a rigid rotation of angle
\theta({\mathbf{x}}_{0}):=\omega{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}_{0}
around the axis
{\boldsymbol{a}}
.

Proof.

We solve the ODE

(5.1)

\dot{\mathbf{x}}(t)=u_{\omega}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\mathbf{x}}(t)),\quad{\mathbf{x}}(0)={\mathbf{x}}_{0}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}.

This equation has two invariants, the first one being the norm
|{\mathbf{x}}(t)|=|{\mathbf{x}}_{0}|
for all
t
, and the second one the projection onto the rotation axis
{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}(t)={\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}_{0}
.
From these and the matrix representation
{\boldsymbol{a}}\times{\mathbf{x}}=A{\mathbf{x}}
, we easily reduce the ODE to

(5.2)

\dot{\mathbf{x}}(t)=\omega({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})A{\mathbf{x}}(t),\quad{\mathbf{x}}(0)={\mathbf{x}}_{0},

for which the solution is
{\mathbf{x}}(t)={\rm e}^{t\omega({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}_{0})A}{\mathbf{x}}_{0}
, which is a rotation of angle
\theta({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=\omega({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}_{0})
around the axis
{\boldsymbol{a}}
.
∎

Up to a fixed initial rotation of the coordinates, it is always possible to reduce the study of the dynamics to the computationally simpler case where
{\boldsymbol{b}}=(0,0,1)
and
{\boldsymbol{a}}=(0,a_{2},a_{3})
, with
a_{2}\neq 0
. This simplification will be adopted from now on throughout the paper and the proofs of the results will be carried out in whichever setting is the most demanding among
{\boldsymbol{b}}=(0,0,1)
and
{\boldsymbol{a}}=(0,a_{2},a_{3})
or the orthogonal case
{\boldsymbol{b}}=(0,0,1)
and
{\boldsymbol{a}}=(0,1,0)
.

For
{\mathbf{x}}=(x,y,z)\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
and
\omega\in[-N,N]
, we have

(5.3)

\displaystyle f_{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{a}}({\mathbf{x}})=\begin{pmatrix}x\cos(\vartheta\omega)+(a_{2}z-a_{3})y\sin(\vartheta\omega)\\
a_{3}x\sin(\vartheta\omega)+y\cos(\vartheta\omega)+a_{2}\vartheta(1-\cos(\vartheta\omega))\\
-a_{2}x\sin(\vartheta\omega)+z\cos(\vartheta\omega)+a_{3}\vartheta(1-\cos(\vartheta\omega))\end{pmatrix},\quad\vartheta=a_{2}y+a_{3}z.

In the special case where
{\boldsymbol{a}}=(0,1,0)
we denote
f^{2}_{\omega}=f^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{\omega}
and
f^{3}_{\omega}=f^{{\boldsymbol{b}}}_{\omega}
and we have

(5.4)

f_{\omega}^{2}({\mathbf{x}})=\begin{pmatrix}x\cos\left(\omega y\right)+z\sin\left(\omega y\right)\\
y\\
-x\sin\left(\omega y\right)+z\cos\left(\omega y\right)\\
\end{pmatrix},\quad f_{\omega}^{3}({\mathbf{x}})=\begin{pmatrix}x\cos\left(\omega z\right)-y\sin\left(\omega z\right)\\
x\sin\left(\omega z\right)+y\cos\left(\omega z\right)\\
z\\
\end{pmatrix},

see Figure
1
.

5.2.
Open small set and topological irreducibility

The first step towards Proposition
5.5
is proving that the family
\{u^{\boldsymbol{a}}({\mathbf{x}}),u^{\boldsymbol{b}}({\mathbf{x}})\}
is bracket-generating for almost all
{\mathbf{x}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}}
be two non-parallel unit axes and denote
{\boldsymbol{c}}={\boldsymbol{a}}\times{\boldsymbol{b}}
. Let
{\mathbf{x}}_{a}^{\pm}=\pm\frac{{\boldsymbol{a}}-({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{b}}){\boldsymbol{b}}}{\sqrt{1-({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{b}})^{2}}}
,
{\mathbf{x}}_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{\pm}=\pm\frac{{\boldsymbol{b}}-({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{b}}){\boldsymbol{a}}}{\sqrt{1-({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{b}})^{2}}}
,
{\mathbf{x}}_{\boldsymbol{c}}^{\pm}=\pm\frac{{\boldsymbol{c}}}{|{\boldsymbol{c}}|}
and
{\mathbf{x}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}\setminus\{{\mathbf{x}}_{a}^{\pm},{\mathbf{x}}_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{\pm},{\mathbf{x}}_{\boldsymbol{c}}^{\pm}\}
. Then the family
\{u^{\boldsymbol{a}}({\mathbf{x}}),u^{\boldsymbol{b}}({\mathbf{x}})\}
is bracket-generating.

Proof.

The two vector fields
u^{\boldsymbol{a}}({\mathbf{x}})
and
u^{\boldsymbol{b}}({\mathbf{x}})
span
T_{\mathbf{x}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
if they are linearly independent. Their cross product reads

(5.5)

u_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\mathbf{x}})\times u_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}({\mathbf{x}})=({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})({\boldsymbol{b}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})[({\boldsymbol{a}}\times{\mathbf{x}})\times({\boldsymbol{b}}\times{\mathbf{x}})]=({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})({\boldsymbol{b}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})({\boldsymbol{c}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}){\mathbf{x}},

which is non-zero provided
{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}\neq 0
,
{\boldsymbol{b}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}\neq 0
and
{\boldsymbol{c}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}\neq 0
. We shall now assume at least one of them is zero, otherwise
\{u^{\boldsymbol{a}}({\mathbf{x}}),u^{\boldsymbol{b}}({\mathbf{x}})\}
is already bracket-generating. We next record

(5.6)

\displaystyle[u^{\boldsymbol{a}},u^{\boldsymbol{b}}]({\mathbf{x}})=({\boldsymbol{b}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})({\boldsymbol{c}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})({\boldsymbol{a}}\times{\mathbf{x}})+({\boldsymbol{c}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})({\boldsymbol{b}}\times{\mathbf{x}})+({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})({\boldsymbol{b}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})({\boldsymbol{c}}\times{\mathbf{x}})

and we argue by cases.

–

If
{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}=0
, then
[u^{\boldsymbol{a}},u^{\boldsymbol{b}}]({\mathbf{x}})=({\boldsymbol{b}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})({\boldsymbol{c}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})({\boldsymbol{a}}\times{\mathbf{x}})
and

(5.7)

\displaystyle u^{\boldsymbol{b}}({\mathbf{x}})\times[u^{\boldsymbol{a}},u^{\boldsymbol{b}}]({\mathbf{x}})=-({\boldsymbol{b}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})^{2}({\boldsymbol{c}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})^{2}{\mathbf{x}}.

If
{\boldsymbol{b}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}=0
, then
{\mathbf{x}}={\mathbf{x}}_{\boldsymbol{c}}^{\pm}
, a contradiction. Likewise, if
{\boldsymbol{c}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}=0
and
{\mathbf{x}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, since
{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{c}}={\boldsymbol{b}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{c}}=0
we have that
{\mathbf{x}}={\mathbf{x}}_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{\pm}
, a contradiction as well.

–

If
{\boldsymbol{b}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}=0
then
[u^{\boldsymbol{a}},u^{\boldsymbol{b}}]({\mathbf{x}})=(a\cdot{\mathbf{x}})({\boldsymbol{c}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})({\boldsymbol{b}}\times{\mathbf{x}})
and
u^{\boldsymbol{a}}({\mathbf{x}})\times[u^{\boldsymbol{a}},u^{\boldsymbol{b}}]({\mathbf{x}})=({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})^{2}({\boldsymbol{c}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})^{2}{\mathbf{x}}
. As before,
{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}=0
leads to
{\mathbf{x}}={\mathbf{x}}_{\boldsymbol{c}}^{\pm}
and
{\boldsymbol{c}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}=0
leads to
{\mathbf{x}}={\mathbf{x}}_{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\pm}
, a contradiction as well.

–

If
{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}\neq 0
,
{\boldsymbol{b}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}\neq 0
and
{\boldsymbol{c}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}=0
, we note that
[u^{\boldsymbol{a}},u^{\boldsymbol{b}}]({\mathbf{x}})=({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})({\boldsymbol{b}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})({\boldsymbol{c}}\times{\mathbf{x}})
and

(5.8)

\displaystyle u^{\boldsymbol{a}}({\mathbf{x}})\times[u^{\boldsymbol{a}},u^{\boldsymbol{b}}]({\mathbf{x}})=({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})^{2}({\boldsymbol{b}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})(({\boldsymbol{b}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})-({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{b}})({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})){\mathbf{x}},

which is never-vanishing for all
{\mathbf{x}}\neq\pm{\boldsymbol{a}}
, since
0\leq|{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{b}}|<1
. Moreover,

(5.9)

\displaystyle u^{\boldsymbol{b}}({\mathbf{x}})\times[u^{\boldsymbol{a}},u^{\boldsymbol{b}}]({\mathbf{x}})=({\boldsymbol{b}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})^{2}({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})(({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})-({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{b}})({\boldsymbol{b}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})){\mathbf{x}},

which is again never-vanishing for all
{\mathbf{x}}\neq\pm{\boldsymbol{b}}
. Hence, either
\{u^{\boldsymbol{a}}({\mathbf{x}}),[u^{\boldsymbol{a}},u^{\boldsymbol{b}}]({\mathbf{x}})\}
or
\{u^{\boldsymbol{b}}({\mathbf{x}}),[u^{\boldsymbol{a}},u^{\boldsymbol{b}}]({\mathbf{x}})\}
have rank 2.

Therefore,
\{u^{\boldsymbol{a}}({\mathbf{x}}),u^{\boldsymbol{b}}({\mathbf{x}})\}
is bracket-generating away from
{\mathbf{x}}_{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\pm}
,
{\mathbf{x}}_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{\pm}
and
{\mathbf{x}}_{\boldsymbol{c}}^{\pm}
.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

We note here that when
{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{b}}=0
, the axes of rotation are orthogonal, then
{\mathbf{x}}_{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\pm}=\pm{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
{\mathbf{x}}_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{\pm}=\pm{\boldsymbol{b}}
, we recover the extra fixed points of the dynamical system.

Proposition 5.5
.

The one-point process is strongly aperiodic, topologically irreducible and admits an open small set.

Proof.

The strong aperiodicity is a consequence of noting that
f_{\omega}(p)=p
for
\omega=(0,0)
and all
p\in\mathsf{X}
and the continuity of
f_{\omega}
with respect to
\omega\in[-N,N]^{2}
. The existence of open small sets is a consequence of Lemma
5.3
after choosing
\omega=(0,0)
for some
{\mathbf{x}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}\setminus\{{\mathbf{x}}_{a}^{\pm},{\mathbf{x}}_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{\pm},{\mathbf{x}}_{\boldsymbol{c}}^{\pm}\}
with
{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}\neq 0
,
{\boldsymbol{b}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}\neq 0
and
{\boldsymbol{c}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}\neq 0
. If
{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{b}}=0
, we have that
\mathrm{M}=\mathsf{X}
so that from Lemma
5.3
and Theorem
4.4
we conclude the topological irreducibility of the random dynamical system. If
{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{b}}\neq 0
, the dynamical system is topologically irreducible in
\mathrm{M}={{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}\setminus\{{\mathbf{x}}_{a}^{\pm},{\mathbf{x}}_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{\pm},{\mathbf{x}}_{\boldsymbol{c}}^{\pm}\}
. To show the topological irreducibility on
\mathsf{X}
, we note that
u^{\boldsymbol{a}}({\mathbf{x}}_{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\pm})\neq 0
and
u^{\boldsymbol{b}}({\mathbf{x}}_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{\pm})\neq 0
, and thus there exists
\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}},\omega_{\boldsymbol{b}}\in[-N,N]
such that
f_{(\omega_{\boldsymbol{b}},0)}({\mathbf{x}}_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{\pm})\in\mathrm{M}
and
f_{(0,\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}})}({\mathbf{x}}_{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\pm})\in\mathrm{M}
.
∎

5.3.
The Lyapunov-drift function

We now construct the Lyapunov-drift function for the one-point process associated to the rotation axis
{\boldsymbol{a}}=(0,1,0)
and
{\boldsymbol{b}}=(0,0,1)
. We comment on the case of general axes of rotation later. Let
p=(x,y,z)
,
p_{1}=f_{\omega_{1}}^{3}(p)
and
p_{2}=f_{\omega_{2}}^{2}(p_{1})
. Due to (
5.4
) we recall that

(5.10)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}\\
y_{1}\\
z_{1}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}x\cos(\omega_{1}z)-y\sin(\omega_{1}z)\\
x\sin(\omega_{1}z)+y\cos(\omega_{1}z)\\
z\end{pmatrix},\quad\begin{pmatrix}x_{2}\\
y_{2}\\
z_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}\cos(\omega_{2}y_{1})+z_{1}\sin(\omega_{2}y_{1})\\
y_{1}\\
-x_{1}\sin(\omega_{2}y_{1})+z_{1}\cos(\omega_{2}y_{1})\\
\end{pmatrix}.

We shall obtain a drift function as a combination of local drifts tailored for each family of fixed points

(5.11)

\displaystyle F_{\mathsf{x}}=\{\pm(1,0,0)\},\quad F_{\mathsf{y}}=\{\pm(0,1,0)\},\quad F_{\mathsf{z}}=\{\pm(0,0,1)\},

since the dynamics near each fixed point is qualitatively different.

5.3.1.
The
F_{\mathsf{x}}
fixed points

We begin with finding a drift function for the dynamics near the fixed points
(1,0,0)
and
(-1,0,0)
, which are hyperbolic stagnation points of
f_{\omega}
. For
p=(x,y,z)\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
we define
r_{\mathsf{x}}(p)=\max\{|y|,|z|\}
and for
R>0
we set

(5.12)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Q}_{\mathsf{x}}(R)=\{p=(x,y,z)\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}:0<r_{\mathsf{x}}(p)<R\}

and, for all
p\in\mathsf{Q}_{\mathsf{x}}(R)
, we define
V_{\mathsf{x}}(p):=r_{\mathsf{x}}(p)^{-\alpha}
, for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
to be determined. Before showing that
V_{\mathsf{x}}
constitutes a valid drift function in a neighbourhood of
F_{\mathsf{x}}
, we first prove a technical intermediate result.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and
N>0
. Let
p_{1}=(x_{1},y_{1},z_{1})=f_{\omega_{1}}^{3}(p)
, for
p=(x,y,z)\in\mathsf{Q}_{\mathsf{x}}(R)
, for some
R>0
small. Then,

(5.13)

{\mathbb{P}}(\{\omega_{1}\in[-N,N]:|y_{1}|\leq\varepsilon\})\leq\frac{2\varepsilon}{N|z|}.

Proof.

We start by noticing that the probability is equal to the length
l
of the interval of values of
\omega
’s for which the inequality holds true, hence at first we can bound the probability with
l/2N
.
We proceed now to give an upper bound for
l
.

(5.14)

\displaystyle|y_{1}|

\displaystyle=|x\sin(\omega_{1}z)+y\cos(\omega_{1}z)|

(5.15)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}\left|\sin\left(\omega_{1}z+\arctan\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)\right)\right|

From this, we can deduce

(5.16)

\displaystyle|y_{1}|\leq\varepsilon

\displaystyle\iff\left|\sin\left(\omega_{1}z+\arctan\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)\right)\right|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}}.

Now, for
y\geq 0
we have
\arctan\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)\in[0,\pi]
and for
NR\leq\frac{\pi}{2}
we see that

(5.17)

\displaystyle-\frac{\pi}{2}\leq\omega_{1}z+\arctan\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)\leq\frac{3\pi}{2}.

Moreover,
\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}}\leq 2\varepsilon
for
R>0
small enough. Hence,
|y_{1}|\leq\varepsilon
if and only if either

(5.18)

\displaystyle\left|\omega_{1}z+\arctan\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)\right|\leq\arcsin\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}}\right)

or

(5.19)

\displaystyle\left|\omega_{1}z+\arctan\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)-\pi\right|\leq\arcsin\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}}\right).

As a result,

(5.20)

\displaystyle\left|\left\{\omega_{1}\in[-N,N]:\left|\sin\left(\omega_{1}z+\arctan\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)\right)\right|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}}\right\}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{|z|}\arcsin\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}}\right)\leq\frac{4\varepsilon}{|z|}

Here we have used that the size of the interval is independent of shifts, and that, for small
\varepsilon
it holds
\arcsin(\varepsilon)\leq 2\varepsilon
.
∎

We are now in position to prove a local drift for
V_{\mathsf{x}}
following the ideas of
[
12
]
*Lemma 13.

Lemma 5.7
.

There exists
s_{0}>0
small and
N>1
large such that for all
p\in\mathsf{Q}_{\mathsf{x}}(s_{0})
and
\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in[-N,N]^{2}
there holds

(5.21)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[V_{\mathsf{x}}(f_{\omega}(p))\right]\leq\gamma V_{\mathsf{x}}(p),

for
\gamma=\frac{95}{100}<1
.

Proof.

Let
N>1
and
s_{0}>0
to be determined along the proof. First of all, for all
\delta_{1}>0
small there exists
c_{1}>0
sufficiently small such that for
p\in\mathsf{Q}_{\mathsf{x}}(s_{0})
and
\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in[-N,N]^{2}
we have that
p_{1}=f_{\omega_{1}}^{3}(p)
satisfies

(5.22)

\displaystyle(1-|x_{1}|)+|y_{1}|+|z_{1}|\leq\delta_{1}

provided that
Ns_{0}\leq c_{1}
. Similarly, for all
\delta_{2}>0
there exists
c_{2}>0
sufficiently small such that
for all
p\in\mathsf{Q}_{\mathsf{x}}(s_{0})
and all
\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in[-N,N]^{2}
we have that
p_{2}=f_{\omega_{2}}^{2}(p_{1})
satisfies

(5.23)

\displaystyle(1-|x_{2}|)+|y_{2}|+|z_{2}|\leq\delta_{2}

if
N^{2}s_{0}\leq c_{2}
. In what follows, we shall assume that (
5.22
) and (
5.23
) hold for
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}>0
sufficiently small and that
c_{1},c_{2}>0
are sufficiently small as well. We observe that
y_{2}=y_{1}
and we distinguish two cases according to the value of
V_{\mathsf{x}}(p)

\diamond

Case
0\leq|y|\leq|z|
.
Hence,
V_{\mathsf{x}}(p)=|z|^{-\alpha}
.

(1)

Let
E_{1}=\{\omega_{1}\in[-N,N]:|y_{1}|\leq\frac{|z|}{4N}\}
. Since

(5.24)

\displaystyle y_{1}=x\sin(\omega_{1}z)+y\cos(\omega_{1}z)

we easily see that
\mathbb{P}(E_{1})\leq\frac{1}{2N^{2}}
. Moreover, for
(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in E_{1}\times[-N,N]
we now have
|\sin(\omega_{2}y_{1})|\leq\frac{|z|}{4}
so that

(5.25)

\displaystyle|z_{2}|\geq|z||\cos(\omega_{2}y_{1})|-|x_{1}||\sin(\omega_{2}y_{1})|\geq\frac{|z|}{2}

and thus
V_{\mathsf{x}}(p_{2})\leq 2^{\alpha}V_{\mathsf{x}}(p)
. Here we used that
|\cos(\omega_{2}y_{1})|\geq\sqrt{1-(\tfrac{|z|}{4})^{2}}\geq\frac{3}{4}
for
|z|\leq 1
.

(2)

Let
E_{2}=\{\omega_{1}\in[-N,N]:\frac{|z|}{4N}<|y_{1}|\leq 2|z|\}
. Now, we have
\mathbb{P}(E_{2})\leq\frac{4}{N}
and we further observe that
V_{\mathsf{x}}(p_{2})\leq(4N)^{\alpha}V_{\mathsf{x}}(p)
for all
(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in E_{2}\times[-N,N]
.

(3)

Let
E_{3}=\{\omega_{1}\in[-N,N]:2|z|<|y_{1}|\}
. We note that for all
(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in E_{3}\times[-N,N]
we have
V_{\mathsf{x}}(p_{2})\leq 2^{-\alpha}V_{\mathsf{x}}(p)
.

Therefore, we have that

(5.26)

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[V_{\mathsf{x}}(f_{\omega}(p))\right]&=\mathbb{E}\left[V_{\mathsf{x}}(f_{\omega}(p))|E_{1}\right]\mathbb{P}(E_{1})+\mathbb{E}\left[V_{\mathsf{x}}(f_{\omega}(p))|E_{2}\right]\mathbb{P}(E_{2})+\mathbb{E}\left[V_{\mathsf{x}}(f_{\omega}(p))|E_{3}\right]\mathbb{P}(E_{3})\\
&\leq\left(\frac{2^{\alpha-1}}{N^{2}}+\frac{4^{1+\alpha}}{N^{1-\alpha}}+2^{-\alpha}\right)V_{\mathsf{x}}(p).\end{split}

\diamond

Case
0\leq|z|\leq|y|
.
We now have
V_{\mathsf{x}}(p)=|y|^{-\alpha}
and we consider the next four settings.

(1)

Let
E_{1}=\{\omega_{1}\in[-N,N]:|y_{1}|\leq\frac{|z|}{4N}\}
. As before, we have
\mathbb{P}(E_{1})\leq\frac{1}{2N^{2}}
and
|z_{2}|\geq\frac{|z|}{2}
as well. Additionally, we note that for
E_{1}
to be non-empty we should have

(5.27)

\displaystyle-\frac{|z|}{4N}-x\sin(\omega_{1}z)\leq y\cos(\omega_{1}z)\leq\frac{|z|}{4N}-x\sin(\omega_{1}z).

In particular, we observe that
|y\cos(\omega_{1}z)|\leq\frac{|z|}{4N}+|\sin(\omega_{1}z)|\leq 2N|z|
and since
|\cos(\omega_{1}z)|\geq\frac{3}{4}
because
|z|\leq s_{0}
and
Ns_{0}\leq c_{1}
is small enough, we further deduce that
\frac{3|y|}{16N}\leq\frac{|z|}{2}\leq|z_{2}|
. Therefore,
V_{\mathsf{x}}(p_{2})\leq(\tfrac{16N}{3})^{\alpha}V_{\mathsf{x}}(p)
for
(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in E_{1}\times[-N,N]
.

(2)

We now define
E_{2}=\{\omega_{1}\in[-N,N]:\frac{|z|}{4N}<|y_{1}|\leq\frac{|y|}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}\}
, so that
\mathbb{P}(E_{2})\leq\frac{2}{N^{\frac{3}{2}}}\frac{|y|}{|z|}
. For
E_{2}
to be non-empty, we now need

(5.28)

\displaystyle-\frac{|y|}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}-x\sin(\omega_{1}z)\leq y\cos(\omega_{1}z)\leq\frac{|y|}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}-x\sin(\omega_{1}z)

which gives
|y\cos(\omega_{1}z)|\leq N|z|+\frac{|y|}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}
. Therefore, we have that
\left(\frac{3}{4}-N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)|y|\leq N|z|
and thus
|y|\leq 2N|z|
for
N
large enough. Hence,
\mathbb{P}(E_{2})\leq\frac{4}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}
,
|y_{2}|=|y_{1}|\geq\frac{|z|}{4N}\geq\frac{|y|}{8N^{2}}
and thus
V_{\mathsf{x}}(p_{2})\leq(8N^{2})^{\alpha}V_{\mathsf{x}}(p)
, for all
(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in E_{2}\times[-N,N]
.

(3)

We next set
E_{3}=\{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in[-N,N]:|y_{1}|>N^{-\frac{1}{2}}|y|\text{ and }|\sin(\omega_{2}N^{-\frac{1}{2}}|y|)|\leq 4|y|\}
. Then, we must have
|\omega_{2}|\leq 8N^{\frac{1}{2}}
so that
\mathbb{P}(E_{3})\leq\frac{8}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}
and
V_{\mathsf{x}}(p_{2})\leq N^{\alpha/2}V_{\mathsf{x}}(p)
.

(4)

Finally, for
E_{4}=(E_{1}\cup E_{2}\cup E_{3})^{c}
we have that
|\sin(\omega_{2}N^{-\frac{1}{2}}|y|)|>4|y|
and
|y_{1}|>N^{-\frac{1}{2}}|y|
. In particular, we deduce that

(5.29)

\displaystyle|z_{2}|\geq|x_{1}||\sin(\omega_{2}y_{1})|-|z|\geq\frac{3}{4}|\sin(\omega_{2}N^{-\frac{1}{2}}|y|)|-|y|\geq 2|y|

because
|x_{1}|\geq\frac{3}{4}
,
|\sin(z)|
is increasing in
z\in(0,\pi/2)
and
|z|\leq|y|
. Thus,
V_{\mathsf{x}}(p_{2})\leq 2^{-\alpha}V_{\mathsf{x}}(p)
in
E_{4}
.

Now, there holds

(5.30)

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[V_{\mathsf{x}}(f_{\omega}(p))\right]&=\mathbb{E}\left[V_{\mathsf{x}}(f_{\omega}(p))|E_{1}\right]\mathbb{P}(E_{1})+\mathbb{E}\left[V_{\mathsf{x}}(f_{\omega}(p))|E_{2}\right]\mathbb{P}(E_{2})\\
&\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[V_{\mathsf{x}}(f_{\omega}(p))|E_{3}\right]\mathbb{P}(E_{3})+\mathbb{E}\left[V_{\mathsf{x}}(f_{\omega}(p))|E_{4}\right]\mathbb{P}(E_{4})\\
&\leq\left(\frac{16^{\alpha}}{2\cdot 3^{\alpha}}\frac{1}{N^{2-\alpha}}+\frac{4\cdot 8^{\alpha}}{N^{\frac{1}{2}-2\alpha}}+\frac{8}{N^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha/2}}+2^{-\alpha}\right)V_{\mathsf{x}}(p)\end{split}

For
\alpha=\frac{1}{8}
,
N>1
sufficiently large and
s_{0}=\frac{c_{0}}{2N_{0}^{2}}
with
c_{0}=\min(c_{1},c_{2})
small enough we see from (
5.26
) and (
5.30
) that
\mathbb{E}\left[V_{\mathsf{x}}(f_{\omega}(p))\right]\leq\frac{95}{100}V_{\mathsf{x}}(p)
.
∎

5.3.2.
The
F_{\mathsf{z}}
fixed points

Set here
r_{\mathsf{z}}(p)=\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}
and

(5.31)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Q}_{\mathsf{z}}(R)=\{p\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}:0<r_{\mathsf{z}}(p)<R\}.

for
R>0
. For
p\in\mathsf{Q}_{\mathsf{z}}(R)
we define
V_{\mathsf{z}}(p)=r_{\mathsf{z}}^{-\alpha}(p)
. We observe that
V_{\mathsf{z}}(f_{\omega_{1}}^{3}(p))=V_{\mathsf{z}}(p)
, for all
p\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
and
\omega_{1}\in[-N,N]
, since
f_{\omega_{1}}^{3}
is a rotation along the
z
-axis and it preserves the distance to the
z
-axis.

Lemma 5.8
.

There exists
s_{0}>0
small and
N>1
large such that for all
p\in\mathsf{Q}_{{\mathsf{z}}}(s_{0})
and
\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in[-N,N]^{2}
there holds

(5.32)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[V_{\mathsf{z}}(f_{\omega}(p))\right]\leq\gamma V_{\mathsf{z}}(p),

for
\gamma=\frac{9}{10}<1
.

Proof.

To ease notation, let
r=r_{\mathsf{z}}(p)
. We assume that
y_{1}>0
and
s_{0}<\frac{1}{4}
small enough such that
z\geq 1-s_{0}
for all
p\in\mathsf{Q}_{\mathsf{z}}(s_{0})
. Let
N>1
and
M>1
, to be determined along the proof. Near the north and south poles
(0,0,1)
and
(0,0,-1)
, the first motion
f^{3}_{\omega_{1}}
rotates the initial point
p
along the
z
-axis, thus preserving
V_{{\mathsf{z}}}
. The second motion
f^{2}_{\omega_{2}}
is shearing in the
x
-direction while preserving the
y
-component. We argue near the north pole
(0,0,1)
and according to the position of the midpoint
p_{1}=(x_{1},y_{1},z_{1})=f_{\omega_{1}}^{3}(p)
.

(1)

For the set
E_{1}=\{\omega_{1}\in[-N,N]:0\leq N^{-\frac{1}{2}}|x_{1}|\leq y_{1}\}
we define the following events.

(1.1)

Let
E_{1,1}=\{\omega_{2}\in[-N,N]:|\omega_{2}|>2\frac{M+1}{1-\delta_{0}}N^{\frac{1}{2}}\}
. For
\omega_{2}\in E_{1,1}
, we have that
|x_{2}|\geq\frac{1}{2}(1-\delta_{0})|\omega_{2}|y_{1}-|x_{1}|\geq\frac{1}{2}(1-\delta_{0})|\omega_{2}|y_{1}-N^{\frac{1}{2}}y_{1}\geq MN^{\frac{1}{2}}y_{1}
, from which we see that

(5.33)

\displaystyle r_{2}\geq y_{1}\sqrt{1+M^{2}N}\geq\sqrt{\frac{1+M^{2}N}{1+N}}r_{1}

and thus
V_{\mathsf{z}}(f_{\omega}(p))\leq\left(\frac{1+N}{1+M^{2}N}\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}V_{\mathsf{z}}(p)
.

(1.2)

Set next
E_{1,2}=(E_{1,1})^{c}
. We observe that
\mathbb{P}(E_{1,2})=2\frac{M+1}{1-\delta_{0}}N^{-\frac{1}{2}}
and for
\omega_{2}\in E_{1,2}
, we further observe that
r_{2}\geq y_{1}\geq\frac{r_{1}}{\sqrt{1+N}}
so that now
V_{\mathsf{z}}(f_{\omega}(p))\leq(1+N)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}V_{\mathsf{z}}(p)
.

(2)

For the set
E_{2}=\{\omega_{1}\in[-N,N]:\frac{|x_{1}|}{2N}\leq y_{1}\leq\frac{|x_{1}|}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}\}
, we have that
r_{2}\geq y_{1}\geq\frac{r_{1}}{\sqrt{1+4N^{2}}}
and thus
V_{\mathsf{z}}(f_{\omega}(p))\leq(1+4N^{2})^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}V_{\mathsf{z}}(p)
. Moreover,
\mathbb{P}(E_{2})\lesssim N^{-\frac{1}{2}}
since
E_{2}
is a subset of
\{\omega_{1}\in[-N,N]:0\leq\beta_{1}\leq N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\}
, where
\beta_{1}=\arctan(\frac{y_{1}}{|x_{1}|})
. In turn,
\beta_{1}=\beta+\omega_{1}z
and since
\mathbb{P}(\{\omega_{1}\in[0,2\pi]:0<\beta+\omega_{1}z<N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\})\lesssim N^{-\frac{1}{2}}
we observe that we may increase the amplitude interval from
[0,2\pi]
to
[-2\pi k,2\pi k]
and yet, due to periodicity and the uniform law,
\mathbb{P}(E_{2})\leq C_{1}N^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, for some uniformly bounded
C_{1}>0
. We next set

(2.1)

E_{2,1}=\{\omega_{2}\in[-N,N]:|x_{2}|\leq\frac{y_{1}}{2}\}
. Arguing as in Lemma
5.6
we can show that
\mathbb{P}(E_{2,1})\leq N^{-1}
. Moreover,
V_{\mathsf{z}}(f_{\omega}(p))\leq(1+4N^{2})^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}V_{\mathsf{z}}(p)
.

(2.2)

E_{2,2}=\{\omega_{2}\in[-N,N]:|x_{2}|\geq\frac{y_{1}}{2}\}
. Here, we immediately have
V_{\mathsf{z}}(f_{\omega}(p))\leq 4^{\alpha}V_{\mathsf{z}}(p)
.

(3)

For the set
E_{3}=\{\omega_{1}\in[-N,N]:0<y_{1}<\frac{|x_{1}|}{2N}\}
we have

(5.34)

\displaystyle|x_{2}|\geq\frac{3}{4}|x_{1}|-|\omega_{2}|y_{1}\geq\frac{3}{4}|x_{1}|-\frac{|\omega_{2}|}{2N}|x_{1}|\geq\frac{|x_{1}|}{4}

from which we deduce that
r_{2}\geq\frac{r_{1}}{4}
and thus
V_{\mathsf{z}}(f_{\omega}(p))\leq 4^{\alpha}V_{\mathsf{z}}(p)
. Furthermore, we have
\mathbb{P}(E_{3})\leq C_{2}N^{-1}
for some
C_{2}>0
uniformly bounded.

Arguing identically for
y_{1}<0
using the symmetry under
\pi
-rotation along the
z
-axis of the flow maps, we find that

(5.35)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[V_{\mathsf{z}}(f_{\omega}(p))\right]

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}\left[V_{\mathsf{z}}(f_{\omega}(p))|E_{1,1}\right]\mathbb{P}(E_{1,1})+2\mathbb{E}\left[V_{\mathsf{z}}(f_{\omega}(p))|E_{1,2}\right]\mathbb{P}(E_{1,2})

(5.36)

\displaystyle\quad+2\mathbb{E}\left[V_{\mathsf{z}}(f_{\omega}(p))|E_{2,1}\right]\mathbb{P}(E_{2,1})+2\mathbb{E}\left[V_{\mathsf{z}}(f_{\omega}(p))|E_{2,2}\right]\mathbb{P}(E_{2,2})

(5.37)

\displaystyle\quad+2\mathbb{E}\left[V_{\mathsf{z}}(f_{\omega}(p))|E_{3}\right]\mathbb{P}(E_{3})

and gathering all the estimates above, we reach

(5.38)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[V(f_{\omega}(p))\right]}{2V(p)}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1+N}{1+M^{2}N}\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}+2\frac{M+1}{1-\delta_{0}}\frac{(1+N)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}+C_{1}\frac{(1+4N^{2})^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}+C_{2}\frac{4^{\alpha}}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}+C_{3}\frac{4^{\alpha}}{N}

For
\alpha=\frac{1}{8}\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and
N\geq 1
we see that
\frac{1+N}{1+M^{2}N}\leq\frac{2}{M^{2}}
and thus

(5.39)

\displaystyle 2\left(\frac{1+N}{1+M^{2}N}\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\leq 2\frac{2^{\frac{1}{16}}}{M^{\frac{1}{8}}}=\frac{4}{5}

for
M=(5/2)^{8}\sqrt{2}
. Next, we choose
N>1
large enough such that

(5.40)

\displaystyle 2\frac{M+1}{1-\delta_{0}}\frac{(1+N)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}+C_{1}\frac{(1+4N^{2})^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}+C_{2}\frac{4^{\alpha}}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}+C_{3}\frac{4^{\alpha}}{N}\leq\frac{1}{20}.

Therefore, we have

(5.41)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[V_{\mathsf{z}}(f_{\omega}(p))\right]\leq\frac{9}{10}V_{\mathsf{z}}(p).

∎

Figure 5
.
Division of the
(x_{1},y_{1})
upper half-plane in three regions
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
, and
E_{3}
for the
F_{\mathsf{z}}
fixed points.

5.3.3.
The
F_{\mathsf{y}}
fixed points

For
p=(x,y,z)\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
we now define
r_{\mathsf{y}}(p)=\sqrt{x^{2}+z^{2}}
and

(5.42)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Q}_{\mathsf{y}}(R)=\{p\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}:0<r_{\mathsf{y}}(p)<R\}

for
R>0
. For
p\in\mathsf{Q}_{\mathsf{y}}(R)
we similarly set
\widetilde{V}_{\mathsf{y}}(p)=r_{\mathsf{y}}^{-\alpha}(p)
and for
N>0
we further set
{V}_{\mathsf{y}}(p)=\widetilde{V}_{\mathsf{y}}(f_{N}^{3}(p))
.

Lemma 5.9
.

There exists
s_{0}>0
small and
N>1
large such that for all
p\in\mathsf{Q}_{{\mathsf{y}}}(s_{0})
and
\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in[-N,N]^{2}
there holds

(5.43)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{V}_{\mathsf{y}}(f_{\omega}(p))\right]\leq\gamma{V}_{\mathsf{y}}(p),

for
\gamma=\frac{9}{10}<1
.

Proof.

Let
p\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
,
\overline{p}=f_{N}^{3}(p)
and
q_{\omega_{1}}=f_{\omega_{1}}^{3}(p)
. Hence,
p=f_{-N}^{3}(\overline{p})
and
q_{\omega_{1}}=f_{\omega_{1}-N}^{3}(\overline{p})
. Moreover,
{V}_{\mathsf{y}}(p)=\widetilde{V}_{\mathsf{y}}(\overline{p})
and
{V}_{\mathsf{y}}(f_{\omega}(p))=\widetilde{V}_{\mathsf{y}}(f_{N}^{3}\circ f_{\omega_{2}}^{2}(q_{\omega_{1}}))
. Arguing as in the proof of Lemma
5.8
, we see that

(5.44)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\omega_{2}}\left[{V}_{\mathsf{y}}(f_{\omega}(p))\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\omega_{2}}\left[\widetilde{V}_{\mathsf{y}}(f_{N}^{3}\circ f_{\omega_{2}}^{2}(q_{\omega_{1}}))\right]

(5.45)

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\left(\frac{1+N}{1+M^{2}N}\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}+C_{1}\frac{(1+4N^{2})^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}+C_{2}\frac{4^{\alpha}}{N}\right)\widetilde{V}_{\mathsf{y}}(q_{\omega_{1}}).

Next, for
\widetilde{\omega}_{1}=N-\omega_{1}\in[0,2N]
we observe that
q_{\omega_{1}}=\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{z}}(f_{\widetilde{\omega}_{1}}^{3}(\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{z}}\overline{p}))
, where
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{z}}(p)=(x,y,-z)
, for
p=(x,y,z)
. Since
\widetilde{V}_{\mathsf{y}}(\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{z}}(p))=\widetilde{V}_{\mathsf{y}}(p)
for all
p\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, we deduce from the arguments of Lemma
5.8
concerning the shear motion that

(5.46)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\omega_{1}}\left[\widetilde{V}_{\mathsf{y}}(q_{\omega_{1}})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{\omega}_{1}}\left[\widetilde{V}_{\mathsf{y}}\circ\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{z}}\circ f_{\widetilde{\omega}_{1}}^{3}\circ\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{z}}(\overline{p})\right]

(5.47)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{\omega}_{1}}\left[\widetilde{V}_{\mathsf{y}}\circ f_{\widetilde{\omega}_{1}}^{3}\circ\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{z}}(\overline{p})\right]

(5.48)

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\left(\frac{1+N}{1+M^{2}N}\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}+2\frac{M+1}{1-\delta_{0}}\frac{(1+N)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\frac{(1+4N^{2})^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}{N}+4^{\alpha}\right)\widetilde{V}_{\mathsf{y}}(\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{z}}(\overline{p}))

(5.49)

\displaystyle\leq 4^{\alpha+1}\widetilde{V}_{\mathsf{y}}(\overline{p})

(5.50)

\displaystyle=4^{\alpha+1}V_{\mathsf{y}}(p),

for
M
and
N
sufficiently large and
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Note that here we cannot appeal to the probability of being at
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
or
E_{3}
since
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{z}}(\overline{p})
is given and does not experience rotating motion, it only undergoes shear motion. Therefore,

(5.51)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}\left[V_{\mathsf{y}}(f_{\omega}(p))\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\left(\frac{1+N}{1+M^{2}N}\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}+C_{1}\frac{(1+4N^{2})^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}+C_{2}\frac{4^{\alpha}}{N}\right)4^{\alpha+1}V_{\mathsf{y}}(p)

(5.52)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{9}{10}V_{\mathsf{y}}(p),

for
\alpha=1/8
,
M>4\sqrt{2}10^{8}
and
N>1
large enough.
∎

5.3.4.
Construction of the drift

We next assemble the partial drifts we defined for
F_{\mathsf{x}}
,
F_{\mathsf{z}}
and
F_{\mathsf{y}}
and construct the Lyapunov-drift function for the one-point process. Let
s_{0}>0
as in Lemmas
5.7
-
5.9
and
\chi:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,1]
be a smooth non-negative bump function such that

(5.53)

\displaystyle\chi(r)=\begin{cases}1,&r\leq s_{0},\\
0,&r\geq 2s_{0}.\end{cases}

For
p=(x,y,z)\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, recall
r_{\mathsf{x}}(p)=\max\{|y|,|z|\}
,
r_{\mathsf{y}}(p)=\sqrt{x^{2}+z^{2}}
and
r_{\mathsf{z}}(p)=\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}
and also

(5.54)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Q}_{\sigma}(s)=\{p\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}:r_{\sigma}(p)<s\},

with the sets
\sigma\in\{{\mathsf{x}},\mathsf{y},{\mathsf{z}}\}=\Sigma
denote the spherical caps centred at the six fixed points. For
s>0
small enough, the
\mathsf{Q}_{\sigma}(s)
are mutually disjoint. We then define

(5.55)

\begin{split}\mathrm{V}(p)&:=\chi(r_{\mathsf{x}})(1-\chi(r_{\mathsf{y}}))(1-\chi(r_{\mathsf{z}}))V_{\mathsf{x}}(p)+\chi(r_{\mathsf{y}})(1-\chi(r_{\mathsf{z}})){V}_{\mathsf{y}}(p)+\chi(r_{\mathsf{z}})V_{\mathsf{z}}(p)\\
&\quad+\hat{c}(1-\chi(r_{\mathsf{x}}))(1-\chi(r_{\mathsf{y}}))(1-\chi(r_{\mathsf{z}}))\end{split}

where we choose
\hat{c}>0
so that
\mathrm{V}\geq 1
and
V_{\mathsf{x}}
,
V_{\mathsf{z}}
and
{V}_{\mathsf{y}}
are given as in Lemma
5.7
,
5.8
and
5.9
, respectively. We further observe that for all
\sigma\in\{{\mathsf{x}},\mathsf{y},{\mathsf{z}}\}
there exists
0<r_{0}<s_{0}
independent of
\sigma
such that
f_{\omega}(p)\in\mathsf{Q}_{\sigma}(s_{0})
for all
p\in\mathsf{Q}_{\sigma}(r_{0})
and all
\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in[-N,N]^{2}
.

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
K(r_{0})=\cap_{\sigma\in\{{\mathsf{x}},\mathsf{y},{\mathsf{z}}\}}\mathsf{Q}_{\sigma}(r_{0})^{c}
. There exists
\beta>0
such that

(5.56)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{V}(f_{\omega}(p))\right]\leq\frac{95}{100}\mathrm{V}(p)+\beta\mathbf{1}_{K(r_{0})}(p).

for all
p\in X
.

Proof.

If
p\in\mathsf{Q}_{\sigma}(r_{0})\subset\mathsf{Q}_{\sigma}(s_{0})
, for some
\sigma\in\{{\mathsf{x}},\mathsf{y},{\mathsf{z}}\}
we then have that
f_{\omega}(p)\in\mathsf{Q}_{\sigma}(s_{0})
for all
\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in[-N,N]^{2}
so that
\chi(r_{\sigma^{\prime}})=0
for all
\sigma^{\prime}\neq\sigma
and from Lemma
5.7
,
5.8
and
5.9
we have that

(5.57)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{V}(f_{\omega}(p))\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[V_{\sigma}(f_{\omega}(p))\right]\leq\gamma V_{\sigma}(p)=\gamma\mathrm{V}(p).

On the other hand, for all
p\in K
a compact set, we observe that
f_{\omega}(p)\not\in F
, for all values
\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in[-N,N]^{2}
lying in a compact set as well. Hence, we set
\hat{\beta}=\max_{p\in K,\omega\in[-N,N]^{2}}\mathrm{V}(f_{\omega}(p))
and we conclude that
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{V}(f_{\omega}(p))\right]\leq\hat{\beta}+\hat{c}
. For
\beta:=\hat{\beta}+\hat{c}
the proposition is proved.
∎

Remark 5.11
.

In case the two axes of rotation are non-orthogonal, namely
{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{b}}\neq 0
, there are only two fixed points for the one-point process, namely
{\mathbf{x}}=\frac{{\boldsymbol{a}}\times{\boldsymbol{b}}}{|{\boldsymbol{a}}\times{\boldsymbol{b}}|}
and
{\mathbf{x}}=-\frac{{\boldsymbol{a}}\times{\boldsymbol{b}}}{|{\boldsymbol{a}}\times{\boldsymbol{b}}|}
. These correspond to the
F_{{\mathsf{x}}}
points already considered, since both
u^{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
u^{\boldsymbol{b}}
generate a (non-orthogonal yet also non-parallel) shearing motion around them, and simple adjustments to the arguments presented in Lemma
5.7
provide the existence of local drift functions around these points and then of a global drift function gluing the local contributions appropriately. We omit the details.

6.
The projective process

To use the LARC arguments introduced in Section
4
, we lift the pointwise projective process to the unit tangent bundle
UT(\mathsf{X})
. For an axis
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
and its respective velocity field
u^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\mathbf{x}})=({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})({\boldsymbol{a}}\times{\mathbf{x}})
we define

(6.1)

\hat{u}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\mathbf{x}},v)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\omega}\hat{f}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}},v)|_{\omega=0}=(u^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\mathbf{x}}),V_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\mathbf{x}},v)),

where the tangential velocity field is given by

(6.2)

V_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\mathbf{x}},v)=D_{\mathbf{x}}u^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\mathbf{x}})v-(D_{\mathbf{x}}u^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\mathbf{x}})v\cdot v)v

and the differential is given by

(6.3)

D_{\mathbf{x}}u^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\mathbf{x}})v=({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot v)({\boldsymbol{a}}\times{\mathbf{x}})+({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})({\boldsymbol{a}}\times v).

One can check that
\hat{u}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}
generates a flow in
UT(\mathsf{X})
by showing
|v(t)|^{2}=|v(0)|^{2}=1
and that
{\mathbf{x}}(t)\cdot v(t)={\mathbf{x}}(0)\cdot v(0)=0
for all times. This flow is the one associated to
\hat{f}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}},v)
.

To show that there exists an open small set for the projective process, we appeal to the Lie algebra rank condition framework. First, we show there exists
{\mathbf{x}}_{0}\in\mathsf{X}
and
v_{0}\in UT_{{\mathbf{x}}_{0}}\mathsf{X}
for which the family
\{\hat{u}_{\boldsymbol{a}},\hat{u}_{\boldsymbol{b}}\}
is bracket generating at
({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\boldsymbol{v}}_{0})
and next we define the appropriate flow maps for which we can use Propositions
4.6
and
6.1
.

To use the abstract LARC framework, let
u\in\mathfrak{X}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
be divergence-free and let
\phi_{t}^{u}
be the unique volume-preserving flow such that

(6.4)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})&=u(\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})),\\
\phi_{0}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})&={\mathbf{x}}.\end{aligned}\right.

The associated projective process is then given by

(6.5)

\displaystyle\hat{\phi}_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}},v)=\left(\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}}),\frac{D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})v}{|D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})v|}\right),

for all
{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathsf{X}
and all
v\in UT_{{\mathbf{x}}}\mathsf{X}
. Since both
v\in UT_{{\mathbf{x}}}\mathsf{X}
and
\frac{D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})v}{|D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})v|}\in UT_{\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})}\mathsf{X}
live in different spaces that depend on
x,\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})\in\mathsf{X}
, we use a local orthonormal reference frame
E_{0}
, see Section
3
, to fix the domain and codomain of the map
\hat{\phi}_{t}^{u}
. More precisely, let
{\mathbf{x}}\in U_{3}
and let
{\mathcal{U}}\subset U_{3}
be an open set on
\mathsf{X}
with
{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathcal{U}}
. Since
UT({\mathcal{U}})\cong{\mathcal{U}}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
, we describe
\hat{\phi}_{t}^{u}
by its action on
{\mathcal{U}}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
. To do so, let
{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathsf{X}
, let
w\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
and
v=E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})w\in T_{{\mathbf{x}}}\mathsf{X}
. Since
\frac{D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})v}{|D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})v|}\in UT_{\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})}\mathsf{X}
and
\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})\in{\mathcal{U}}
for
|t|
small enough, there exists
w_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}},w)\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
such that
\frac{D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})w}{|D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})w|}=E_{0}(\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}}))w_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}},w)
, that is,

(6.6)

\displaystyle w_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}},w)=E_{0}(\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}}))^{T}\frac{D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})w}{|D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})w|}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}.

Define the projective flow map
\widetilde{\hat{\phi}_{t}^{u}}:{\mathcal{U}}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathcal{U}}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
by

(6.7)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\hat{\phi}_{t}^{u}}({\mathbf{x}},w):=\left(\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}}),E_{0}(\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}}))^{T}\frac{D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})w}{|D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})w|}\right)

together with its associated projective velocity field

(6.8)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\hat{u}}({\mathbf{x}},w):=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}\widetilde{\hat{\phi}_{t}^{u}}({\mathbf{x}},w)=\left(u({\mathbf{x}}),\mathsf{w}_{u}({\mathbf{x}},w)\right),

where, for
{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathsf{X}
and
w\in\mathbb{S}^{1}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, we define
\mathsf{w}_{u}({\mathbf{x}},w)\in T_{w}\mathbb{S}^{1}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
by

(6.9)

\displaystyle\mathsf{w}_{u}({\mathbf{x}},w)=E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})^{T}V_{u}({\mathbf{x}},E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})w)-E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})^{T}(\nabla_{u}E_{0}({\mathbf{x}}))w,

with

(6.10)

\displaystyle V_{u}({\mathbf{x}},v):=\nabla_{v}u({\mathbf{x}})-(v\cdot\nabla_{v}u({\mathbf{x}}))v

for
v=E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})w
. Since
v\in T\mathsf{X}
, we have that
v\cdot\nabla_{v}u({\mathbf{x}})=v({\mathbf{x}})\cdot D_{\mathbf{x}}u({\mathbf{x}})v({\mathbf{x}})
. Together with Lemma
3.1
and
E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})^{T}E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})=I_{2}
, it follows that

(6.11)

\displaystyle\mathsf{w}_{u}({\mathbf{x}},w)=E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})^{T}D_{\mathbf{x}}u({\mathbf{x}})E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})w-\left(v({\mathbf{x}})\cdot D_{\mathbf{x}}u({\mathbf{x}})v({\mathbf{x}})\right)w-E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})^{T}(\nabla_{u}E_{0}({\mathbf{x}}))w.

The next result shows that there exists
{\mathbf{x}}_{0}\in\mathsf{X}
and
v_{0}\in UT_{{\mathbf{x}}_{0}}\mathsf{X}
(equivalently
w_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
) for which the family
\{\widetilde{\hat{u}}_{\boldsymbol{a}},\widetilde{\hat{u}}_{\boldsymbol{b}}\}
is bracket generating at
({\mathbf{x}}_{0},v_{0})
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Assume
{\boldsymbol{a}}=(0,a_{2},a_{3})\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
with
a_{2}\neq 0
and
{\boldsymbol{b}}=(0,0,1)
. Let
{\mathbf{x}}_{0}=(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},0)\in\mathsf{X}
and
v_{0}=(0,0,1)
. Then the family
\{\widetilde{\hat{u}}_{\boldsymbol{a}},\widetilde{\hat{u}}_{\boldsymbol{b}}\}
is bracket generating at
({\mathbf{x}}_{0},v_{0})
.
More precisely,

(6.12)

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{1}:=\widetilde{\hat{u}}_{\boldsymbol{a}},\quad\hat{X}_{2}:=\widetilde{\hat{u}}_{\boldsymbol{b}},\quad\hat{X}_{3}:=[\hat{X}_{2},\hat{X}_{1}]

are linearly independent vectors at
({\mathbf{x}}_{0},v_{0})
.

Proof.

We recall that
E_{0}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=(e_{1},e_{2})
, with
e_{1}=e_{1}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=(0,0,1)
and
e_{2}=e_{2}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},0\right)
. Then,
v_{0}=e_{1}=E_{0}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})w_{0}
, for
w_{0}=(1,0)\in\mathbb{S}^{1}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and
(\nabla_{u}E_{0})({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=0
for all
u\in T\mathsf{X}
due to Lemma
3.2
. Next, from (
6.11
) we note that

(6.13)

\displaystyle u^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=\frac{a_{2}}{2}(-a_{3},a_{3},-a_{2})=-\frac{a_{2}^{2}}{2}e_{1}-a_{2}a_{3}\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}e_{2}\in T_{{\mathbf{x}}_{0}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}

and

(6.14)

\displaystyle\mathsf{w}_{u_{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},w_{0})=(0,\frac{a_{2}^{2}-2a_{3}^{2}}{2})\in T_{w_{0}}\mathbb{S}^{1}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}

while

(6.15)

\displaystyle u^{\boldsymbol{b}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=0,\quad\mathsf{w}_{u_{\boldsymbol{b}}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},w_{0})=(0,-1)\in T_{w_{0}}\mathbb{S}^{1}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}.

Identifying
T_{w_{0}}\mathbb{S}^{1}=\{0\}\times{\mathbb{R}}\cong{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

(6.16)

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{1}=\left(-\frac{a_{2}^{2}}{2}e_{1}-a_{2}a_{3}\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}e_{2},\frac{a_{2}^{2}-2a_{3}^{2}}{2}\right),\quad\hat{X}_{2}=\left(0,-1\right)

Next, we compute
\hat{X}_{3}=[\hat{X}_{2},\hat{X}_{1}]
. In fact, for our purposes it is enough to compute
[u^{\boldsymbol{b}},u^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}]({\mathbf{x}}_{0})
. Since
u^{\boldsymbol{b}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=0
, we have

(6.17)

[u^{\boldsymbol{b}},u^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}]({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=-D_{\mathbf{x}}u^{\boldsymbol{b}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})u^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}(-a_{2}^{2},a_{2}^{2},0)=-\frac{a_{2}^{2}}{2}e_{2}

and we write
\hat{X}_{3}=(-\frac{a_{2}^{2}}{2\sqrt{2}}e_{2},w_{*})
, for some
w_{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. To see that
\{\hat{X}_{1},\hat{X}_{2},\hat{X}_{3}\}
are linearly independent, assume there exist
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
c_{1}\hat{X}_{1}+c_{2}\hat{X}_{2}+c_{3}\hat{X}_{3}=0
. Since
\hat{X}_{1}
is the only vector with non-trivial
e_{1}
component (recall
a_{2}\neq 0
), we deduce that
c_{1}=0
. Next, since
u^{\boldsymbol{b}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=0
we must have
c_{3}=0
since
\hat{X}_{3}
has non-trivial
e_{2}
component. Finally, we obtain
c_{2}=0
as
\hat{X}_{2}
has non-zero tangential vector at
w_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
. Hence,
\{\widetilde{\hat{u}}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}},\widetilde{\hat{u}}_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}\}
is bracket generating at
({\mathbf{x}}_{0},v_{0})
and the proof is concluded.
∎

Since
{\mathcal{U}}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\subset{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
once we identify
\mathbb{S}^{1}
as the unit circle of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
centred at the origin, for
U_{0}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, with
{\mathcal{U}}\subset U_{0}
and
U_{0}\cap\{\lambda\mathbf{e}_{3}:\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\}=\emptyset
, we define the extension
\hat{v}_{u}:U_{0}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
of
\widetilde{\hat{u}}
by

(6.18)

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{u}({\mathbf{y}},w)=(u({\mathbf{y}}),\mathsf{w}_{u}({\mathbf{y}},w))

for
{\mathbf{y}}\in U_{0}
and
w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, where now
\Pi_{\mathbf{y}}=Id-\frac{{\mathbf{y}}{\mathbf{y}}^{T}}{\|{\mathbf{y}}\|^{2}}
and the covariant derivative
\nabla
is extended accordingly. Clearly, this extension is such that
\hat{v}_{u}({\mathbf{x}},w)=\widetilde{\hat{u}}({\mathbf{x}},w)
, for all
{\mathbf{x}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
and all
w\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
and thus if
\hat{\Phi}_{t}^{u}:U_{0}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\rightarrow U_{0}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
denotes the flow map defined by
\hat{v}_{u}
, namely

(6.19)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\hat{\Phi}_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{y}},w)=\hat{v}_{u}(\hat{\Phi}_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{y}},w)),&\\
\hat{\Phi}_{0}^{u}({\mathbf{y}},w)=({\mathbf{y}},w)&\end{cases}

then
\hat{\Phi}_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}},w)=\widetilde{\hat{\phi}_{t}^{u}}({\mathbf{x}},w)
, for all
{\mathbf{x}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
,
w\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
and all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
for which
\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})\in{\mathcal{U}}
.

Proposition 6.2
.

The projective process admits an open small set.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}=\{\hat{v}_{u_{\boldsymbol{a}}},\hat{v}_{u_{\boldsymbol{b}}}\}
and
\hat{\Phi}_{t}^{u_{\boldsymbol{a}}}
and
\hat{\Phi}_{t}^{u_{\boldsymbol{b}}}
be their respective flow maps in
U_{0}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
. The vector fields
\hat{v}_{u_{\boldsymbol{a}}},\hat{v}_{u_{\boldsymbol{b}}},[\hat{v}_{u_{\boldsymbol{a}}},\hat{v}_{u_{\boldsymbol{b}}}]
are linearly independent at
{\mathbf{x}}_{0}=(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},0)
and
v_{0}=(0,0,1)
, corresponding to
w_{0}=(1,0)\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
, due to Proposition
6.1
. Hence, thanks to Proposition
4.6
there exist words
\varpi_{i}
, for
i=1,2,3
associated to each
\hat{v}_{u_{\boldsymbol{a}}},\hat{v}_{u_{\boldsymbol{b}}}
, and
[\hat{v}_{u_{\boldsymbol{a}}},\hat{v}_{u_{\boldsymbol{b}}}]
, and there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that
D_{\varpi}\hat{\Phi}_{\varpi_{*}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},w_{0})
has at least rank 3, for
\varpi_{*}=(\varpi_{1}(a_{1}^{*}),\varpi_{2}(a_{2}^{*}),\varpi_{3}(a_{3}^{*}))\in[-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0}]^{d_{0}}
, for some
d_{0}>1
and all
a_{*}=(a_{1}^{*},a_{2}^{*},a_{3}^{*})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
with
0<\|a_{*}\|<\varepsilon_{0}
.

On the other hand, for fixed
{\mathbf{x}}_{0}=(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},0)
and
v_{0}=(0,0,1)
, namely
w_{0}=(1,0)\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
, we have that
\hat{\Phi}_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},w_{0})\in{\mathcal{U}}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
with
{\mathcal{U}}\subset{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, for
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
small enough and thus

(6.20)

\displaystyle D_{\varpi}\hat{\Phi}_{\varpi_{*}}^{u}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},w_{0}):{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{0}}\rightarrow T_{\hat{\Phi}_{\varpi_{*}}^{u}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},w_{0})}({\mathcal{U}}\times\mathbb{S}^{1})\cong{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}

has at least rank 3 due to Proposition
4.6
. Hence,
\hat{\Phi}_{\varpi}^{u}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},w_{0})
is a submersion at
\varpi=\varpi_{*}
and with Lemma
2.4
we conclude that the projective process admits an open small set.
∎

To deduce the irreducibility of the projective process we inspect the behaviour of the projective flow maps, and in particular how the differential maps act on the tangent bundle. Reducing the dynamics to a suitable point on the sphere, an interesting “windshield wiper” behaviour arises: alternating the differentials of the flow maps for specific times moves vectors around the chosen base point and spans its whole tangent space. We carefully rely on this intrinsic behaviour and show that Lemma
2.5
can be directly applied to prove topological irreducibility of the projective process.

Proposition 6.3
.

The projective process is topologically irreducible.

Proof.

We shall show that the projective process is in fact exactly controllable, that is, for all
({\mathbf{x}},v_{0}),({\mathbf{y}},v_{1})\in\mathsf{Y}=UT(\mathsf{X})
there exist
n\geq 1
and a sequence of random amplitudes
\underline{\omega}^{n}
such that
f_{\underline{\omega}^{n}}({\mathbf{x}},v_{0})=({\mathbf{y}},v_{1})
, to then apply Lemma
2.5
. Upon noting that
(\hat{f}_{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{a}})^{-1}=\hat{f}_{-\omega}^{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
(\hat{f}_{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{b}})^{-1}=\hat{f}_{-\omega}^{\boldsymbol{b}}
for all
\omega\in{\mathbb{R}}
, it is enough to map both
({\mathbf{x}},v_{0})
and
({\mathbf{y}},v_{1})
to some common point
({\mathbf{x}}_{0},v)\in\mathsf{Y}
. For convenience, we choose
{\mathbf{x}}_{0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(1,1,0)
and
v=(0,0,1)\in UT_{{\mathbf{x}}_{0}}\mathsf{X}
and we assume without loss of generality that we already have
{\mathbf{x}}={\mathbf{x}}_{0}
and
v_{0}\in UT_{{\mathbf{x}}_{0}}\mathsf{X}
. The proof is concluded if we can find
\underline{\omega}
such that
\hat{f}_{\underline{\omega}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},v_{0})=({\mathbf{x}}_{0},v)
.

First, we shall only consider
\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
\omega_{\boldsymbol{b}}
such that
{\mathbf{x}}_{0}=f_{\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=f_{\omega_{\boldsymbol{b}}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})
. Since
u^{\boldsymbol{b}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=0
we note that
f_{\omega_{\boldsymbol{b}}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})={\mathbf{x}}_{0}
, for all
\omega_{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Similarly,
f_{\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})={\mathbf{x}}_{0}
if
\theta({\mathbf{x}}_{0})\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}=2\pi k
, for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, where we recall that
\theta({\mathbf{x}}_{0})={\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}_{0}=\frac{a_{2}}{\sqrt{2}}
. Hence, we have
f_{\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})={\mathbf{x}}_{0}
for all
\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}(k)=\frac{2\sqrt{2}\pi}{a_{2}}k
, with
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Restricted to such
\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}
, in the orthonormal reference frame
E_{0}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})
given by

(6.21)

\displaystyle e_{1}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=(0,0,1),\quad e_{2}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},0\right)

the differentials
D_{\mathbf{x}}f^{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\omega_{\boldsymbol{b}}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})
and
D_{\mathbf{x}}f^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})
are given by the
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
matrices

(6.22)

\displaystyle\mathrm{g}_{\boldsymbol{b}}(\omega_{\boldsymbol{b}})=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\omega_{\boldsymbol{b}}&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathrm{g}_{\boldsymbol{a}}(\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}})=\begin{pmatrix}1-\frac{\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}a_{2}a_{3}}{\sqrt{2}}&\frac{a_{2}^{2}\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}}{2}\\
-{\boldsymbol{a}}_{3}^{2}\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}&1+\frac{\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}a_{2}a_{3}}{\sqrt{2}}\end{pmatrix}.

By definition, they are such that

(6.23)

\displaystyle D_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\omega_{{\mathbf{e}}}}^{{\mathbf{e}}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})v=E_{0}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})\mathrm{g}_{\mathbf{e}}(\omega_{\mathbf{e}})w

for
v=E_{0}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})w\in T_{{\mathbf{x}}_{0}}\mathsf{X}
with
w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and
{\mathbf{e}}\in\{{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}}\}
. Now, in view of the orthonormal basis
E_{0}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})
, showing that for all non-zero
v\in T_{{\mathbf{x}}_{0}}\mathsf{X}
there exists
\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}(k),\omega_{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
D_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\omega_{\boldsymbol{b}}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})D_{\mathbf{x}}f_{\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}(k)}^{\boldsymbol{a}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})v=\lambda e_{1}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})
for some
\lambda>0
is equivalent to showing that for all non-zero
w=(w_{1},w_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
there exists
\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}(k),\omega_{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\mathrm{g}_{\boldsymbol{b}}(\omega_{\boldsymbol{b}})\mathrm{g}_{\boldsymbol{a}}(\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}(k))w=\lambda(1,0)
, for some
\lambda>0
.

To achieve this controllability in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, let
w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
,
w^{(1)}=\mathrm{g}_{\boldsymbol{a}}(\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}(k))w
and observe that

(6.24)

\displaystyle w^{(1)}=\begin{pmatrix}\left(1-\frac{\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}a_{2}a_{3}}{\sqrt{2}}\right)w_{1}+\frac{a_{2}^{2}}{{2}}\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}(k)w_{2}\\
-w_{1}a_{3}^{2}\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}(k)+\left(1+\frac{\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}a_{2}a_{3}}{\sqrt{2}}\right)w_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

Note that if
\frac{a_{2}}{2}w_{2}-\frac{a_{3}}{\sqrt{2}}w_{1}=0
then
w^{(1)}=w
and additionally
w_{1}\neq 0
since otherwise
w_{2}=0
as
a_{2}\neq 0
. Moreover, for any
\omega_{\boldsymbol{b}}\neq 0
we would have

(6.25)

\displaystyle w^{(2)}=\mathrm{g}_{\boldsymbol{b}}(\omega_{\boldsymbol{b}})w^{(1)}=\mathrm{g}_{\boldsymbol{b}}(\omega_{\boldsymbol{b}})w=\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}\\
w_{2}-w_{1}\omega_{\boldsymbol{b}}\end{pmatrix}

and, in particular
\frac{a_{2}}{2}w_{2}^{(2)}-\frac{a_{3}}{\sqrt{2}}w_{1}^{(2)}=-\omega_{\boldsymbol{b}}\frac{a_{2}}{2}w_{1}\neq 0
. As a result, we may assume without loss that
\frac{a_{2}}{2}w_{2}-\frac{a_{3}}{\sqrt{2}}w_{1}\neq 0
. Then, since
a_{2}\neq 0
, it is easy to see that for
|\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}(k)|
large enough, that is
|k|\in{\mathbb{N}}
large enough, we have
w^{(1)}_{1}>0
. As a result,

(6.26)

\displaystyle\mathrm{g}_{\boldsymbol{b}}(\omega_{\boldsymbol{b}})w^{(1)}=\begin{pmatrix}w^{(1)}_{1}\\
w^{(1)}_{2}-\omega_{\boldsymbol{b}}w^{(1)}_{1}\end{pmatrix}=w^{(1)}_{1}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}

for
\omega_{\boldsymbol{b}}=\frac{w^{(1)}_{2}}{w^{(1)}_{1}}
. Since we can send any non-zero
w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
to the ray generated by
(1,0)
, we deduce that the map
\mathrm{g}_{\boldsymbol{b}}(\omega_{\boldsymbol{b}})\mathrm{g}_{\boldsymbol{a}}(\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}(k))
is exactly controllable in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
. Consequently,
\hat{f}_{\underline{\omega}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},v)
is also exactly controllable as a map from
\{{\mathbf{x}}_{0}\}\times UT_{{\mathbf{x}}_{0}}(\mathsf{X})
to itself for
\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}=\omega_{\boldsymbol{a}}(k)
as the normalizing factor in the projective process does not influence the direction of the final vector but only ensures it has unit norm.
∎

We finish this section recording that the projective process admits a functional satisfying the Lyapunov-drift condition.

Proposition 6.4
.

The function
\widehat{\mathrm{V}}:UT(\mathsf{X})\rightarrow[1,\infty)
given by
\widehat{\mathrm{V}}({p},v):=\mathrm{V}({p})
as defined in (
5.55
) for all
({p},v)\in UT(\mathsf{X})
satisfies the drift condition for the projective chain.

7.
Positivity of the top Lyapunov exponent

In this section we show that the top Lyapunov exponent
\lambda_{1}
associated to the one-point process
f_{\omega}
is positive. First, we prove the following equivalence statement.

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
V,U,W
be (finite-dimensional) real vector spaces and let
L:V\to U,K:V\to W
be linear maps. Define the joint linear map
F:V\to U\times W,F(v):=(Lv,Kv).
Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

F
is surjective.

(ii)

L
is surjective and the restricted map
K|_{\ker L}:\ker L\to W
is surjective (equivalently,
K(\ker L)=W
).

Proof of Proposition
7.1
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i).

Assume that
L
is surjective and that
K|_{\ker L}
is surjective.
Let
(u,w)\in U\times W
be arbitrary. Since
L
is surjective, there exists
v_{0}\in V
such that
Lv_{0}=u
.
Consider the element
w-Kv_{0}\in W
. By surjectivity of
K|_{\ker L}
, there exists
v_{1}\in\ker L
such that
Kv_{1}=w-Kv_{0}
.
Then

(7.1)

F(v_{0}+v_{1})=(L(v_{0}+v_{1}),K(v_{0}+v_{1}))=(Lv_{0}+Lv_{1},Kv_{0}+Kv_{1})=(u,w),

since
Lv_{1}=0
for
v_{1}\in\ker L
. Hence
F
is surjective.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii).

Assume that
F
is surjective. Composing with the projection

\pi_{U}:U\times W\to U
yields
\pi_{U}\circ F=L
, hence
L
is surjective.
Next, let
w\in W
be arbitrary and consider the point
(0,w)\in U\times W
. By surjectivity of
F
, there exists
v\in V
such that
F(v)=(0,w)
.
Thus
Lv=0
, so
v\in\ker L
, and simultaneously
Kv=w
. Therefore every
w\in W
lies in
K(\ker L)
, i.e.
K|_{\ker L}
is surjective.
∎

With Propositions
2.7
and
7.1
, to ensure the positivity of
\lambda_{1}
it is enough to find
{\mathbf{x}}_{0}\in\mathsf{X}
,
n\geq 1
and
\underline{\omega}_{*}^{n}
for which
\widehat{\Psi}_{{\mathbf{x}}_{0}}:\Omega_{0}^{n}\rightarrow SL(\mathsf{X})
is a submersion at
\underline{\omega}^{n}=\underline{\omega}_{*}^{n}
. As
\widehat{\Psi}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
takes values in
SL(\mathsf{X})
, we first derive expressions for
\widehat{\Psi}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
that will be suitable for computations. As mentioned in Section
3
, the main difficulty in the spherical case compared to the flat-geometric settings of
[
5
,
12
,
16
]
is the non-parallelizability of
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, which we overcome by working with a local smooth orthonormal reference frame in a neighbourhood of a given
{\mathbf{x}}_{0}\in\mathsf{X}
. For convenience, we consider the orthonormal and unit-area reference frame
E_{0}({\mathbf{x}}):=(e_{1}({\mathbf{x}}),e_{2}({\mathbf{x}}))\in(T_{\mathbf{x}}\mathsf{X})^{2}
over
{\mathcal{U}}_{3}=\mathsf{X}\setminus\{\pm\mathbf{e}_{3}\}
and we recall that

(7.2)

e_{1}({\mathbf{x}})=\frac{1}{r}(-zx,-zy,r^{2}),\quad e_{2}({\mathbf{x}})=\frac{1}{r}(y,-x,0),\quad r:=\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}.

Recall further that for any open subset
{\mathcal{U}}\subset{\mathcal{U}}_{3}
, we can identify the principal
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
-bundle of oriented area-preserving frames
\mathcal{F}^{SL}(\mathsf{X})
on
{\mathcal{U}}
by

(7.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{SL}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})\big|_{\mathcal{U}}\cong{\mathcal{U}}\times SL(2,{\mathbb{R}}).

Let
u\in\mathfrak{X}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
be divergence-free and let
\phi_{t}^{u}
be the associated volume-preserving flow on
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
.
If
{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{3}
then
\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})\in{\mathcal{U}}_{3}
for all
0\leq t\leq t_{0}
, for some small
t_{0}({\mathbf{x}})
. Since
D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}:T_{{\mathbf{x}}}\mathsf{X}\rightarrow T_{\phi_{t}^{u}}\mathsf{X}
with
\text{det}(D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u})=1
because
u\in\mathfrak{X}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
and hence we define
\widetilde{\widetilde{\phi_{t}^{u}}}:\mathcal{F}^{SL}(\mathsf{X})\to\mathcal{F}^{SL}(\mathsf{X})
by

(7.4)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\widetilde{\phi_{t}^{u}}}({\mathbf{x}},E):=(\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}}),D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})E)

where, for
E=(e_{1},e_{2})\in(T_{\mathbf{x}}\mathsf{X})^{2}
,

(7.5)

D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})E:=(D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})e_{1},D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})e_{2})\in(T_{\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})}(\mathsf{X}))^{2}.

While
\widetilde{\widetilde{\phi_{t}^{u}}}
maps
\mathcal{F}^{SL}(\mathsf{X})
into itself, the main obstacle in employing it is that for any unit-area frame
E
, the basis
E({\mathbf{x}})
and
D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}E({\mathbf{x}})
live in
T_{\mathbf{x}}\mathsf{X}
and
T_{\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})}\mathsf{X}
, respectively, which are in general distinct tangent spaces. More crucially,
D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}E\in(T_{\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})}\mathsf{X})^{2}
, which depends on
\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})
. In order for the final target space to be independent of
\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})
, we use the reference frame
E_{0}
as follows: since
E({\mathbf{x}})
is a unit-area frame on
{\mathcal{U}}
, for all
{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{3}
there exists
\mathsf{g}_{\mathbf{x}}\in SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
such that

(7.6)

\displaystyle E({\mathbf{x}})=E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})\mathsf{g}_{\mathbf{x}}.

Similarly, for all
\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})\in{\mathcal{U}}
,
D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})E({\mathbf{x}})
is a unit-area basis on
T_{\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})}\mathsf{X}
and thus there exists
\mathsf{h}\in SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
with

(7.7)

\displaystyle D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})E({\mathbf{x}})=E_{0}(\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}}))\mathsf{h}_{\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})}.

As
E_{0}
is an orthonormal frame,
E_{0}^{-1}:=E_{0}^{T}
is a left inverse of
E_{0}
when viewed as matrices and we see that

(7.8)

\displaystyle\mathrm{h}_{\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})}=E_{0}(\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}}))^{T}D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})E=E_{0}(\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}}))^{T}D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})\mathrm{g}_{\mathbf{x}}\in SL(2,{\mathbb{R}}).

Consequently, we define
\widetilde{\phi_{t}^{u}}:{\mathcal{U}}\times SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})\rightarrow{\mathcal{U}}_{3}\times SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
by

(7.9)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi_{t}^{u}}({\mathbf{x}},\mathrm{g})=(\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}}),E_{0}(\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}}))^{T}D_{\mathbf{x}}\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})\mathrm{g}).

To understand the vector field associated to the lifted process
\widetilde{\phi_{t}^{u}}
, we recall that if
w
denotes a smooth vector field in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
such that
w|_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}\in\mathfrak{X}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
and
v\in T_{x}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, the Levi-Civita covariant derivative
\nabla_{v}w
on
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
is then given by

(7.10)

\displaystyle\nabla_{v}w({\mathbf{x}})=\Pi_{\mathbf{x}}(D_{\mathbf{x}}w({\mathbf{x}})v({\mathbf{x}})).

for all
{\mathbf{x}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
. Then,

(7.11)

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}({\mathbf{x}},\mathrm{g})

\displaystyle:=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}\widetilde{\phi_{t}^{u}}({\mathbf{x}},\mathrm{g})=(u({\mathbf{x}}),\Omega_{u}({\mathbf{x}})\mathrm{g}),

with

(7.12)

\displaystyle\Omega_{u}({\mathbf{x}})

\displaystyle=E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})^{T}(\nabla_{E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})}u)({\mathbf{x}})-E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})^{T}(\nabla_{u}E_{0})({\mathbf{x}}).

Here, for
E_{0}=(e_{1},e_{2})
given by (
7.2
) we denote

(7.13)

\displaystyle(\nabla_{E_{0}}u)({\mathbf{x}})

\displaystyle:=(\nabla_{e_{1}}u({\mathbf{x}}),\nabla_{e_{2}}u({\mathbf{x}}))

and we recall that

(7.14)

\displaystyle\nabla_{u}E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})

\displaystyle:=(\nabla_{u}e_{1}({\mathbf{x}}),\nabla_{u}e_{2}({\mathbf{x}})).

We observe that
\Omega_{u}
not only encodes the variation of
u
, but also of the fixed frame
E_{0}
under the action of
u
. On a parallelizable manifold, one can choose
E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})\equiv E_{0}
and thus
\nabla_{u}E_{0}\equiv 0
. In view of Lemma
3.1
,

(7.15)

\displaystyle\Omega_{u}({\mathbf{x}})=E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})^{T}(D_{\mathbf{x}}u)({\mathbf{x}})E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})-E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})^{T}(D_{\mathbf{x}}E_{0})({\mathbf{x}})u({\mathbf{x}}),

with
D_{\mathbf{x}}E_{0}({\mathbf{x}})u({\mathbf{x}})=(D_{\mathbf{x}}e_{1}({\mathbf{x}})u({\mathbf{x}}),D_{\mathbf{x}}e_{2}({\mathbf{x}})u({\mathbf{x}}))
. Let
\widetilde{u}_{\boldsymbol{a}}=(u^{\boldsymbol{a}},\Omega_{u_{\boldsymbol{a}}})
and
\widetilde{u}_{\boldsymbol{b}}=(u^{\boldsymbol{b}},\Omega_{u_{\boldsymbol{b}}})
be the vector fields associated to
\widetilde{\phi_{t}^{u_{\boldsymbol{a}}}}
and
\widetilde{\phi_{t}^{u^{\boldsymbol{b}}}}
respectively. We now show that a set of certain commutators of the velocity fields
\widetilde{u}_{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
\widetilde{u}_{\boldsymbol{b}}
satisfy the LARC. For convenience, set
{\mathbf{x}}_{0}=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},0\right)
and define

(7.16)

{\mathbf{e}}_{1}:=e_{1}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=(0,0,1),\quad{\mathbf{e}}_{2}:=e_{2}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},0\right)

so that
E_{0}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=({\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2})
is a positively oriented unit-area orthonormal basis of
T_{{\mathbf{x}}_{0}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
. From Lemma
3.2
we now have

(7.17)

\displaystyle\Omega_{u}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=E_{0}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})^{T}(D_{\mathbf{x}}u)({\mathbf{x}}_{0})E_{0}({\mathbf{x}}_{0}).

for all
u\in\mathfrak{X}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
.

Proposition 7.2
.

Assume
{\boldsymbol{a}}=(0,a_{2},a_{3})\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
with
a_{2}\neq 0
and
{\boldsymbol{b}}=(0,0,1)
. Let
\widetilde{u}_{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
\widetilde{u}_{\boldsymbol{b}}
be given by (
7.11
). Then the family
\{\widetilde{u}_{\boldsymbol{a}},\widetilde{u}_{\boldsymbol{b}}\}
satisfies the Lie algebra rank condition at
({\mathbf{x}}_{0},Id_{2})
.
More precisely,

(7.18)

\displaystyle X_{1}:=\widetilde{u}_{\boldsymbol{a}},\quad X_{2}:=\widetilde{u}_{\boldsymbol{b}},\quad X_{3}:=[X_{1},X_{2}],\quad X_{4}:=[X_{1},X_{3}],\quad X_{5}:=[X_{2},X_{4}],

are linearly independent at
({\mathbf{x}}_{0},Id_{2})
.

Proof.

Set
w_{1}({\mathbf{x}})=u^{\boldsymbol{a}}({\mathbf{x}})
,
w_{2}({\mathbf{x}})=u^{\boldsymbol{b}}({\mathbf{x}})
and define
w_{3}({\mathbf{x}}):=[w_{1},w_{2}]({\mathbf{x}})
,
w_{4}({\mathbf{x}})=[w_{1},w_{3}]({\mathbf{x}})
and
w_{5}({\mathbf{x}}):=[w_{2},w_{4}]({\mathbf{x}})
. To compute
[v,w]=[v,w]_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}
for
v,w\in\mathfrak{X}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
, which denotes the commutator of vector fields on
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, we note that
[v,w]_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}({\mathbf{x}})=\Pi_{\mathbf{x}}[v,w]_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}({\mathbf{x}})
, for all
v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathfrak{X}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
and all
{\mathbf{x}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
. However, since
v,w\in\mathfrak{X}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
are already tangent to the sphere, it follows that
[v,w]_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\in\mathfrak{X}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
is also tangent to
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
and thus
[v,w]_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}=[v,w]_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}
. Next, since Lemma
A.4
yields

(7.19)

\displaystyle[\widetilde{v},\widetilde{w}]=\widetilde{[v,w]}

for all
v,w\in\mathfrak{X}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
we have that
X_{i}=\widetilde{w_{i}}
, for
i=1,...,5
. Hence, there holds

(7.20)

\displaystyle X_{i}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},Id)=(w_{i}({\mathbf{x}}_{0}),\Omega_{i}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})),\quad\Omega_{i}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=E_{0}^{T}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})(D_{\mathbf{x}}w_{i})({\mathbf{x}}_{0})E_{0}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})

for all
i=1,...,5
. We compute
3
3

3

See Appendix
A
for the computations.

(7.21)

\displaystyle w_{1}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=-\frac{a_{2}^{2}}{2}{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-a_{2}a_{3}\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}{\mathbf{e}}_{2},\quad w_{2}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=0,\quad w_{3}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=\frac{a_{2}^{2}}{2}{\mathbf{e}}_{2},\quad w_{4}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=-\frac{3\sqrt{2}}{4}a_{2}^{3}a_{3}{\mathbf{e}}_{2},\quad w_{5}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=0,

and

(7.22)

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{a_{2}a_{3}}{\sqrt{2}}&0\\
\frac{a_{2}^{2}}{2}-a_{3}^{2}&\frac{a_{2}a_{3}}{\sqrt{2}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle,\quad\Omega_{2}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\Omega_{3}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
\sqrt{2}a_{2}a_{3}&0\end{pmatrix},

(7.23)

\displaystyle\Omega_{4}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})

\displaystyle=a_{2}^{2}\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{a_{2}a_{3}}{2\sqrt{2}}&-2a_{2}^{2}\\
2a_{2}^{2}-3a_{3}^{2}&\frac{a_{2}a_{3}}{2\sqrt{2}}\end{pmatrix},\quad\Omega_{5}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=\begin{pmatrix}2a_{2}^{4}&0\\
-\frac{a_{2}^{3}a_{3}}{\sqrt{2}}&-2a_{2}^{4}\end{pmatrix}.

Suppose now that
\sum_{i=1}^{5}c_{i}{X}_{i}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},Id)=0
, for some
c_{1},...,c_{5}
. Recall that
a_{2}\neq 0
and
\{{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\}
constitute an orthonormal basis of
T_{{\mathbf{x}}_{0}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
. Since
w_{1}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})
is the only vector with non-trivial
{\mathbf{e}}_{1}
component, we deduce that
c_{1}=0
. Next, we observe that
\Omega_{4}
is the only element with non-trivial
{\mathbf{e}}_{1}\otimes{\mathbf{e}}_{2}
component, and thus we must have
c_{4}=0
. In turn, since
w_{2}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=w_{5}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=0
, we deduce that
c_{3}=0
. Finally,
c_{5}=0
because
\Omega_{5}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})
has non-zero
{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\otimes{\mathbf{e}}_{2}
and
{\mathbf{e}}_{1}\otimes{\mathbf{e}}_{1}
components, and this ultimately forces
c_{2}=0
as well. Therefore, all
c_{i}=0
and the set
\{{X}_{i}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},Id)\}_{i=1,...,5}
is linearly independent.
∎

With Proposition
7.2
at hand, we now extend
\widetilde{\phi_{t}^{u}}
from
{\mathcal{U}}\times SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
to an ambient Euclidean space in order to use Proposition
4.6
from the LARC framework. Note that
{\mathcal{U}}\times SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})\subset{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}\times SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
once we identify
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
