as a subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
via the isomorphism
\iota:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\rightarrow M^{2\times 2}({\mathbb{R}})
given by
\iota(h)=\mathrm{h}:=\begin{pmatrix}h_{1}&h_{2}\\
h_{3}&h_{4}\end{pmatrix}
for
h=(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
. For
{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
with
{\mathcal{U}}\subset{\mathcal{U}}_{0}
and
{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\cap\{\lambda\mathbf{e}_{3}:\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\}=\emptyset
we define the extension
v_{u}:{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
of
\widetilde{u}
by

(7.24)

\displaystyle v_{u}({\mathbf{y}},h)=(u({\mathbf{y}}),\iota^{-1}\circ\Omega_{u}({\mathbf{y}})\iota(h))

for all
({\mathbf{y}},{h})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, where
\Pi_{\mathbf{y}}w=w-(w\cdot{\mathbf{y}})\frac{{\mathbf{y}}}{\|{\mathbf{y}}\|^{2}}
for
w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
and the covariant derivative
\nabla_{w}
is extended accordingly. Moreover, we denote
\Phi_{t}^{u}:{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\rightarrow{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
the flow map defined by
v_{u}
, namely

(7.25)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{y}},{h})=v_{u}(\Phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{y}},{h})),&\\
\Phi_{0}^{u}({\mathbf{y}},{h})=({\mathbf{y}},{h})&\end{cases}

In particular, since
v_{u}({\mathbf{x}},{g})=\widetilde{u}({\mathbf{x}},\mathrm{g})
, for all
{\mathbf{x}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
and
g\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
with
\iota(g)=\mathrm{g}\in SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
, there holds
\Phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}},{g})=\widetilde{\phi_{t}^{u}}({\mathbf{x}},\mathrm{g})
, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
{\mathbf{x}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
and
\iota(g)=\mathrm{g}\in SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
for which
\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}})\in{\mathcal{U}}_{3}
.

Proposition 7.3
.

The top Lyapunov exponent is positive.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}=\{v_{u^{\boldsymbol{a}}},v_{u^{\boldsymbol{b}}}\}
and
\Phi_{t}^{v_{u^{\boldsymbol{a}}}}
,
\Phi_{t}^{v_{u^{\boldsymbol{b}}}}
their associated flow maps. Let
g_{0}=(1,0,0,1)
such that
\iota(g_{0})=Id\in SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
. Since
v_{u}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},g_{0})=\widetilde{u}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},Id)
and the set
\{X_{i}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},Id)\}_{i=1}^{5}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
is linearly independent due to Proposition
7.2
, we obtain from Proposition
4.6
that there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and words
\varpi_{i}
associated to each
X_{i}
such that
D_{\varpi}\Phi_{\varpi_{*}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},Id)
has rank at least
5
, for
\varpi_{*}=(\varpi_{1}(a_{1}^{*}),...,\varpi_{5}(a_{5}^{*}))\in[-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0}]^{d_{0}}
, for some
d_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and some
a_{*}=(a_{1}^{*},...,a_{5}^{*})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{5}
with
0<\|a_{*}\|\leq\varepsilon_{0}
. On the other hand, for fixed
({\mathbf{x}},\mathrm{g})\in{\mathcal{U}}\times SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
we have that
\Phi({\mathbf{x}},\iota^{-1}(\mathrm{g})):[-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0}]^{d_{0}}\rightarrow({\mathcal{U}}_{3}\subset{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})\times\iota^{-1}(SL(2,{\mathbb{R}}))
, after choosing
\varepsilon_{0}
smaller, if necessary. In particular,

(7.26)

\displaystyle D_{\varpi}\Phi_{\varpi_{*}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},g_{0}):{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{0}}\rightarrow T_{\Phi_{\varpi_{*}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},\iota^{-1}(Id))}({\mathcal{U}}_{3}\times\iota^{-1}(SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})))

with
\text{dim }T_{\Phi_{\varpi_{*}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},\iota^{-1}(Id))}({\mathcal{U}}_{3}\times\iota^{-1}(SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})))=2+3=5
as

(7.27)

\displaystyle\iota^{-1}(SL(2,{\mathbb{R}}))=\{h=(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}:h_{1}h_{4}-h_{2}h_{3}=1\}.

Since
D_{\varpi}\Phi_{\varpi_{*}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},g_{0})
has rank at least 5, we conclude that
D_{\varpi}\Phi_{\varpi_{*}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},g_{0})
is surjective and thus
\Phi_{\varpi}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},g_{0})
is a submersion at
\varpi=\varpi_{*}
. In view of the definition of
\Phi_{\varpi}
, we have from Propositions
2.7
and
7.1
that the top Lyapunov exponent is positive.
∎

8.
The two point process

We start the discussion relative to the two-point process by introducing the two point vector field

(8.1)

u^{(2)}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=(u^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\mathbf{x}}),u^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\mathbf{y}})),\quad{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}},

which generates the two-point flow map

(8.2)

f^{(2),{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{t}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=(f^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{t}({\mathbf{x}}),f^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{t}({\mathbf{y}})),\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

The goal of this section is to prove that the two-point process
f_{\omega}^{(2)}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=(f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}({\mathbf{y}}))
is
\mathrm{W}
-geometrically ergodic. It is readily seen that on the product space
\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}
, the diagonal set

(8.3)

\displaystyle\Delta=\{({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\in\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}:{\mathbf{x}}={\mathbf{y}}\}

is invariant under
f_{\omega}^{(2)}
and thus there does not exist a unique ergodic stationary measure for the two-point process on
\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}
. The following result describes the set of symmetries that are almost-surely invariant for the two-point process.

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
be two non-parallel and non-orthogonal axes. The family
{\mathcal{R}}\subset SO(3)
of rotations of the sphere that are invariant under the two-point process
f_{\omega}^{(2)}
is
{\mathcal{R}}=\{I,\mathcal{R}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}\}
, where
I
is the identity and
\mathcal{R}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}
is the rotation of angle
\pi
around the axis
{\boldsymbol{c}}=\frac{{\boldsymbol{a}}\times{\boldsymbol{b}}}{|{\boldsymbol{a}}\times{\boldsymbol{b}}|}
.
Moreover, if
{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{b}}=0
, then
{\mathcal{R}}=\{I,\mathcal{R}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}},\mathcal{R}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}},\mathcal{R}_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}\}
.

Proof.

Let
S\in SO(3)
such that
f_{\omega}(S{\mathbf{x}})=Sf_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}})
almost surely in
\omega
for all
{\mathbf{x}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
. In particular, due to the randomness of
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
, we require further that
u^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(S{\mathbf{x}})=Su^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}({\mathbf{x}})
and
u^{{\boldsymbol{b}}}(S{\mathbf{x}})=Su^{{\boldsymbol{b}}}({\mathbf{x}})
for all
{\mathbf{x}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
.
This forces
S
to preserve the zero sets of both
u^{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
u^{\boldsymbol{b}}
, hence
S
must satisfy

(8.4)

S{\boldsymbol{a}}={\varepsilon}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}{\boldsymbol{a}},\quad S{\boldsymbol{b}}={\varepsilon}_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}{\boldsymbol{b}},\qquad{\varepsilon}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}},{\varepsilon}_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}\in\{1,-1\}.

The four cases are the following:

–

Case
({\varepsilon}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}},{\varepsilon}_{{\boldsymbol{b}}})=(1,1)
: the two points are fixed and linearly independent, so
S=I
.

–

Case
({\varepsilon}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}},{\varepsilon}_{{\boldsymbol{b}}})=(1,-1)
: the point
{\boldsymbol{a}}
is fixed while the point
{\boldsymbol{b}}
is sent to
-{\boldsymbol{b}}
by
S
. This is possible only if
{\boldsymbol{a}}\perp{\boldsymbol{b}}
via a
\pi
rotation around the
{\boldsymbol{a}}
axis. Hence, only if
{\boldsymbol{a}}\perp{\boldsymbol{b}}
we have
S={\mathcal{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}
.

–

Case
({\varepsilon}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}},{\varepsilon}_{{\boldsymbol{b}}})=(-1,1)
: analogously to the previous case, only if
{\boldsymbol{a}}\perp{\boldsymbol{b}}
then
S={\mathcal{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}
.

–

Case
({\varepsilon}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}},{\varepsilon}_{{\boldsymbol{b}}})=(-1,-1)
: in this case we exchange both
{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
{\boldsymbol{b}}
to
-{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
-{\boldsymbol{b}}
respectively, in the plane spanned by the two axes. This is a
\pi
rotation around the
{\boldsymbol{c}}=\frac{{\boldsymbol{a}}\times{\boldsymbol{b}}}{|{\boldsymbol{a}}\times{\boldsymbol{b}}|}
axis, hence
S={\mathcal{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}
.

∎

For the most demanding case
{\boldsymbol{a}}=(0,1,0)
and
{\boldsymbol{b}}=(0,0,1)
we define
\Sigma:=\{{\mathsf{x}},{\mathsf{y}},{\mathsf{z}}\}
,
\Sigma_{\mathsf{D}}:=\Sigma\cup\{\mathsf{D}\}
and the set

(8.5)

{\mathcal{S}}:=\bigcup_{\sigma\in\{{\mathsf{x}},\mathsf{y},{\mathsf{z}},\mathsf{D}\}}\mathrm{R}_{\sigma},\qquad\mathrm{R}_{\sigma}=\{(p,q)\in\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}:q=\mathcal{R}_{\sigma}p\},

with
{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathsf{D}}
the identity map.
{\mathcal{S}}
denotes the subset of
\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}
that is almost-surely invariant under the two-point process while
\mathrm{R}_{\sigma}
and
\mathrm{R}_{\sigma^{\prime}}
are mutually disjoint, for all
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{D}}
with
\sigma\neq\sigma^{\prime}
.
We now show that the two-point process is
\mathrm{W}
-geometrically ergodic on the phase space
\mathsf{X}^{(2)}=\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}
using the abstract Harris Theorem
2.2
.
For this, we first prove that the two-point chain admits an open small set in Proposition
8.3
. Next, we show the topological irreducibility in Proposition
8.4
. Lastly, since the phase space
\mathsf{X}^{(2)}
is not compact, we construct in Proposition
8.5
a function
\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}
that satisfies a Lyapunov-Foster drift condition for the two-point process.

8.1.
Open small set

We rely on the LARC framework.

Proposition 8.2
.

For non-parallel axes
{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, there exists a point
({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\mathbf{y}}_{0})\in\mathsf{X}^{(2)}
such that
{\text{Lie}}_{({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\mathbf{y}}_{0})}(\{u^{(2)}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}},u^{(2)}_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}\})
has dimension 4. More precisely,

(8.6)

\displaystyle X^{(2)}_{1}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle:=u_{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(2)}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}),\quad X^{(2)}_{2}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}):=u_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{(2)}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}),

(8.7)

\displaystyle X^{(2)}_{3}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle:=[X^{(2)}_{1},X^{(2)}_{2}]({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}),\quad X^{(2)}_{4}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}):=[X^{(2)}_{1},X^{(2)}_{3}]({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}),

are linearly independent for some
({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\mathbf{y}}_{0})\in\mathsf{X}^{(2)}
.

Proof.

Let
w_{1}:=u^{\boldsymbol{a}}
,
w_{2}:=u^{\boldsymbol{b}}
,
w_{3}:=[w_{1},w_{2}]
and
w_{4}=[w_{1},w_{3}]
. First, for
{\boldsymbol{a}}=(0,1,0)
and
{\boldsymbol{b}}=(0,0,1)
we have

(8.8)

w_{3}({\mathbf{x}})=(x(y^{2}-z^{2}),y(z^{2}-x^{2}),z(x^{2}-y^{2})),

and hence

(8.9)

D_{\mathbf{x}}w_{3}({\mathbf{x}})=\begin{pmatrix}y^{2}-z^{2}&2xy&-2xz\\
-2xy&z^{2}-x^{2}&2yz\\
2xz&-2yz&x^{2}-y^{2}\end{pmatrix}.

With this, we compute

(8.10)

w_{4}({\mathbf{x}})=D_{\mathbf{x}}w_{3}({\mathbf{x}})u^{\boldsymbol{a}}({\mathbf{x}})-D_{\mathbf{x}}u^{\boldsymbol{a}}({\mathbf{x}})w_{3}({\mathbf{x}})=-(2yz(2z^{2}-1),4xy^{2}z,2xy(2x^{2}-1)).

At
{\mathbf{x}}_{0}=(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2},0,\frac{1}{2})
and
{\mathbf{y}}_{0}=(\frac{3}{4},\frac{\sqrt{3}}{4},\frac{1}{2})
we have
({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\mathbf{y}}_{0})\in\mathsf{X}^{(2)}
from which

(8.11)

\displaystyle X^{(2)}_{1}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\mathbf{y}}_{0})

\displaystyle=\left(0,0,0,\frac{\sqrt{3}}{8},0,-\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{16}\right),

(8.12)

\displaystyle X^{(2)}_{2}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\mathbf{y}}_{0})

\displaystyle=\left(0,\frac{\sqrt{3}}{4},0,-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{8},\frac{3}{8},0\right),

(8.13)

\displaystyle X^{(2)}_{3}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\mathbf{y}}_{0})

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{8},0,\frac{3}{8},-\frac{3}{64},-\frac{5\sqrt{3}}{64},\frac{3}{16}\right),

(8.14)

\displaystyle X^{(2)}_{4}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\mathbf{y}}_{0})

\displaystyle=\left(0,0,0,\frac{\sqrt{3}}{8},-\frac{9}{32},-\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{64}\right).

These vectors are linearly independent: if
\sum_{i=1}^{4}c_{i}X^{(2)}_{i}=0
, from the first three components we first observe that First,
c_{2}=c_{3}=0
. Then, as
X^{(2)}_{4}
has non-zero fifth component we deduce that
c_{4}=0
and finally
c_{1}=0
.

For the case of non-orthogonal axes, recall that we may assume without loss that
{\boldsymbol{a}}=(0,a_{2},a_{3})
with
a_{2},a_{3}\neq 0
and
a_{2}^{2}+a_{3}^{2}=1
while
{\boldsymbol{b}}=e_{3}
. For
{\mathbf{x}}_{0}=(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2},-\frac{a_{3}}{2},\frac{a_{2}}{2})
we now set
{\mathbf{y}}_{0}=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},0\right)
. There holds
({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\mathbf{y}}_{0})\in\mathsf{X}^{(2)}
and we have

(8.16)

\displaystyle X^{(2)}_{1}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\mathbf{y}}_{0})

\displaystyle=\left(0,0,0,-\frac{a_{2}a_{3}}{2},\frac{a_{2}a_{3}}{2},-\frac{a_{2}^{2}}{2}\right),

(8.17)

\displaystyle X^{(2)}_{2}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\mathbf{y}}_{0})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{a_{2}a_{3}}{4},\frac{a_{2}\sqrt{3}}{4},0,0,0,0\right),

(8.18)

\displaystyle X^{(2)}_{3}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\mathbf{y}}_{0})

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{a_{2}^{2}\sqrt{3}}{8},-\frac{3a_{2}^{2}a_{3}}{8},\frac{3a_{2}^{3}}{8},a_{2}^{2}\frac{\sqrt{2}}{4},-a_{2}^{2}\frac{\sqrt{2}}{4},0\right),

(8.19)

\displaystyle X^{(2)}_{4}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\mathbf{y}}_{0})

\displaystyle=\left(0,0,0,-\frac{3a_{2}^{3}a_{3}}{4},\frac{3a_{2}^{3}a_{3}}{4},0\right).

These vectors are linearly independent as well: If
\sum_{i=1}^{4}c_{i}X^{(2)}_{i}=0
, since
X^{(2)}_{3}
is the only vector with non-trivial third component we have that
c_{3}=0
. Next, we deduce that
c_{2}=0
since
a_{2}\neq 0
and both
X^{(2)}_{1}
and
X^{(2)}_{4}
have trivial projection onto the first three components. Similarly,
c_{1}=0
since the sixth component of
X^{(2)}_{1}
is non-zero and lastly
c_{4}=0
because
X^{(2)}_{4}\neq 0
as we assumed that
a_{3}\neq 0
.
∎

The existence of an open small set for the two-point process is obtained from the LARC.
For
u\in\mathfrak{X}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
, let
\phi_{t}^{u}
denote the flow map of
u
on
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
and let
\overline{u}
denote the smooth radial extension of
u
in an open spherical shell
U_{0}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
containing
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
. For the sake of simplicity, we still denote it
u
. We next define the extension
v^{(2)}_{u}:U_{0}\times U_{0}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
by

(8.21)

\displaystyle v^{(2)}_{u}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=(u({\mathbf{x}}),u({\mathbf{y}})),

for all
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in U_{0}
. Let
\Phi^{(2),u}_{t}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}):U_{0}\times U_{0}\rightarrow U_{0}\times U_{0}
be the flow map defined by
v^{(2)}_{u}
, that is

(8.22)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Phi^{(2),u}_{t}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=v_{u}^{(2)}(\Phi^{(2),u}_{t}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})),&\\
\Phi^{(2),u}_{0}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})&\end{cases}

and observe that
\Phi^{(2),u}_{t}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=(\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{x}}),\phi_{t}^{u}({\mathbf{y}}))
for all
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
and all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

Proposition 8.3
.

The two-point process admits an open small set.

Proof.

We shall use Lemma
2.4
. For this, let
{\mathcal{V}}=\{v_{u^{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{(2)},v_{u^{\boldsymbol{b}}}^{(2)}\}
and
\Phi^{(2),u^{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{t}
,
\Phi^{(2),u^{\boldsymbol{b}}}_{t}
, their associated flow maps. From Proposition
8.2
we obtain that
\{X^{(2)}_{i}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\}_{i=1}^{4}
is a set of linearly independent vector fields at some
({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\mathbf{y}}_{0})
. Thus, from Proposition
4.6
we derive the existence of a word
\{\varpi\}_{i=1}^{4}
and
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that
D_{\varpi}\Phi^{(2)}_{\varpi_{*}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\mathbf{y}}_{0})
has rank at least 4, for
\varpi_{*}=(\varpi_{1}(a_{1}^{*}),...,\varpi_{4}(a_{4}^{*}))\in[-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0}]^{d_{0}}
, for some
d_{0}>1
and all
a_{*}=(a_{1}^{*},...,a_{4}^{*})
with
0<\|a_{*}\|<\varepsilon_{0}
. Additionally, since
\Phi_{t}^{(2),u}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\mathbf{y}}_{0})\in\mathsf{X}^{(2)}
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
u\in\{u^{\boldsymbol{a}},u^{\boldsymbol{b}}\}
, we have that

(8.23)

\displaystyle D_{\varpi}\Phi^{(2)}_{\varpi_{*}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\mathbf{y}}_{0}):{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{0}}\rightarrow T_{\Phi^{(2)}_{\varpi_{*}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\mathbf{y}}_{0})}\mathsf{X}^{(2)}

has rank at least 4. Since
\text{dim }T_{\Phi^{(2)}_{\varpi_{*}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\mathbf{y}}_{0})}\mathsf{X}^{(2)}=4
, we conclude that
\Phi^{(2)}_{\varpi}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},{\mathbf{y}}_{0})
is a submersion at
\varpi=\varpi_{*}
and Lemma
2.4
yields the existence of an open small set.
∎

8.2.
Topological irreducibility

Since the set
{\mathcal{S}}
is composed of mutually disjoint subsets that are each invariant under the two-point process, we prove the topological irreducibility of the two-point chain away from
{\mathcal{S}}
. We argue for the orthogonal case
{\boldsymbol{a}}=(0,1,0)
and
{\boldsymbol{b}}=(0,0,1)
.

Proposition 8.4
.

The two-point process is topologically irreducible in
\mathsf{X}^{(2)}
.

Proof.

Let
(q_{1},q_{2})\in\mathsf{X}^{(2)}
and
(p_{1},p_{2})\in\mathsf{X}^{(2)}
. For all
\varepsilon>0
we shall find a sequence of times
\omega_{n}
such that
(f_{\omega_{n}}(p_{1}),f_{\omega_{n}}(p_{2}))\in(B_{\varepsilon}(q_{1}),B_{\varepsilon}(q_{2}))
. We shall achieve this approximate controllability in six steps, taking advantage of the stagnation sets of each
u^{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
u^{\boldsymbol{b}}
.

Step 1.
Since the one-point process is exactly controllable, we can find
\omega^{1}=(\omega^{1}_{1},\omega^{1}_{2})
such that
\mathsf{p}_{1}:=f_{\omega^{1}}(p_{1})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(0,1,1)
. Given
\mathsf{p}_{2}:=f_{\omega^{1}}(p_{2})
, we denote
\mathsf{p}_{2}=(x,y,z)
.

Step 2.
Let
\omega_{S}=\frac{\pi\sqrt{2}}{2}
and let
\mathsf{p}_{j}^{S}=f_{\omega_{S}}^{2}(\mathsf{p}_{j})
for
j=1,2
. We have that

(8.24)

\displaystyle\mathsf{p}_{1}^{S}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathsf{p}_{2}^{S}=\begin{pmatrix}{x}\cos(\omega_{S}{y})+{z}\sin(\omega_{S}{y})\\
{y}\\
-{x}\sin(\omega_{S}{y})+{z}\cos(\omega_{S}{y})\end{pmatrix}

Similarly, let
\omega_{N}=\frac{3\pi\sqrt{2}}{2}
and
\mathsf{p}_{j}^{N}=f_{\omega_{N}}^{2}(\mathsf{p}_{j})
for
j=1,2
. There now holds

(8.25)

\displaystyle\mathsf{p}_{1}^{N}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}-1\\
1\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathsf{p}_{2}^{N}=\begin{pmatrix}{x}\cos(\omega_{N}{y})+{z}\sin(\omega_{N}{y})\\
{y}\\
-{x}\sin(\omega_{N}{y})+{z}\cos(\omega_{N}{y})\end{pmatrix}.

Step 3.
We now claim that since
(p_{1},p_{2})\in\mathsf{X}^{(2)}
, we have either
(\mathsf{p}_{2}^{S})_{z}\neq 0
or
(\mathsf{p}_{2}^{N})_{z}\neq 0
. Indeed, assume towards a contradiction that both are zero. Then, for
\alpha=\omega_{S}{y}
we have
\omega_{N}{y}=3\alpha
and

(8.26)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}-\sin(\alpha)&\cos(\alpha)\\
-\sin(3\alpha)&\cos(3\alpha)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{x}\\
{z}\end{pmatrix}=0.

The determinant of the above linear system is
\sin(3\alpha)\cos(\alpha)-\sin(\alpha)\cos(3\alpha)=\sin(2\alpha)
.

\diamond

Non-zero determinant.
We thus obtain the trivial solution
{x}={z}=0
, which forces
{y}=\pm 1
, namely
\mathsf{p}_{2}\not\in\mathsf{X}
.

\diamond

Zero determinant.
We thus have
2\alpha=\pi k
for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, namely

(8.27)

\displaystyle{y}=\frac{k}{\sqrt{2}}

for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Since
\mathsf{p}_{2}\in\mathsf{X}
, we have
{y}\in(-1,1)
so that
|k|\leq 1
.

–

If
k=0
, this gives
{y}=0
, so that
\mathsf{p}_{2}=\mathsf{p}_{2}^{S}=\mathsf{p}_{2}^{N}
. If
{z}=0
, we then see that
|{x}|=1
and thus
\mathsf{p}_{2}\not\in\mathsf{X}
.

–

If
k=\pm 1
, we then find that
{y}=\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
and thus
\sin(\omega_{S}{y})=-\sin(\omega_{N}{y})=\pm 1
. In particular,

(8.28)

\displaystyle(\mathsf{p}_{2}^{S})_{z}=\mp{x},\quad(\mathsf{p}_{2}^{N})_{z}=\pm{x}

from which we deduce that
{x}=0
. Hence, we obtain that
{z}=\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
and thus

(8.29)

\displaystyle\mathsf{p}_{2}^{S},\mathsf{p}_{2}^{N}\in\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(1,1,0),\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(-1,1,0),\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(1,-1,0),\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(-1,-1,0)\right\}.

We shall argue for
\mathsf{p}_{2}^{S}
. Now, the first possibility gives
\mathsf{p}_{2}^{S}=\mathsf{p}_{1}^{S}
, a contradiction with
(\mathsf{p}_{1}^{S},\mathsf{p}_{2}^{S})\not\in\Delta
. The second option gives a contradiction with
(\mathsf{p}_{1}^{S},\mathsf{p}_{2}^{S})\not\in\mathrm{R}_{\mathsf{z}}
, the third possibility contradicts
(\mathsf{p}_{1}^{S},\mathsf{p}_{2}^{S})\not\in\mathrm{R}_{\mathsf{y}}
and the last possibility contradicts
(\mathsf{p}_{1}^{S},\mathsf{p}_{2}^{S})\not\in\mathrm{R}_{\mathsf{x}}
.

Hence, we have that either
(\mathsf{p}_{2}^{S})_{z}\neq 0
or
(\mathsf{p}_{2}^{N})_{z}\neq 0
. We shall assume from now on that
(\mathsf{p}_{2}^{S})_{z}\neq 0
and we set
\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{1}=\mathsf{p}_{1}^{S}
and
\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{2}=\mathsf{p}_{2}^{S}
.

Step 4.
Thanks to the exact controllability of the one-point process, there exists
\omega^{0}=(\omega^{0}_{1},\omega^{0}_{2})
such that
f_{\omega^{0}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{1})=q_{1}
. Let
\mathsf{q}_{2}=f_{\omega^{0}}^{-1}(q_{2})
denote the pre-image of
q_{2}
by the map
f_{\omega^{0}}
. By definition, it is such that
f_{\omega^{0}}(\mathsf{q}_{2})=q_{2}
and by continuity of
f_{\omega^{0}}
, for all
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that
f_{\omega^{0}}(B_{\delta}(\mathsf{q}_{2}))\subset B_{\varepsilon}(q_{2})
. We remark here that
f^{-1}_{\omega_{0}}=(f_{\omega_{1}^{0}}^{3})^{-1}\circ(f_{\omega_{2}^{0}}^{2})^{-1}
where for fixed
\omega_{1}^{0}
and
\omega_{2}^{0}
both
f_{\omega_{1}^{0}}^{3}
and
f_{\omega_{2}^{0}}^{2}
are invertible.

Step 5.
We shall now approximate
\mathsf{q}_{2}
from
\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{2}
.
Since
(\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{2})_{z}\neq 0
, using the rotation dynamics of
f^{3}
around the
z
-axis, there exists
\omega_{1}^{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that for
\omega^{2}=(\omega_{1}^{2},0)
we have
2\pi\sqrt{2}(f_{\omega^{2}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{2}))_{y}\not\in{\mathbb{Q}}
. Note that since
(\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{1})_{z}=0
, we preserve
f_{\omega^{2}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{1})=\mathsf{p}_{1}^{2}
.

\diamond

Case 1.
Assume first that
(\mathsf{q}_{2})_{z}\neq 0
and assume further that
(\mathsf{q}_{2})_{z}>0
. Since
2\pi\sqrt{2}(f_{\omega^{2}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{2}))_{y}\not\in{\mathbb{Q}}
, for
\omega^{3}_{2}=2\pi\sqrt{2}
and
\omega^{3}=(0,\omega_{2}^{3})
we have
f_{\omega^{3}}^{n}(\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{1})=\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{1}
, for all
n\geq 1
, while

(8.30)

\displaystyle 0<(\mathsf{q}_{2})_{z}-\delta/100<(f_{\omega^{3}}^{m}\circ f_{\omega^{2}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{2}))_{z}<(\mathsf{q}_{2})_{z}

for some
m\geq 1
and
\delta/100<(\mathsf{q}_{2})_{z}/2
small enough. Let
\overline{p}_{2}=f_{\omega^{3}}^{m}\circ f_{\omega^{2}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{2})
, then, for
\delta>0
smaller if necessary, there exists
\omega^{4}=(\omega_{1}^{4},0)
such that

(8.31)

\displaystyle(f_{\omega^{4}}(\overline{\mathsf{p}}_{2}))_{y}\in B_{\delta/25}((\mathsf{q}_{2})_{y}),\quad(f_{\omega^{4}}(\overline{\mathsf{p}}_{2}))_{x}\in B_{\delta/25}((\mathsf{q}_{2})_{x})

Note that since
(\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{1})_{z}=0
, we preserve
f_{\omega^{4}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{1})=\mathsf{p}_{1}^{2}
.

\diamond

Case 2.
Assume next that
(\mathsf{q}_{2})_{z}=0
. Then, because
2\pi\sqrt{2}(f_{\omega^{2}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{2}))_{y}\not\in{\mathbb{Q}}
, for
\omega^{3}_{2}=2\pi\sqrt{2}
and
\omega^{3}=(0,\omega_{2}^{3})
we have
f_{\omega^{3}}^{n}(\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{1})=\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{1}
, for all
n\geq 1
and

(8.32)

\displaystyle 0<\delta/200<|(f_{\omega^{3}}^{m}\circ f_{\omega^{2}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{2}))_{z}|<\delta/100

for some
m\geq 1
. Hence, for
\overline{p}_{2}=f_{\omega^{3}}^{m}\circ f_{\omega^{2}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{2})
, there exists
\omega^{4}=(\omega_{1}^{4},0)
such that

(8.33)

\displaystyle(f_{\omega^{4}}(\overline{\mathsf{p}}_{2}))_{y}\in B_{\delta/25}((\mathsf{q}_{2})_{y}),\quad(f_{\omega^{4}}(\overline{\mathsf{p}}_{2}))_{x}\in B_{\delta/25}((\mathsf{q}_{2})_{x})

and we still preserve
f_{\omega^{4}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{1})=\mathsf{p}_{1}^{2}
as
(\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{1})_{z}=0
.

Step 6.
As a result,
f_{\omega^{4}}(\overline{\mathsf{p}}_{2})\in B_{\delta}(\mathsf{q}_{2})
. We now apply
f_{\omega^{0}}
to both
\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{1}
and
f_{\omega^{4}}(\overline{\mathsf{p}}_{2})
, thus obtaining
f_{\omega_{0}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{p}}_{1})=q_{1}
and
f_{\omega^{0}}\circ f_{\omega^{4}}(\overline{\mathsf{p}}_{2})\in B_{\varepsilon}(q_{2})
. With this, the proof of the proposition is complete.
∎

8.3.
Drift function for the two-point process

We have seen in Proposition
8.1
above that the symmetry set
{\mathcal{S}}
is almost-surely invariant under the two-point process and the measure supported on
{\mathcal{S}}
is invariant. Thus,
P^{(2)}
cannot be uniformly geometrically ergodic in
\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}
with respect to
\pi^{(2)}
. Instead, we work in the non-compact phase space
\mathsf{X}^{(2)}
, which requires the introduction of a Lyapunov function
\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}
that satisfies a Lyapunov-Foster drift condition in order to use the abstract Harris Theorem
2.2
.

To construct this Lyapunov function, we note that both the one-point and projective processes are uniformly geometrically ergodic. Moreover, we have further shown in Proposition
7.3
that the top Lyapunov exponent is positive. Therefore, appealing to Proposition
2.6
, there exists
\xi,\gamma\in(0,1)
,
s_{*}>0
and
\psi:X\times X\setminus\Delta\rightarrow[1,\infty)
such that

(8.34)

\displaystyle W(p,q):=d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}(p,q)^{-\xi}\psi(p,q)

satisfies

(8.35)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[W(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))\right]\leq\gamma W(p,q),

for all
(p,q)\in\Delta(s_{*})=\{(p,q)\in\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}:0<d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}(p,q)<s_{*}\}
. We next define

(8.36)

\displaystyle W_{\sigma}(p,q):=W(p,\mathcal{R}_{\sigma}q)

for
\sigma\in\Sigma
, so that we likewise have

(8.37)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[W_{\sigma}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))\right]\leq\gamma W_{\sigma}(p,q).

provided that
0<d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}(p,\mathcal{R}_{\sigma}q)<s_{*}
. For
p,q\in\mathsf{X}
, let

(8.38)

\displaystyle\tilde{r}_{\sigma}(p,q):=d_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}(p,\mathcal{R}_{\sigma}q)

for
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{D}}
and further define the local Lyapunov function
\mathrm{W}
for the two-point process by

(8.39)

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}(p,q)

\displaystyle:=\chi(\tilde{r}_{\mathsf{D}}(p,q))(1-\chi(\tilde{r}_{\mathsf{x}}(p,q)))(1-\chi(\tilde{r}_{\mathsf{y}}(p,q)))(1-\chi(\tilde{r}_{\mathsf{z}}(p,q)))W(p,q)

(8.40)

\displaystyle\quad+\chi(\tilde{r}_{\mathsf{x}}(p,q))(1-\chi(\tilde{r}_{\mathsf{y}}(p,q)))(1-\chi(\tilde{r}_{\mathsf{z}}(p,q)))W_{\mathsf{x}}(p,q)

(8.41)

\displaystyle\quad+\chi(\tilde{r}_{\mathsf{y}}(p,q))(1-\chi(\tilde{r}_{\mathsf{z}}(p,q)))W_{\mathsf{y}}(p,q)

(8.42)

\displaystyle\quad+\chi(\tilde{r}_{\mathsf{z}}(p,q))W_{\mathsf{z}}(p,q),

where
\chi
is the bump function introduced for the one-point drift function. To construct a function
\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}
that satisfies the Lyapunov-Foster drift condition for the two-point chain, we note that any compact subset
\widetilde{K}
of
\mathsf{X}^{(2)}
avoids not only the invariant set
{\mathcal{S}}
, but also avoids the fixed points in both coordinates,
F\times\mathsf{X}\cup\mathsf{X}\times F
. In particular, away from any such compact set
\widetilde{K}
we shall prove a contraction of
\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}
in expectation.
A priori, no such behaviour for
\mathrm{W}
is guaranteed for points close to
F\times\mathsf{X}\cup\mathsf{X}\times F
, in contrast with the drift achieved by
\mathrm{W}
near
{\mathcal{S}}
(combine its definition and Proposition
2.6
). To overcome this obstruction, we use the drift function
\mathrm{V}
for the one-point process and we prove the following.

Proposition 8.5
.

There exists
\nu>0
small enough such that

(8.43)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}(p,q):=\max\{\mathrm{W}(p,q),\nu\mathrm{V}(p),\nu\mathrm{V}(q)\}

satisfies the Lyapunov-drift condition. More precisely, there exists
\gamma\in(0,1)
,
\beta>0
,
s_{0}>0
,
r_{0}\leq s_{0}/2
and
\varepsilon>0
such that for the compact set

(8.44)

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}=\{(p,q)\in\mathsf{X}^{(2)}:p,q\in K(\varepsilon)\text{ and }\tilde{r}_{\sigma}(p,q)\geq r_{0},\text{ for all }\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{D}}\}

there holds

(8.45)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))\right]\leq\gamma\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}(p,q)+\beta\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{K}}(p,q)

for all
(p,q)\in\mathsf{X}^{(2)}
.

Proof.

Fix
s_{0}>0
sufficiently small, let
r_{0}<\frac{s_{0}}{2}
small enough such that
f_{\omega}(p)\in\mathsf{Q}_{\sigma}(s_{0}/2)
whenever
p\in\mathsf{Q}_{\sigma}(r_{0})
, for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
, and
\tilde{r}_{\sigma}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))<s_{0}
whenever
\tilde{r}_{\sigma}(p,q)<r_{0}
, for all
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{D}}
. Moreover, let
\eta\in(0,r_{0})
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\eta)
. We first show that

(8.46)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))\right]\leq\gamma\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}(p,q)

for all
(p,q)\not\in\widetilde{K}
, for
\gamma\in(0,1)
given the maximum contraction constant of the local drift functions
W
and
\mathrm{V}
.

\diamond

Case 1.
Assume
p,q\in K^{c}(\eta)
, that is
p\in\mathsf{Q}_{\sigma_{p}}(\eta)
and
q\in\mathsf{Q}_{\sigma_{q}}(\eta)
.

\diamond

Case 1.1.
Assume further that
\sigma_{p}\neq\sigma_{q}
. Then,
\widetilde{r}_{\mathsf{D}}(p,q)\geq 1
for
\eta
small enough and,
\mathcal{R}_{\sigma^{\prime}}p\in\mathsf{Q}_{\sigma_{p}}(\eta)
for all
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma
. In particular,
\widetilde{r}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(p,q)\geq 1
for all
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma
, for
\eta
small enough. Further choosing
\eta
smaller in terms of
N
if necessary, we also have
\widetilde{r}_{\sigma}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))\geq\frac{1}{2}
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{D}}
. For
s_{0}
small enough, we have that
\mathrm{W}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))=\mathrm{W}(p,q)=0
and thus

(8.47)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))\right]=\nu{\mathbb{E}}\left[\max\{\mathrm{V}(f_{\omega}(p)),\mathrm{V}(f_{\omega}(q))\}\right]\leq\nu\gamma\max\{\mathrm{V}(p),\mathrm{V}(q)\}=\gamma\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}(p,q).

\diamond

Case 1.2.
Suppose instead that
\sigma_{p}=\sigma_{q}
. For
\eta<r_{0}
small enough we either have
\widetilde{r}_{\mathsf{D}}\leq r_{0}
or
\widetilde{r}_{\mathsf{D}}\geq 1
.

\diamond

Case 1.2.1.
If
\tilde{r}_{\mathsf{D}}(p,q)\leq r_{0}
then
p,q
belong to the same connected component of
\mathsf{Q}_{\sigma}(\eta)
, they are close to the same fixed point
p_{F}\in F
and
\tilde{r}_{\tilde{\sigma}}(p,q)\geq 1
for
\tilde{\sigma}\neq\sigma,\mathsf{D}
. Hence, for
r_{0}
small enough, we further have that
\tilde{r}_{\tilde{\sigma}}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))\geq\frac{1}{2}
, for all
\omega\in[-N,N]^{2}
, for
\tilde{\sigma}\neq\sigma,\mathsf{D}
. Moreover,

(8.48)

\displaystyle\tilde{r}_{\sigma}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))=d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}(f_{\omega}(q),\mathcal{R}_{\sigma}f_{\omega}(p))

\displaystyle\leq d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}(f_{\omega}(q),p_{F})+d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}(p_{F},\mathcal{R}_{\sigma}f_{\omega}(p))

(8.49)

\displaystyle=d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}(f_{\omega}(q),p_{F})+d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}(p_{F},f_{\omega}(p))

(8.50)

\displaystyle\leq r_{0}

for
\eta
small enough so that
f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q)\in\mathsf{Q}_{\sigma}(r_{0}/2)
for all
\omega\in[-N,N]
whenever
p,q\in\mathsf{Q}_{\sigma}(\eta)
. Therefore,

(8.51)

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))=W_{\sigma}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))

for all
\omega\in[-N,N]^{2}
and thus we obtain (
8.46
).

\diamond

Case 1.2.2.
If
\tilde{r}_{\mathsf{D}}(p,q)\geq 1
then
\tilde{r}_{\mathsf{D}}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))\geq\frac{1}{2}
for
\eta
and
r_{0}
small enough. In particular,
f_{\omega}(p)
and
f_{\omega}(q)
belong to different connected components of
\mathsf{Q}_{\sigma}(r_{0})
, each close to
p_{F}\in F
and
q_{F}\in F
, respectively, and thus
\tilde{r}_{\sigma}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))\geq\frac{1}{4}
as well, for all
\omega\in[-N,N]^{2}
. Since
q_{F}=\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{\sigma}}p_{F}
for
\tilde{\sigma}\neq\sigma
, we have

(8.52)

\displaystyle\tilde{r}_{\tilde{\sigma}}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))\leq r_{0},

for
\tilde{\sigma}\neq\sigma
,
\tilde{\sigma}\neq\mathsf{D}
, for all
\omega\in[-N,N]^{2}
and thus
{\mathrm{W}}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))=W_{\tilde{\sigma}}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))
, for all
\omega\in[-N,N]^{2}
, for some
\tilde{\sigma}\in\{{\mathsf{x}},\mathsf{y},{\mathsf{z}}\}
, with
\tilde{\sigma}\neq\sigma
. In particular, we conclude that (
8.46
) holds.

\diamond

Case 2.
Assume now that
p\in K^{c}(\eta)
and
q\in K(\eta)
. Hence,
p\in\mathsf{Q}_{\sigma_{p}}(\eta)
for some
\sigma_{p}\in\Sigma
and
q\in\mathsf{Q}^{c}_{\sigma}(\eta)
, for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
.

\diamond

Case 2.1.
Assume there exists
\tilde{\sigma}\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{D}}
such that
\tilde{r}_{\tilde{\sigma}}(p,q)<r_{0}
. Then,
\tilde{r}_{\tilde{\sigma}}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))\leq s_{0}
for all
\omega\in[-N,N]^{2}
and there exists
\varpi\in\Sigma_{D}
such that
\mathrm{W}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))=W_{\varpi}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))
for all
\omega\in[-N,N]^{2}
. Hence,
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{W}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))\right]\leq\gamma\mathrm{W}(p,q)
. Next, let

(8.53)

\displaystyle\beta_{0}=\max_{\sigma\in\Sigma}\{\mathrm{V}(f_{\omega}(q)):q\in\mathsf{Q}^{c}_{\sigma}(\eta),\omega\in[-N,N]^{2}\}\sim\eta^{-\alpha}

and note that
\beta_{0}\leq C_{N}\eta^{-\frac{1}{8}}
, for
\eta
small enough in view of Lemma
9.2
. Therefore,

(8.54)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\nu\mathrm{V}(f_{\omega}(q))]\leq\nu C_{N}\eta^{-\frac{1}{8}}\leq\gamma W(p,q)

once we set
\nu=\frac{1}{4}\frac{\gamma\eta^{\frac{1}{8}}\min\psi}{r_{0}^{\xi}C_{N}}
. Consequently,
\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))\right]\leq\gamma\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}(p,q)
as well.

\diamond

Case 2.2.
On the other hand, if
\tilde{r}_{\tilde{\sigma}}(p,q)\geq r_{0}
for all
\widetilde{\sigma}\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{D}}
and
(p,q)\not\in\widetilde{K}
we must have
p\in\mathsf{Q}_{\sigma_{p}}(\varepsilon)
, for some
\sigma_{p}\in\Sigma
as
q\in K(\eta)\subset K(\varepsilon)
. Hence, set

(8.55)

\displaystyle\rho_{0}:=\min_{\sigma\in\{\mathsf{D},{\mathsf{x}},\mathsf{y},{\mathsf{z}}\},}\{\tilde{r}_{\sigma}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q)):\omega\in[-N,N]^{2},\tilde{r}_{\sigma}(p,q)\geq r_{0}\}.

and observe that
\mathrm{W}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))\leq\rho_{0}^{-\xi}\max\psi
. Recall also that since
q\in\mathsf{Q}_{\sigma}(\eta)^{c}
we have

(8.56)

\nu\mathrm{V}(f_{\omega}(q))\leq\nu C_{N}\eta^{-\alpha}\leq\gamma r_{0}^{-\xi}\min\psi.

In particular,

(8.57)

\displaystyle\max\{\rho_{0}^{-\xi}\max\psi,\gamma r_{0}^{-\xi}\min\psi\}\leq\gamma\nu V(p)\leq\gamma\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}(p,q)

provided that

(8.58)

\displaystyle\varepsilon\leq\left(\frac{\nu}{C_{N}\max\{\rho_{0}^{-\xi}\max\psi,\gamma r_{0}^{-\xi}\min\psi\}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}

as
\mathrm{V}(p)\geq C_{N}\varepsilon^{-\alpha}
for some universal
C_{N}>0
. Together with
{\mathbb{E}}[\mathrm{V}(f_{\omega}(p))]\leq\gamma\mathrm{V}(p)
, we reach (
8.46
).

\diamond

Case 3.
Assume finally that
p,q\in K(\eta)\subset K(\varepsilon)
. Hence,
p,q\in\mathsf{Q}_{\sigma}^{c}(\eta)
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
and we must then have
\tilde{r}_{\sigma}(p,q)<r_{0}
, for some
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{D}}
. Therefore,
\tilde{r}_{\sigma}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))<s_{0}
for all
\omega\in[-N,N]^{2}
and there exists
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{D}}
such that
\mathrm{W}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))=W_{\sigma^{\prime}}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))
, for all
\omega\in[-N,N]^{2}
. Thus,
\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{W}}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))\right]\leq\gamma\mathrm{W}(p,q)
. Moreover, recall that
\max\{\nu\mathrm{V}(f_{\omega}(p)),\nu\mathrm{V}(f_{\omega}(q))\}\leq\nu C_{N}\eta^{-\alpha}\leq\gamma W(p,q)
. As a result, (
8.46
) holds.

End of proof.
For
(p,q)\in\widetilde{K}
, we have that
\mathrm{W}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))\leq\rho^{-\xi}\max\psi
and
\mathrm{V}(f_{\omega}(p))\leq C_{N}\varepsilon^{-\alpha}
and similarly for
\mathrm{V}(f_{\omega}(q))
, for all
\omega\in[-N,N]^{2}
in view of Lemma
9.2
. Hence,
{\mathbb{E}}[\widetilde{\mathrm{W}}(f_{\omega}(p),f_{\omega}(q))]\leq\beta
, for some
\beta>0
.
∎

9.
The stochastic two-point process

In this section we derive the main estimates for the stochastic flow map
f_{\omega}^{\kappa}=f_{\omega_{2}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}},\kappa}\circ f_{\omega_{1}}^{{\boldsymbol{b}},\kappa}
that are later used to prove the existence of a
\kappa
-independent open small set and a drift function for the stochastic two-point process. Such function is defined in Section
11
and combines a local drift function
\mathrm{W}_{m}
for the stochastic two-point process near the diagonal and the symmetry sets, together with a suitably modified drift function
\mathrm{V}_{\kappa}
for the one-point process. In the remainder of the manuscript we assume without loss of generality that the two rotation axes are orthogonal and correspond as usual to
{\boldsymbol{a}}=(0,1,0)
and
{\boldsymbol{b}}=(0,0,1)
.

The local drift function
\mathrm{W}_{m}
for the stochastic two-point process reduces to the local drift function
\mathrm{W}
for the deterministic two-point process, which satisfies a Lyapunov-drift condition for the deterministic
P^{(2)}
Markov transition kernel. Using perturbative arguments and stochastic stability results, we show in Section
9.2
that the local drift function
\mathrm{W}
also satisfies a Lyapunov-drift condition for the stochastic
P^{(2)}_{\kappa}
Markov transition kernel near the diagonal, for all
\kappa>0
sufficiently small.

On the other hand, the drift function
\mathrm{V}_{\kappa}
for the one-point process localises on each fixed point of the deterministic dynamics. Introducing a cutoff at scale
\sqrt{\kappa}
, which corresponds to the size of the effective dynamics coming from the Brownian motion, we show in Section
10
the existence of functions
V_{\sigma,\kappa}
, with
\sigma\in\{{\mathsf{x}},{\mathsf{y}},{\mathsf{z}}\}
that satisfy a Lyapunov-drift condition for the stochastic
P_{\kappa}
Markov transition kernel.

9.1.
Stochastic stability for SDEs

Let
\kappa\geq 0
and
u\in\mathfrak{X}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
. We define
v_{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}):=u({\mathbf{x}})-2\kappa{\mathbf{x}}
and consider the stochastic flow
\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})
defined by the stochastic differential equation

(9.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})

\displaystyle=v_{\kappa}(\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}))\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2\kappa}\Pi_{\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})}\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{B}}_{t},

(9.2)

\displaystyle\phi_{0}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})

\displaystyle={\mathbf{x}}.

For simplicity, we denote
\phi_{t}=\phi_{t}^{0}
the associated deterministic flow. In this section we obtain several stochastic stability estimates for
\phi_{t}^{\kappa}
. We begin by showing the standard fact that the deviation of
\phi_{t}^{\kappa}
from the deterministic evolution
\phi_{t}
is no larger, up to a constant, than
\sqrt{\kappa t}
in expectation.

Lemma 9.1
.

There exists
C_{u}=C(\|u\|_{C^{1}})>0
such that

(9.3)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left|\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{t}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})\right|^{2}\leq C_{u}{\kappa t},

for all
\kappa\leq 1
and all
t\leq 1
.

Proof.

We observe that

(9.4)

\displaystyle\left|\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{t}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\left|v_{\kappa}(\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}))-v_{\kappa}(\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{x}}))\right|\mathrm{d}s+\sqrt{2\kappa}\left|\int_{0}^{t}\left(\Pi_{\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})}-\Pi_{\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})}\right)\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{B}}_{s}\right|

(9.5)

\displaystyle\quad+{2\kappa}\int_{0}^{t}\left|\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})\right|\mathrm{d}s+\sqrt{2\kappa}\left|\int_{0}^{t}\Pi_{\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})}\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{B}}_{s}\right|.

Since
\left|\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})\right|=1
for all
t\geq 0
and
\|\Pi\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim 1
, for
h(t):={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{B}}}\left|\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{t}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})\right|^{2}
we obtain

(9.6)

\displaystyle h(t)\leq C\kappa t+\left(\|v_{\kappa}\|_{C^{1}}^{2}+2\kappa\|\Pi\|_{C^{1}}^{2}\right)\int_{0}^{t}h(s)\mathrm{d}s.

Further noting that
\|\Pi\|_{C^{1}}\lesssim 1
and
\|v_{\kappa}\|_{C^{1}}\lesssim 1
for all
\kappa\leq 1
we conclude with Grönwall’s inequality that

(9.7)

\displaystyle h(t)\leq C\kappa t\exp(Ct),

for some
C>1
. With this the lemma is proved.
∎

The next Lemma records the usual Lipschitz property of
\phi_{t}^{\kappa}
in expectation and the bi-Lipschitz property of
\phi_{t}
.

Lemma 9.2
.

There exists
C_{0}>0
such that for all
\kappa\leq 1
and
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
we have

(9.8)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left|\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})\right|^{2}\leq C_{0}|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{2},

for all
t\in[0,1]
. Moreover,

(9.9)

\displaystyle C_{0}^{-1}|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{2}\leq\left|\phi_{t}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{t}^{0}({\mathbf{y}})\right|^{2}\leq C_{0}|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{2},

for all
t\in[0,1]
.

Proof.

Let
v_{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})=u({\mathbf{x}})-2\kappa{\mathbf{x}}
, we observe that

(9.10)

\displaystyle\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})={\mathbf{x}}+\int_{0}^{t}v_{\kappa}(\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}))\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{2\kappa}\Pi_{\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})}\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{B}}_{s},

and similarly for
\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})
. Hence,

(9.11)

\displaystyle\left|\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{2}+\left|\int_{0}^{t}\left(v_{\kappa}(\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}))-v_{\kappa}(\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}}))\right)\mathrm{d}s\right|^{2}

(9.12)

\displaystyle\quad+2\kappa\left|\int_{0}^{t}\left(\Pi_{\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})}-\Pi_{\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})}\right)\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{B}}_{s}\right|^{2}.

In particular, for
h(t):={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left|\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})\right|^{2}
we have

(9.13)

\displaystyle h(t)\lesssim h(0)+\|v_{\kappa}\|_{C^{1}}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}h(s)\mathrm{d}s+2\kappa\|\Pi\|_{C^{1}}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}h(s)\mathrm{d}s,

due to the Itô isometry
{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left|\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{G}(s)\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{B}}_{s}\right|^{2}=\int_{0}^{t}\|\mathbf{G}(s)\|^{2}\mathrm{d}s
. Using Grönwall’s lemma, we reach

(9.14)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left|\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})\right|^{2}\lesssim|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{2},

for all
0\leq t\leq 1
, as
\|v_{\kappa}\|_{C^{1}}+\|\Pi\|_{C^{1}}\lesssim 1
uniformly for all
0\leq\kappa\leq 1
. In particular, for
\kappa=0
we obtain a uniform Lipschitz bound for
\phi_{t}^{0}
for all
t\in[0,1]
. We finish the proof by showing that
\phi_{t}^{0}
is in fact bi-Lipschitz. To that purpose, note that

(9.15)

\displaystyle\phi_{1-t}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{1-t}^{0}({\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{1}^{0}({\mathbf{y}})-\int_{1-t}^{1}\left(u(\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{x}}))-u(\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{y}}))\right)\mathrm{d}s

so that for
g(t):=\left|\phi_{1-t}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{1-t}^{0}({\mathbf{y}})\right|^{2}
we now have

(9.16)

\displaystyle g(t)\lesssim g(0)+\|u\|_{C^{1}}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}g(s)\mathrm{d}s,

from which we deduce with Grönwall inequality that
g(t)\lesssim g(0)
, that is,
|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{2}\lesssim|\phi_{1-t}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{1-t}^{0}({\mathbf{y}})|^{2}
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
∎

The next result is key for proving drift-function estimates for the stochastic two-point process. A similar estimate was derived for the Kraichnan model in
[
15
]
, see also
[
24
,
44
]
.

Lemma 9.3
.

There exists
C_{0}>0
such that for all
\xi\in(0,1]
,
\kappa\leq 1
and
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
with
{\mathbf{x}}\neq{\mathbf{y}}
we have

(9.17)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left[\frac{1}{|\phi_{1}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{1}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})|^{\xi}}\right]\leq\frac{C_{0}^{\xi}}{|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{\xi}}.

Proof.

For convenience, let
Y_{t}=\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})
. It satisfies the stochastic differential equation

(9.18)

\displaystyle dY_{t}

\displaystyle=(v_{\kappa}(\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}))-v_{\kappa}(\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})))\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2\kappa}\left(\Pi_{\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})}-\Pi_{\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})}\right)\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{B}}_{t}

(9.19)

\displaystyle=b(t)\mathrm{d}t+G(t)\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{B}}_{t},

and we note that

(9.20)

\displaystyle\left|b^{i}(t)\right|+\left|G^{ij}(t)\right|\leq\left(\|v_{\kappa}\|_{C^{1}}+{\sqrt{2\kappa}}\|\Pi\|_{C^{1}}\right)\left|Y_{t}\right|\leq C\left|Y_{t}\right|,

for all
i,j=1,2,3
, for some
C>1
uniformly for all
\kappa\leq 1
. We also define the process
r_{t}^{\varepsilon}=(|Y_{t}|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{-1/2}
and the function
g_{\varepsilon}(x)=(|x|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{-1/2}
for
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
and
\varepsilon>0
. Since

(9.21)

\displaystyle\partial_{i}g_{\varepsilon}(x)=-\frac{x_{i}}{(|x|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}},\quad\partial_{i}\partial_{j}g_{\varepsilon}(x)=3\frac{x_{i}x_{j}}{(|x|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{5}{2}}}-\frac{1}{(|x|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}\delta_{ij},

we obtain from Itô’s Lemma that

(9.22)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}r_{t}^{\varepsilon}=-\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{Y_{t}^{i}}{(|Y_{t}|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}\mathrm{d}Y_{t}^{i}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{3}\left(3\frac{Y_{t}^{i}Y_{t}^{j}}{(|Y_{t}|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{5}{2}}}-\frac{1}{(|Y_{t}|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}\delta_{ij}\right)\sum_{k=1}^{3}G^{ik}(t)G^{jk}(t)\mathrm{d}t.

Hence, there holds

(9.23)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}r_{t}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=r_{0}^{\varepsilon}-{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{Y_{s}^{i}}{(|Y_{s}|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}b^{i}(s)\mathrm{d}s

(9.24)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i,j=1}^{3}\left(3\frac{Y_{s}^{i}Y_{s}^{j}}{(|Y_{s}|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{5}{2}}}-\frac{1}{(|Y_{s}|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}\delta_{ij}\right)\sum_{k=1}^{3}G^{ik}(s)G^{jk}(s)\mathrm{d}s,

where we have used the martingale property. Since
r_{t}^{\varepsilon}>0
for all
t\in[0,1]
, there holds
{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}[|r_{t}^{\varepsilon}|]={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}[r_{t}^{\varepsilon}]
and thus

(9.25)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}[r_{t}^{\varepsilon}]\leq r_{0}^{\varepsilon}+C\int_{0}^{t}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}[r_{s}^{\varepsilon}]\mathrm{d}s

due to (
9.20
). For
h_{\varepsilon}(t)={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}r_{t}^{\varepsilon}
from Grönwall inequality we deduce that

(9.26)

\displaystyle h_{\varepsilon}(t)\leq Ch_{\varepsilon}(0)\exp(Ct)\leq C_{0}|Y_{0}|^{-1},

and thus the lemma for
\xi=1
follows after using Fatou’s Lemma for
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. Finally, for
\xi\in(0,1)
the function
x\mapsto x^{\xi}
is concave and thus Jensen’s inequality yields

(9.27)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left[\frac{1}{|\phi_{1}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{1}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})|^{\xi}}\right]\leq\left({\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left[\frac{1}{|\phi_{1}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{1}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})|}\right]\right)^{\xi}\leq\frac{C_{0}^{\xi}}{|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{\xi}}.

∎

Using triangular inequality and the stochastic stability of Lemma
9.1
, we easily obtain the following.

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
u\in\mathfrak{X}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
. For all
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
and
t\in[0,1]
define

(9.28)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}):=\left|\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})-(\phi_{t}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{t}^{0}({\mathbf{y}}))\right|.

Then, there exists
C>0
such that

(9.29)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\vartheta_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\leq C\sqrt{\kappa},

for all
\kappa\leq 1
and all
t\in[0,1]
.

The next lemma improves the estimate on the deviation of the separation vector under the random flow,
X_{t}^{\kappa}:=\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})
from the separation vector under the deterministic flow,
X_{t}:=\phi_{t}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{t}^{0}({\mathbf{y}})
.

Lemma 9.5
.

Let
u\in\mathfrak{X}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
. For all
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
and
t\in[0,1]
define

(9.30)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}):=\left|\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})-(\phi_{t}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{t}^{0}({\mathbf{y}}))\right|.

Then, there exists
C>0
such that

(9.31)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\vartheta_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\leq C\left(\sqrt{\kappa}+|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|\right)\left|\phi_{1}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{1}^{0}({\mathbf{y}})\right|,

for all
\kappa\leq 1
.

Proof.

Note that

(9.32)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\leq\left|\int_{0}^{t}\left(v_{\kappa}(\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}))-v_{\kappa}(\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}}))-\left(v_{\kappa}(\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{x}}))-v_{\kappa}(\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{y}}))\right)\right)\mathrm{d}s\right|

(9.33)

\displaystyle\quad+\sqrt{2\kappa}\left|\int_{0}^{t}\left(\Pi_{\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})}-\Pi_{\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})}-\left(\Pi_{\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})}-\Pi_{\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{y}})}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{B}}_{s}\right|

(9.34)

\displaystyle\quad+2\kappa\left|\int_{0}^{t}\left(\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{y}})\right)\mathrm{d}s\right|+\left|\sqrt{2\kappa}\int_{0}^{t}\left(\Pi_{\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})}-\Pi_{\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{y}})}\right)\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{B}}_{s}\right|.

Setting
h(t):={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}(\vartheta_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}))^{2}
and using Itô’s isometry we obtain

(9.35)

\displaystyle h(t)

\displaystyle\leq\|v_{\kappa}\|_{C^{1}}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}h(s)\mathrm{d}s+2\kappa\|\Pi\|_{C^{1}}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}h(s)\mathrm{d}s

(9.36)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{t}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left|D_{\mathbf{x}}v_{\kappa}(\xi_{1}(s))-D_{\mathbf{x}}v_{\kappa}(\xi_{2}(s))\right|^{2}\left|\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{y}})\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}s

(9.37)

\displaystyle\quad+2\kappa\int_{0}^{t}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left|D_{\mathbf{x}}\Pi_{\tilde{\xi}_{1}(s)}-D_{\mathbf{x}}\Pi_{\tilde{\xi}_{2}(s)}\right|^{2}\left|\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{y}})\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}s

(9.38)

\displaystyle\quad+4\kappa^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left|\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{y}})\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}s+2\kappa\|\Pi\|_{C^{1}}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left|\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{y}})\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}s

for some
\xi_{1}(s),\tilde{\xi}_{1}(s),\xi_{2}(s),\tilde{\xi}_{2}(s)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
with

(9.39)

\displaystyle\left|\xi_{1}(s)-\xi_{2}(s)\right|\leq\max\{\left|\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})\right|,\left|\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})-\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{y}})\right|,\left|\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{y}})\right|,\left|\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})-\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})\right|\}

and likewise for
\left|\tilde{\xi}_{1}(s)-\tilde{\xi}_{2}(s)\right|
. In view of Lemma
9.1
and Lemma
9.2
, we deduce that

(9.40)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left|\xi_{1}(s)-\xi_{2}(s)\right|^{2}\leq C(\sqrt{\kappa}+|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|)^{2}

for some
C>1
. Therefore, noting that
\|v_{\kappa}\|_{C^{2}}^{2}+2\kappa\|\Pi\|_{C^{2}}^{2}\lesssim 1
uniformly for all
\kappa\leq 1
, Lemma
9.2
gives

(9.41)

\displaystyle h(t)

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{t}h(s)\mathrm{d}s+C(\sqrt{\kappa t}+|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|)^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left|\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{s}^{0}({\mathbf{y}})\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}s

(9.42)

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{t}h(s)\mathrm{d}s+C(\sqrt{\kappa t}+|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|)^{2}\left|\phi_{1}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{1}^{0}({\mathbf{y}})\right|^{2}.

As a result, Grönwall inequality yields

(9.43)

\displaystyle h(t)\leq C(\sqrt{\kappa t}+|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|)^{2}\left|\phi_{1}^{0}({\mathbf{x}})-\phi_{1}^{0}({\mathbf{y}})\right|^{2}\exp(Ct)

and the lemma follows from
{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\vartheta_{t}^{\kappa}\leq\sqrt{h(t)}
.
∎

We finish the subsection by specializing to the flow maps considered in this manuscript. Recall that, for
N>1
and for
\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in[-N,N]^{2}
, we denote with
f_{\omega}^{\kappa}:=f_{\omega_{2}}^{2,\kappa}\circ f_{\omega_{1}}^{3,\kappa}
and
f_{\omega}:=f_{\omega_{2}}^{2}\circ f_{\omega_{1}}^{3}
the stochastic and deterministic one-point processes, respectively, arising from (
1.8
). The usual Grönwall-type arguments and Burkhölder inequality yield the following result.

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
and
n\geq 1
. Then, there exists
C_{N,n}>0
such that

(9.44)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left[\sup_{\underline{\omega}\in[-N,N]^{2n}}|f_{\underline{\omega}}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})-f_{\underline{\omega}}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})|\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{N,n}|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|

and

(9.45)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left[\sup_{{\underline{\omega}}\in[-N,N]^{2n}}|f_{\underline{\omega}}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})-f_{\underline{\omega}}({\mathbf{x}})|\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{N,n}\sqrt{\kappa},

for all
\kappa\in(0,1)
.

Proof.

We show the first inequality, as the ideas to prove both bounds are similar. Fix
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
and
\kappa\in(0,1)
, let
g_{\underline{\omega}}(t)=\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}};{\underline{\omega}})-\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}};{\underline{\omega}})
, where
\phi_{t}^{\kappa}(\cdot,{\underline{\omega}}):=\phi_{t}^{\kappa}(\cdot)
solves the Itô differential equation (
1.8
) with vector field
u(t,\cdot,\underline{\omega};{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})
. In particular,
g_{\underline{\omega}}(2n)=f_{\underline{\omega}}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})-f_{\underline{\omega}}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})
. There holds

(9.46)

\displaystyle g_{\underline{\omega}}(s)

\displaystyle={\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}+\int_{0}^{s}\left(u(r,\phi_{r}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},{\underline{\omega}});{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})-u(r,\phi_{r}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}},{\underline{\omega}});{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})\right)\mathrm{d}r+2\kappa\int_{0}^{s}g_{\underline{\omega}}(r)\mathrm{d}r

(9.47)

\displaystyle\quad+\sqrt{2\kappa}\int_{0}^{s}\left(\Pi_{\phi_{r}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},{\underline{\omega}})}-\Pi_{\phi_{r}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}},{\underline{\omega}})}\right)\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{B}}_{r}.

Let
p\geq 1
and
G_{\underline{\omega}}(t)={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left[|\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}|g_{\underline{\omega}}(s)||^{p}\right]
. Using Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality,

(9.48)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{B}}}\left[\left(\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\left|\int_{0}^{s}\left(\Pi_{\phi_{r}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},{\underline{\omega}})}-\Pi_{\phi_{r}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}},{\underline{\omega}})}\right)\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{B}}_{r}\right|\right)^{p}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{B}}}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left(\Pi_{\phi_{r}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},{\underline{\omega}})}-\Pi_{\phi_{r}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}},{\underline{\omega}})}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}r\right)^{p/2}\right]

(9.49)

\displaystyle\lesssim{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{B}}}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{t}|g_{\underline{\omega}}(r)|^{2}\mathrm{d}r\right)^{p/2}\right]

(9.50)

\displaystyle\lesssim{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{B}}}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{t}(\sup_{0\leq s\leq r}|g_{\underline{\omega}}(s)|)^{2}\mathrm{d}r\right)^{p/2}\right]

(9.51)

\displaystyle\lesssim{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{B}}}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\left(\sup_{0\leq s\leq r}|g_{\underline{\omega}}(s)|\right)^{p}\mathrm{d}r\right]

(9.52)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}G_{\underline{\omega}}(r)\mathrm{d}r,

while we also have

(9.53)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{B}}}\left[\left(\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\left|\int_{0}^{s}\left(u(r,\phi_{r}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},{\underline{\omega}});{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})-u(r,\phi_{r}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},{\underline{\omega}});{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})\right)\mathrm{d}r\right|\right)^{p}\right]\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}G_{\underline{\omega}}(r)\mathrm{d}r,

and similarly for the remaining contribution. Hence, using Grönwall on
G_{\underline{\omega}}(t)
we conclude that

(9.54)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,2n]}G_{\underline{\omega}}(t)\leq C_{N}|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{p},

for all
\underline{\omega}\in[-N-1,N+1]^{2n}
, for some
C_{N}>0
independent of
\underline{\omega}
.

Now, to exchange the supremum in
{\underline{\omega}}
and the expectation in
{\mathbf{B}}
we use the Sobolev inequality. For this, we consider first
\partial_{j}\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}};\underline{\omega}):=\partial_{\omega_{j}}\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}};\underline{\omega})
and
g_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}=\partial_{\omega_{j}}g_{\underline{\omega}}(t)
, where
\underline{\omega}=(\omega_{1},...,\omega_{2n})
and
1\leq j\leq 2n
. There holds

(9.55)

\displaystyle\partial_{j}\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}};\underline{\omega})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left(\left(Du(s,\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},\underline{\omega});{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})-2\kappa Id\right)\partial_{j}\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}};\underline{\omega})\right)\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{\omega_{j}}u(s,\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},\underline{\omega});{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})\mathrm{d}s

(9.56)

\displaystyle\quad+\sqrt{2\kappa}\int_{0}^{t}D\Pi_{\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},\underline{\omega})}\partial_{j}\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},\underline{\omega})\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{B}}_{s}.

We next note that
\partial_{\omega_{j}}u(s,\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},\underline{\omega});{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})=u^{\sigma}(\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},\underline{\omega}))1_{[t_{j},t_{j}+1)}(s)
for some
\sigma\in\{{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}}\}
and some
t_{j}\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
0\leq t_{j}\leq 2n-1
and
u^{\sigma}
uniformly bounded. Hence, a Grönwall-type argument and Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality yields

(9.57)

\displaystyle\sup_{\underline{\omega}\in[-N-1,N+1]^{2n}}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}(\sup_{t\in[0,2n]}|\partial_{j}\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},\underline{\omega})|)^{p}\leq C,

for some
C>0
. On the other hand,

(9.58)

\displaystyle g_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}(\partial_{\omega_{j}}u(s,\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},\underline{\omega}),{\underline{\omega}};{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})-\partial_{\omega_{j}}u(s,\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}},\underline{\omega}),{\underline{\omega}};{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}}))\mathrm{d}s

(9.59)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\left(Du(s,\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},\underline{\omega}),{\underline{\omega}};{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})\partial_{j}\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},\underline{\omega})-Du(s,\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}},\underline{\omega}),{\underline{\omega}};{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})\partial_{j}\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}},\underline{\omega})\right)\mathrm{d}s

(9.60)

\displaystyle\quad+2\kappa\int_{0}^{t}g_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(s)\mathrm{d}s+\sqrt{2\kappa}\int_{0}^{t}\left(D\Pi_{\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},\underline{\omega})}\partial_{j}\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},\underline{\omega})-D\Pi_{\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}},\underline{\omega})}\partial_{j}\phi_{s}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}},\underline{\omega})\right)\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{B}}_{s},

and we note that for
G_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(t):={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left[|\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}|g_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(s)||^{p}\right]
we have

(9.61)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{B}}}\left[\left|\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\int_{0}^{s}(\partial_{\omega_{j}}u(r,\phi_{r}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},\underline{\omega}),{\underline{\omega}};{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})-\partial_{\omega_{j}}u(r,\phi_{r}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}},\underline{\omega}),{\underline{\omega}};{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}}))\mathrm{d}r\right|^{p}\right]\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}G_{\underline{\omega}}(r)\mathrm{d}r\lesssim|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{p},

while also

(9.62)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{B}}}

\displaystyle\left[\left|\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\int_{0}^{s}\left(Du(r,\phi_{r}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},\underline{\omega}),{\underline{\omega}};{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})\partial_{j}\phi_{r}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},\underline{\omega})-Du(s,\phi_{r}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}},\underline{\omega}),{\underline{\omega}};{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{b}})\partial_{j}\phi_{r}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}},\underline{\omega})\right)\mathrm{d}r\right|^{p}\right]

(9.63)

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{B}}}\left[\int_{0}^{t}|g_{\underline{\omega}}(r)|^{p}|\partial_{j}\phi_{r}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},\underline{\omega})|^{p}\mathrm{d}r\right]+{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{B}}}\left[\int_{0}^{t}|g_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(r)|^{p}\mathrm{d}r\right]

(9.64)

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{B}}}\left[\int_{0}^{t}(\sup_{0\leq s\leq r}|g_{\underline{\omega}}(r)|)^{p}(\sup_{0\leq s\leq r}|\partial_{j}\phi_{r}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},\underline{\omega})|)^{p}\mathrm{d}r\right]+{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{B}}}\left[\int_{0}^{t}(\sup_{0\leq s\leq r}|g_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(r)|)^{p}\mathrm{d}r\right]

(9.65)

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{p}+\int_{0}^{t}G_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(r)\mathrm{d}r.

In the above we used that
E(|XY|)\leq E(X^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}E(Y^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
and that (
9.54
) and (
9.57
) hold for all
p\geq 1
. Appealing again to the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality for the martingale term and the Grönwall inequality, we conclude that

(9.66)

\displaystyle\sup_{\underline{\omega}\in[-N-1,N+1]^{2n}}\sup_{t\in[0,2n]}G_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(t)\lesssim|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{p}.

We are now in position to prove (
9.44
). Since
[-N,N]^{2n}\Subset[-N-1,N+1]^{2n}
we note that for all
\underline{\omega}\in[-N,N]^{2n}
and all
t\in[0,2n]
, we have the Sobolev inequality

(9.67)

\displaystyle\sup_{\underline{\omega}\in[-N,N]^{2n}}|g_{\underline{\omega}}(t)|\lesssim\left(\int_{[-N-1,N+1]^{2n}}|g_{\underline{\varpi}}(t)|^{p}\mathrm{d}\underline{\varpi}+\sum_{j=0}^{2n}\int_{[-N-1,N+1]^{2n}}|g_{\underline{\varpi}}^{j}(t)|^{p}\mathrm{d}\underline{\varpi}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

for all
p>2n
. Hence, in view of (
9.54
) and (
9.66
),

(9.68)

\displaystyle\left({\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{B}}}\left[\sup_{\underline{\omega}\in[-N,N]^{2n}}|g_{\underline{\omega}}(t)|\right]\right)^{p}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{B}}}\left[\sup_{\underline{\omega}\in[-N,N]^{2n}}|g_{\underline{\omega}}(t)|^{p}\right]

(9.69)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{[-N-1,N+1]^{2n}}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{B}}}\left[|g_{\underline{\varpi}}(t)|^{p}\right]\mathrm{d}\underline{\varpi}+\sum_{j=0}^{2n}\int_{[-N-1,N+1]^{2n}}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left[|g_{\underline{\varpi}}^{j}(t)|^{p}\right]\mathrm{d}\underline{\varpi}

(9.70)

\displaystyle\lesssim|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{p}

for all
t\in[0,2n]
. The bound (
9.44
) then follows.
∎

The ideas presented in the proof of Lemma
9.6
also show the Lipschitz continuity of
f_{\omega}^{\kappa}
with respect to the random-amplitude noise
\omega
.

Lemma 9.7
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
N>1
. There exists
C_{N,n}>0
such that

(9.71)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left[\sup_{\underline{\varpi}\in[-N,N]^{2n}}|D_{\underline{\omega}}f_{\underline{\varpi}}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})|\right]\leq C_{N,n}

In particular, for all
\kappa\in[0,1]
and all
{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathsf{X}
,

(9.72)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left[\sup_{\underset{\underline{\omega}\neq\underline{\varpi}}{\underline{\omega},\underline{\varpi}\in[-N,N]^{2n}}}\frac{|f_{\underline{\omega}}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})-f_{\underline{\varpi}}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})|^{2}}{|\underline{\omega}-\underline{\varpi}|_{\infty}^{2}}\right]\leq C_{N,n}.

Proof.

We obtain first uniform-in-time bounds on
p
-moments of
D_{\underline{\omega}}\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},\underline{\varpi})
that are uniform in
\varpi\in[-N-1,N+1]^{2n}
. These are then used to obtain uniform-in-time bounds on
p
-moments of
D^{2}_{\underline{\omega}}\phi_{t}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}},\underline{\varpi})
that are also uniform in
\varpi\in[-N-1,N+1]^{2n}
. The Sobolev inequality then yields the claimed estimate. We omit the routine details.
∎

9.2.
Local stochastic drift function for the two-point process

We begin by recalling from Proposition
2.6
that

(9.73)

W({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=d_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})^{-\xi}\psi({\mathbf{x}},\theta({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})),

for
\theta({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}):=\frac{\mathrm{exp}_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{-1}({\mathbf{y}})}{d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})}
, some
\psi:\mathsf{X}\times T\mathsf{X}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, and some
\xi\in(0,1)
. Here
\mathrm{exp}_{\mathbf{x}}:T_{\mathbf{x}}\mathsf{X}\to\mathsf{X}
denotes the exponential map at time
1
. We note that for
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, its distance
d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
on
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
is given by
d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=\arccos({\mathbf{x}}\cdot{\mathbf{y}})
, while if we view
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, then
d_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|
. The two distances are related by

(9.74)

\displaystyle|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|=2\sin\left(\frac{d_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})}{2}\right),\quad d_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=2\arcsin\left(\frac{|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|}{2}\right),\quad

and since
|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|\leq 2
and
d_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\leq\pi
for all
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
we have the expansions

(9.75)

\begin{split}|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|&=d_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-\mathfrak{R}_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})d_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})^{3},\\
d_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})&=|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|-\mathfrak{R}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{3},\end{split}

with
\|\mathfrak{R}_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}\times{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})}+\|\mathfrak{R}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\|_{L^{\infty}({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}\times{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})}\leq C
, for some
C>0
. Moreover,

(9.76)

\begin{split}|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{-\alpha}&=d_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})^{-\alpha}-\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})d_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})^{2-\alpha},\\
d_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})^{-\alpha}&=|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{-\alpha}-\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{2-\alpha},\end{split}

for
0<\alpha\leq 1
, where now
\|\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Delta(s_{0}))}+\|\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Delta(s_{0}))}\leq\widetilde{C}
for some
\widetilde{C}>0
and some
s_{0}>0
. In particular, for all
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that
|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|<\varepsilon
for all
d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})<\delta
. Then, in view of (
9.76
), for all
\xi\in(0,1]
there holds

(9.77)

\displaystyle d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})^{-\xi}\leq C_{2}|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{-\xi},\quad|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{-\xi}\leq C_{3}d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})^{-\xi},

for all
d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})<\delta
, for some universal constants
C_{2},C_{3}>0
.

We first show that the local drift function for the deterministic two-point process is stable under the stochastic dynamics if
\kappa
is small enough.

Proposition 9.8
.

Let
\varepsilon_{0}>0
. There exists
s_{*}>0
and
\kappa_{*}>0
such that

(9.78)

\displaystyle{{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left|W(f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}}))-W(f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}({\mathbf{y}}))\right|}<\varepsilon_{0}{W({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})}

for all
({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\in\Delta(\varepsilon)
, all
\omega\in[-N,N]^{2}
, all
\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{*})
and all
0<\varepsilon<s_{*}
.

Proof.

Fix
\varepsilon_{0}>0
. Note that

(9.79)

\displaystyle\left|W(f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}}))-W(f_{\omega}^{0}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}^{0}({\mathbf{y}}))\right|

\displaystyle=\left|(Y_{1}^{\kappa})^{-\xi}\psi^{\kappa}-(Y_{1})^{-\xi}\psi^{0}\right|

(9.80)

\displaystyle\leq\psi^{\kappa}\left|(Y_{1}^{\kappa})^{-\xi}-(Y_{1})^{-\xi}\right|+(Y_{1})^{-\xi}\left|\psi^{\kappa}-\psi^{0}\right|,

where

(9.81)

\displaystyle Y_{1}^{\kappa}:=d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}(f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})),\quad Y_{1}:=d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}(f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}({\mathbf{y}}))

and for
\theta({\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}):=\frac{\mathrm{exp}_{{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}}^{-1}({\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})}{d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}({\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})}
we denote

(9.82)

\displaystyle\psi^{\kappa}=\psi(f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}),\theta(f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}}))),\quad\psi^{0}=\psi(f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}}),\theta(f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}({\mathbf{y}}))).

Let
\eta:=\frac{Y_{1}}{2}
and consider the event
E:=\{Y_{1}^{\kappa}\geq\eta\}
. The mean value Theorem then yields

(9.83)

\displaystyle||Y_{1}^{\kappa}|^{-\xi}-|Y_{1}|^{-\xi}|\leq\xi\left(|Y_{1}^{\kappa}|\wedge|Y_{1}|\right)^{-(\xi+1)}|Y_{1}^{\kappa}-Y_{1}|,

where
a\wedge b:=\min(a,b)
. Hence, on
E
we have

(9.84)

\left(|Y_{1}^{\kappa}|\wedge|Y_{1}|\right)^{-(\xi+1)}\leq\eta^{-(\xi+1)}.

We introduce also

(9.85)

\displaystyle X_{1}^{\kappa}:=|f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})-f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})|,\quad X_{1}:=|f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}})-f_{\omega}({\mathbf{y}})|.

From (
9.75
) and noting that
|X_{1}^{\kappa}|\leq 2
because
f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
for all
\kappa\geq 0
, we obtain

(9.86)

\displaystyle\left|Y_{1}^{\kappa}-Y_{1}\right|\leq|X_{1}^{\kappa}-X_{1}|+C\left(|X_{1}^{\kappa}|^{2}+|X_{1}|^{2}\right)

and since
|X_{1}^{\kappa}-X_{1}|\leq\vartheta_{1}^{\kappa}
we deduce from Lemmas
9.2
and
9.5
that

(9.87)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left|Y_{1}^{\kappa}-Y_{1}\right|\lesssim\left(\sqrt{\kappa}+|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|\right)|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|+|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{2}.

Therefore

(9.88)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left[|\psi^{\kappa}||Y_{1}^{\kappa}|^{-\xi}-|Y_{1}|^{-\xi}|\mathbf{1}_{E}\right]\lesssim\eta^{-(\xi+1)}|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|\left(\sqrt{\kappa}+|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|\right).

An application of (
9.77
) and Lemma
9.2
together with
1+\xi>0
and
\inf\psi>0
yields

(9.89)

\displaystyle\eta^{-(\xi+1)}\lesssim|Y_{1}|^{-(\xi+1)}\lesssim|X_{1}|^{-(\xi+1)}\lesssim|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{-(\xi+1)}\lesssim|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{-1}\mathrm{d}_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})^{-\xi}\lesssim|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{-1}W({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}).

As a result, we conclude that

(9.90)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left[|\psi^{\kappa}||Y_{1}^{\kappa}|^{-\xi}-|Y_{1}|^{-\xi}|\mathbf{1}_{E}\right]\lesssim\left(\sqrt{\kappa}+|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|\right)W({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}).

On the other hand, for the event
E^{c}=\{|Y_{1}^{\kappa}|<\eta\}
, we observe that
\vartheta_{1}^{\kappa}\geq|Y_{1}|-|Y_{1}^{\kappa}|\geq\eta
and thus
E^{c}\subseteq\{\vartheta_{1}^{\kappa}\geq\eta\}
. In particular, Markov inequality yields

(9.91)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(E^{c})\leq{\mathbb{P}}\left(\vartheta_{1}^{\kappa}\geq\eta\right)\leq\eta^{-1}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\vartheta_{1}^{\kappa}\leq C\left(\sqrt{\kappa}+|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|\right),

after noting that
\eta^{-1}\lesssim|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{-1}
in view of (
9.77
) and Lemma
9.2
. Moreover,

(9.92)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left[|\psi^{\kappa}||Y_{1}^{\kappa}|^{-\xi}-|Y_{1}|^{-\xi}|\mathbf{1}_{E^{c}}\right]\lesssim{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}[|Y_{1}^{\kappa}|^{-\xi}\mathbf{1}_{E^{c}}]+{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}[|Y_{1}|^{-\xi}\mathbf{1}_{E^{c}}]\lesssim{|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{-\xi}}\lesssim W({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}),

where we used Lemma
9.3
and the Hölder inequality

(9.93)

{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}[|Y_{1}^{\kappa}|^{-\xi}\mathbf{1}_{E^{c}}]\leq\left({\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}[|Y_{1}^{\kappa}|^{-1}]\right)^{\xi}({\mathbb{P}}(E^{c}))^{1-\xi}

with
p=\frac{1}{\xi}>1
and
q=\frac{1}{1-\xi}>1
. Hence, combining (
9.90
), (
9.91
), and (
9.93
),

(9.94)

\begin{split}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left[|\psi^{\kappa}||Y_{1}^{\kappa}|^{-\xi}-|Y_{1}|^{-\xi}|\right]&={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left[|\psi^{\kappa}||Y_{1}^{\kappa}|^{-\xi}-|Y_{1}|^{-\xi}|\mathbf{1}_{E}\right]+{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left[|\psi^{\kappa}||Y_{1}^{\kappa}|^{-\xi}-|Y_{1}|^{-\xi}|\mathbf{1}_{E}^{c}\right]\\
&\lesssim(\sqrt{\kappa}+|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|)^{1-\xi}W({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}).\end{split}

We next argue for
{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left[|Y_{1}|^{-\xi}|\psi^{\kappa}-\psi^{0}|\right]
. First, the function
\psi
is uniformly continuous, namely for all
({\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},\theta_{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}),({\mathbf{y}}^{\prime},\theta_{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})\in UT{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
and all
\epsilon_{0}>0
there exists
\delta_{0}>0
such that
|\psi({\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},\theta_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\prime})-\psi({\mathbf{y}}^{\prime},\theta_{{\mathbf{y}}}^{\prime})|<\epsilon_{0}
whenever
d(({\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},\theta_{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}),({\mathbf{y}}^{\prime},\theta_{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}))<\delta_{0}
. In our setting,

(9.95)

\displaystyle d(({\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},\theta_{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}),({\mathbf{y}}^{\prime},\theta_{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}))\leq d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}(f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}}))+\left|\theta(f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}}))-\theta(f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}({\mathbf{y}}))\right|

after denoting

(9.96)

({\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},\theta_{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}):=(f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}),\theta(f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})),\quad({\mathbf{y}}^{\prime},\theta_{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}):=(f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}}),\theta(f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}({\mathbf{y}})).

Moreover, for
{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, let
\varphi({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})=\mathrm{exp}_{\mathbf{z}}^{-1}({\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})
so that
\mathrm{exp}_{\mathbf{z}}(\varphi({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime}))={\mathbf{z}}^{\prime}
, by definition of exponential map, and
\|\varphi({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})\|=d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})
.
On
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, we have that

(9.97)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime}=\cos(d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})){\mathbf{z}}+\sin(d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime}))\frac{\varphi({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})}{d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})}

and, since
\theta({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})=\frac{\varphi({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})}{d_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})}
, it holds

(9.98)

\displaystyle f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle=\cos(Y_{1}^{\kappa})f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})+\sin(Y_{1}^{\kappa})\theta(f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})),

(9.99)

\displaystyle f_{\omega}({\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle=\cos(Y_{1})f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}})+\sin(Y_{1}){\theta(f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}({\mathbf{y}}))}.

Subtracting leads to

(9.100)

\displaystyle\theta(f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}}))-\theta(f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}({\mathbf{y}}))

\displaystyle=\frac{(f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}})-f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}))-(f_{\omega}({\mathbf{y}})-f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}}))}{\sin(Y_{1})}+\frac{(1-\cos(Y_{1}))(f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})-f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}}))}{\sin(Y_{1})}

(9.101)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})\left(\cos(Y_{1}^{\kappa})-\cos(Y_{1})\right)}{\sin(Y_{1})}+\frac{\theta(f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}}))\left(\sin(Y_{1}^{\kappa})-\sin(Y_{1})\right)}{\sin(Y_{1})}.

For
d_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})<s_{*}
and
s_{*}>0
small enough, combining the trivial inequality

(9.102)

Y_{1}\lesssim X_{1}\lesssim|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|\lesssim d_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})<\frac{\pi}{4},

with (
9.77
) gives
\sin^{-1}(Y_{1})\leq 2(Y_{1})^{-1}\lesssim|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{-1}
and
|1-\cos(Y_{1})|\lesssim|Y_{1}|^{2}
.
Therefore, since
\|f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})\|=\|\theta(f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}}))\|=1
, a direct application of the mean value theorem and Lemmas
9.1
and
9.5
concludes that

(9.103)

\begin{split}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left|\theta(f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}}))-\theta(f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}({\mathbf{y}}))\right|&\lesssim|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{-1}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}[\vartheta_{1}^{\kappa}]+{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}[\left|f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}})-f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}})\right|]\\
&\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa}+|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|.\end{split}

Note that
|Y_{1}|^{-\xi}\lesssim W({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
follows from Lemma
9.2
and consider now the set
F=\{d(({\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},\theta_{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}),({\mathbf{y}}^{\prime},\theta_{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}))<\delta_{0}\}
. Then,

(9.104)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left[|Y_{1}|^{-\xi}|\psi^{\kappa}-\psi^{0}|\big|F\right]\lesssim\epsilon_{0}W({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{8}W({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}),

for
\epsilon_{0}
sufficiently small. On its complement,
F^{c}
, from

(9.105)

\displaystyle\{d(({\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},\theta_{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}),({\mathbf{y}}^{\prime},\theta_{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}))\geq\delta_{0}\}\subseteq\left\{d_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}(f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}}))\geq\frac{\delta_{0}}{2}\right\}\bigcup\left\{|\theta(f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}^{\kappa}({\mathbf{y}}))-\theta(f_{\omega}({\mathbf{x}}),f_{\omega}({\mathbf{y}}))|\geq\frac{\delta_{0}}{2}\right\}

we have
{\mathbb{P}}(F^{c})\lesssim_{\delta_{0}}\sqrt{\kappa}+|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|
due to Lemma
9.2
and (
9.103
).
In addition, Lemma
9.2
implies

(9.106)

|Y_{1}|^{-\xi}|\psi^{\kappa}-\psi^{0}|\lesssim|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|^{-\xi}\lesssim W({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})

from which it follows

(9.107)

\begin{split}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left[|Y_{1}|^{-\xi}|\psi^{\kappa}-\psi^{0}|\right]&={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left[|Y_{1}|^{-\xi}|\psi^{\kappa}-\psi^{0}|\big|F\right]{\mathbb{P}}(F)+{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{B}}\left[|Y_{1}|^{-\xi}|\psi^{\kappa}-\psi^{0}|\big|F^{c}\right]{\mathbb{P}}(F^{c})\\
&\lesssim_{\delta_{0}}(\sqrt{\kappa}+|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}|)W({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}).\end{split}

Gathering (
9.94
) and (
9.107
), the proposition is proved after choosing
s_{*}>0
and
\kappa_{*}>0
small enough.
∎

10.
Drift function for the stochastic one-point process

In this section we adapt the drift function
\mathrm{V}
for the one-point process to the presence of the stochastic term coming from the diffusion operator. We thus define
\mathrm{V}_{\kappa}
