Appendix A
Commutators of vector fields

In this section we show that the commutators of the lifted vector fields
\widetilde{u_{\boldsymbol{a}}}
and
\widetilde{u_{\boldsymbol{b}}}
of Proposition
7.2
can be computed in terms of
u^{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
u^{\boldsymbol{b}}
.

A.1.
Commutators on smooth manifolds

We begin by characterising the commutator of vector fields on a smooth manifold
\mathrm{M}
in terms of the flow maps they locally generate.

Lemma A.1

(
[
32
]
*Corollary 9.14)
.

Let
\mathrm{M}
,
\mathrm{N}
be smooth manifolds and let
F:\mathrm{M}\rightarrow\mathrm{N}
be a diffeomorphism. If
Y\in T\mathrm{M}
is a vector field on
\mathrm{M}
and
\Phi_{s}^{Y}
is the flow of
Y
, then the flow of the push-forward vector field
F_{*}Y
is given by
\Phi_{s}^{F_{*}Y}=F\circ\Phi_{s}^{Y}\circ F^{-1}
.

Definition A.2
.

Let
\mathrm{M}
be a smooth manifold,
X\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathrm{M})
be a smooth vector field on
\mathrm{M}
and
\Phi_{t}^{X}
be the flow associated to
X
. Let
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathrm{M})
. Then, the Lie derivative of
Y
with respect to
X
is

(A.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{X}Y=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}(d\Phi_{-t}^{X})|_{\Phi_{t}^{X}}(Y(\Phi_{t}^{X}))

Lemma A.3
.

Let
\mathrm{M}
be a smooth manifold,
X\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathrm{M})
be a smooth vector field on
\mathrm{M}
and
\Phi_{t}^{X}
be the flow associated to
X
. Let
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathrm{M})
. Then,

(A.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{X}Y=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}(\Phi_{-t}^{X})_{*}Y.

Proof.

Let
\Phi_{s}^{Y}
denote the flow of
Y
, with
\Phi_{0}^{Y}=I_{\mathrm{M}}
. For all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we recall that

(A.3)

\displaystyle(\Phi_{-t}^{X})_{*}Y

\displaystyle:=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\Big|_{s=0}\left(\Phi_{-t}^{X}\circ\Phi_{s}^{Y}\circ\Phi_{t}^{X}\right)=(d\Phi_{-t}^{X})|_{\Phi_{t}^{X}}(Y(\Phi_{t}^{X})).

Hence, the Lemma follows from taking
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}
above.
∎

A.2.
The commutator lift and the lifted commutator

Let
\mathrm{M}
be a
d
-dimensional manifold. We say that
\mathrm{B}
is a fiber bundle over
\mathrm{M}
with model fiber
\mathrm{F}
, if
\mathrm{B}
is a topological space and there exists a continuous surjective map
{\pi}:\mathrm{B}\rightarrow\mathrm{M}
such that for each
\mathrm{x}\in\mathrm{M}
there exists a neighbourhood
\mathrm{U}
of
\mathrm{x}
in
\mathrm{M}
and a homeomorphism
\Psi:\pi^{-1}(\mathrm{U})\rightarrow\mathrm{U}\times\mathrm{F}
such that the chain

(A.4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}\overset{\pi}{\longleftarrow}\pi^{-1}(\mathrm{U})\overset{\Psi}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{U}\times\mathrm{F}\overset{\pi_{1}}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{U}

commutes. Let
\Phi_{t}
be a smooth local diffeomorphism on
\mathrm{U}
such that
\Phi_{0}=Id
. Then, for all
\mathrm{U}_{0}\subset\subset\mathrm{U}
there exists
t_{0}>0
such that
\Phi_{t}(\mathrm{U}_{0})\subset\subset\mathrm{U}
, for all
|t|<t_{0}
. The lift of
\Phi_{t}
to
\mathrm{B}
is locally given by
\widehat{\Phi_{t}}=\pi_{1}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{t}\circ\pi_{1}
, where
\pi_{1}
is the projection onto
\mathrm{U}
of the local trivialisation
\mathrm{U}\times\mathrm{F}
of
\mathrm{B}
. Then, given any two smooth local diffeomorphisms
\Phi_{t}^{1}
and
\Phi_{t}^{2}
on
\mathrm{U}
with
\Phi_{0}^{1}=\Phi_{0}^{2}=Id
, we have that
\Phi_{s}^{2}\Phi_{t}^{1}:\mathrm{U}_{0}\rightarrow\mathrm{U}
and

(A.5)

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi_{s}^{2}\circ\Phi_{t}^{1}}=\widehat{\Phi_{s}^{2}}\circ\widehat{\Phi_{t}^{1}}

for all
|t|\leq t_{0}
and
|s|\leq s_{0}
for some
t_{0},s_{0}>0
.
Let
X\in T(\mathrm{M})
have flow
\Phi_{t}^{X}
and let
\widetilde{\Phi_{t}^{X}}
denote its local lift to
\mathrm{B}
. We then define the lifted vector field
\widetilde{X}
on
T\mathrm{B}
locally by

(A.6)

\widetilde{X}:=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\widehat{\Phi_{t}^{X}}.

The next result shows that the commutator of lifted vector fields coincides with the lift of their commutator.

Lemma A.4
.

There holds
[\widetilde{X},\widetilde{Y}]=\widetilde{[X,Y]}
, for all
X,Y\in T\mathrm{M}
.

Proof.

Let
X,Y\in T\mathrm{M}
, let
\Phi_{t}^{X}
and
\Phi_{s}^{Y}
denote their flows and
\widehat{\Phi_{t}^{X}}
,
\widehat{\Phi_{s}^{Y}}
their lifted flows. We begin by noting that

(A.7)

\widehat{\left(\Phi_{t}^{X}\circ\Phi_{s}^{Y}\circ\Phi_{-t}^{X}\right)}=\widehat{\Phi_{t}^{X}}\circ\widehat{\Phi_{s}^{Y}}\circ\widehat{\Phi_{-t}^{X}}

for all
t,s\in{\mathbb{R}}
small enough. Moreover, since
\Phi_{t}^{X}
is a local diffeomorphism and
\Phi_{s}^{Y}
is the flow of the vector field
Y\in T\mathrm{M}
, we have from Proposition
A.1
that

(A.8)

\displaystyle(\widehat{\Phi^{X}})_{*}\widetilde{Y}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\Big|_{s=0}\widehat{\Phi^{X}_{t}}\circ\widehat{\Phi^{Y}_{s}}\circ\widehat{\Phi^{X}_{-t}}.

On the other hand, let
Z_{t}:=(\Phi_{t}^{X})_{*}Y\in T\mathrm{M}
be the push-forward of
Y
by the local diffeomorphism
\Phi_{t}^{X}
generated by
X
for
|t|\leq t_{0}
for some
t_{0}>0
and let
\Phi_{s}^{Z_{t}}
denote its flow, that is,

(A.9)

\displaystyle\Phi_{s}^{Z_{t}}=\Phi_{t}^{X}\circ\Phi_{s}^{Y}\circ\Phi_{-t}^{X}.

Then, we have that

(A.10)

\displaystyle\widetilde{(\Phi_{t}^{X})_{*}Y}=\widetilde{Z_{t}}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\Big|_{s=0}\widehat{\Phi_{s}^{Z_{t}}}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\Big|_{s=0}\left(\widehat{\Phi_{t}^{X}\circ\Phi_{s}^{Y}\circ\Phi_{-t}^{X}}\right),

and, from (
A.7
), we conclude that
(\widehat{\Phi_{t}^{X}})_{*}\widetilde{Y}\;=\;\widetilde{(\Phi_{t}^{X})_{*}Y}
. Next, from Lemma
A.3
we have that

(A.11)

\displaystyle[X,Y]=\mathcal{L}_{X}Y=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}(\Phi_{-t}^{X})_{*}Y=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\Big|_{s=0}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}\Phi_{s}^{Z_{t}}

and thus

(A.12)

\displaystyle\widetilde{[X,Y]}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\Big|_{r=0}\widehat{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}\Phi_{r}^{Z_{t}}}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\Big|_{r=0}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}\widehat{\Phi_{r}^{Z_{t}}}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\Big|_{r=0}\widehat{\Phi_{r}^{Z_{t}}}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}\widetilde{(\Phi_{-t}^{X})_{*}Y}.

Likewise, since

(A.13)

\displaystyle[\widetilde{X},\widetilde{Y}]=\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{X}}\widetilde{Y}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}(\widehat{\Phi_{-t}^{X}})_{*}\widetilde{Y}

we conclude that
[\widetilde{X},\widetilde{Y}]=\widetilde{[X,Y]}
.
∎

A.3.
Commutators on a reference frame

In this section we expand on the computations used in Proposition
6.1
. We set

(A.14)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{x}}_{0}=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},0\right),\qquad{\mathbf{e}}_{1}=(0,0,1),\qquad{\mathbf{e}}_{2}=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},0\right),

and we consider the orthonormal and unit-area basis
E_{0}=({\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2})
of
T_{{\mathbf{x}}_{0}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
. We observe that
\{{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\}
is an oriented orthonormal basis of
T_{{\mathbf{x}}_{0}}{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
. We note that
\widetilde{\phi_{t}^{u_{\boldsymbol{a}}}}
and
\widetilde{\phi_{t}^{u_{\boldsymbol{b}}}}
defined in (
7.9
) are the local lifted flows of
\phi_{t}^{u^{\boldsymbol{a}}}
and
\phi_{t}^{u^{\boldsymbol{b}}}
on the local trivialisation
{\mathcal{U}}\times SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
of the fiber bundle
SL({{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
.

We recall that for
{\boldsymbol{a}}=(0,a_{2},a_{3})\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
and
{\boldsymbol{b}}=(0,0,1)
we have

(A.15)

\displaystyle u^{\boldsymbol{a}}({\mathbf{x}})=(a_{2}y+a_{3}z)\left((a_{2}z-a_{3}y),a_{3}x,-a_{2}x\right),\quad u^{\boldsymbol{b}}({\mathbf{x}})=z(-y,x,0),

for all
{\mathbf{x}}=(x,y,z)\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
. Moreover, for any
w\in T{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
we have
5
5

5

We also abuse notation by denoting a smooth extension on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
of
w
by
w
itself.

(A.16)

\displaystyle\Omega_{w}({\mathbf{x}}_{0},E_{0})=E_{0}^{T}(D_{\mathbf{x}}w)({\mathbf{x}}_{0})E_{0}\in\mathfrak{sl}(2,{\mathbb{R}}).

Let
w_{1}({\mathbf{x}})=u^{\boldsymbol{a}}({\mathbf{x}})
and
w_{2}({\mathbf{x}})=u^{\boldsymbol{b}}({\mathbf{x}})
. Then,
w_{1}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=-\frac{a_{2}^{2}}{2}{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-a_{2}a_{3}\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}{\mathbf{e}}_{2}
and

(A.17)

\displaystyle(D_{\mathbf{x}}w_{1})({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{a_{2}a_{3}}{2\sqrt{2}}&-\frac{3a_{2}a_{3}}{2\sqrt{2}}&\frac{a_{2}^{2}-2a_{3}^{2}}{2\sqrt{2}}\\
\frac{a_{2}a_{3}}{2\sqrt{2}}&\frac{3a_{2}a_{3}}{2\sqrt{2}}&-\frac{a_{2}^{2}-2a_{3}^{2}}{2\sqrt{2}}\\
-\frac{a_{2}^{2}}{\sqrt{2}}&-\frac{a_{2}^{2}}{\sqrt{2}}&-\frac{a_{2}a_{3}}{\sqrt{2}}\end{pmatrix},\quad\Omega_{w_{1}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{a_{2}a_{3}}{\sqrt{2}}&0\\
\frac{a_{2}^{2}}{2}-a_{3}^{2}&\frac{a_{2}a_{3}}{\sqrt{2}}\end{pmatrix}

and
w_{2}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=0
, with

(A.18)

\displaystyle(D_{\mathbf{x}}w_{2})({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\\
0&0&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\Omega_{w_{2}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}.

For
w_{3}({\mathbf{x}})=[w_{1},w_{2}]({\mathbf{x}})
we have

(A.19)

\displaystyle w_{3}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=\frac{a_{2}^{2}}{2}{\mathbf{e}}_{2},\quad(D_{\mathbf{x}}w_{3})({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{3a_{2}^{2}}{4}&\frac{3a_{2}^{2}}{4}&a_{2}a_{3}\\
-\frac{3a_{2}^{2}}{4}&-\frac{3a_{2}^{2}}{4}&-a_{2}a_{3}\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\Omega_{w_{3}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
\sqrt{2}a_{2}a_{3}&0\end{pmatrix}.

Next, for
w_{4}({\mathbf{x}})=[w_{1},w_{3}]({\mathbf{x}})
there holds
w_{4}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=-\frac{3\sqrt{2}}{4}a_{2}^{3}a_{3}{\mathbf{e}}_{2}
and

(A.20)

\displaystyle(D_{\mathbf{x}}w_{4})({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=a_{2}^{2}\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{11a_{2}a_{3}}{4\sqrt{2}}&-\frac{13a_{2}a_{3}}{4\sqrt{2}}&a_{2}^{2}\sqrt{2}-\frac{3a_{3}^{2}}{\sqrt{2}}\\
\frac{11a_{2}a_{3}}{4\sqrt{2}}&\frac{13a_{2}a_{3}}{4\sqrt{2}}&-a_{2}^{2}\sqrt{2}+\frac{3a_{3}^{2}}{\sqrt{2}}\\
-a_{2}^{2}\sqrt{2}&a_{2}^{2}\sqrt{2}&-\frac{a_{2}a_{3}}{2\sqrt{2}}\end{pmatrix},\quad\Omega_{w_{4}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=a_{2}^{2}\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{a_{2}a_{3}}{2\sqrt{2}}&-2a_{2}^{2}\\
2a_{2}^{2}-3a_{3}^{2}&\frac{a_{2}a_{3}}{2\sqrt{2}}\end{pmatrix}.

Finally, for
w_{5}({\mathbf{x}})=[w_{2},w_{4}]({\mathbf{x}})
we have
w_{5}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=0
and

(A.21)

\displaystyle(D_{\mathbf{x}}w_{5})({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=a_{2}^{2}\begin{pmatrix}-a_{2}^{2}&a_{2}^{2}&-\frac{a_{2}a_{3}}{2}\\
a_{2}^{2}&-a_{2}^{2}&\frac{a_{2}a_{3}}{2}\\
0&0&2a_{2}^{2}\end{pmatrix},\quad\Omega_{w_{5}}({\mathbf{x}}_{0})=\begin{pmatrix}2a_{2}^{4}&0\\
-\frac{a_{2}^{3}a_{3}}{\sqrt{2}}&-2a_{2}^{4}\end{pmatrix}.

Appendix B
Leading order expansion for commutator loops

This appendix develops the small-time asymptotic expansion of commutator loop maps built from the local flows of a finite family of smooth vector fields
{\mathcal{V}}=\{V_{1},\dots,V_{m}\}\subset\mathfrak{X}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
treated in Section
4
.
The main goal is to justify the Lie-algebraic heuristic that concatenations of iterated flow maps, defined in (
4.9
), generate the corresponding iterated Lie brackets at leading order: for every multi-index
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})
,

(B.1)

\Gamma_{t}^{I}(x)=x+t^{|I|}V_{I}(x)+t^{|I|+1}{\mathcal{E}}_{I}(t,x),\qquad V_{I}=[V_{i_{1}},[V_{i_{2}},\dots,[V_{i_{n-1}},V_{i_{n}}]\dots]],

with a locally uniformly bounded remainder
{\mathcal{E}}_{I}
.

From
[
32
]
*Ch. 9, given a smooth vector field
V\in\mathfrak{X}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
the Fundamental Theorem on flows ensures the existence of a unique smooth maximal flow
\Phi\colon\mathrm{D}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
on an open flow domain
\mathrm{D}\subset{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, with

(B.2)

\partial_{t}\Phi_{t}(x)=V(\Phi_{t}(x)),\quad\Phi_{0}(x)=x.

In particular, in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
the chain rule yields the usual ODE identity

(B.3)

\partial_{tt}\Phi_{t}(x)=DV(\Phi_{t}(x))[V(\Phi_{t}(x))],

hence at
t=0
one has
\partial_{t}\Phi_{0}(x)=V(x)
and
\partial_{tt}\Phi_{0}(x)=DV(x)[V(x)]
.
Fixing a compact
K\Subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, openness of
\mathrm{D}
gives a uniform
t_{0}>0
with
[-t_{0},t_{0}]\times K\subset\mathrm{D}
, so that applying the one-dimensional Taylor theorem in time to
t\mapsto\Phi_{t}(x)
(componentwise) produces the expansion

(B.4)

\Phi_{t}(x)=x+tV(x)+\frac{t^{2}}{2}DV(x)[V(x)]+t^{3}{\mathcal{E}}(t,x),

with
\|{\mathcal{E}}(t,x)\|_{C^{1}([-t_{0},t_{0}]\times K)}\lesssim 1
.
As a direct consequence we obtain on
[-t_{0},t_{0}]\times K
also

(B.5)

D\Phi_{t}(x)={\mathbb{I}}+tDV(x)+t^{2}{\mathcal{S}}(t,x),

where
\|{\mathcal{S}}(t,x)\|_{L^{\infty}([-t_{0},t_{0}]\times K)}\lesssim 1
.
Building on this standard setup, the following lemma establishes the leading order expansion for vector fields defined via iterated Lie brackets.

Lemma B.1

(Taylor expansion of commutator loops)
.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}=\{V_{1},\dots,V_{m}\}\subset\mathfrak{X}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
be smooth vector fields with (local) flows
\Phi_{t}^{i}
. For a multi-index
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})\in\{1,\dots,m\}^{n}
define the iterated commutator vector field

(B.6)

V_{I}:=[V_{i_{1}},[V_{i_{2}},\dots,[V_{i_{n-1}},V_{i_{n}}]\dots]],

and define the associated commutator loop maps
\Gamma_{t}^{I}
inductively by

(B.7)

\Gamma_{t}^{(i)}:=\Phi_{t}^{i},\qquad\Gamma_{t}^{(j,I)}:=\Gamma_{t}^{(j)}\circ\Gamma_{t}^{I}\circ(\Gamma_{t}^{(j)})^{-1}\circ(\Gamma_{t}^{I})^{-1}.

Then for every compact
K\Subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
there exists
t_{0}>0
and maps
{\mathcal{E}}_{I}:[-t_{0},t_{0}]\times K\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
(continuous in
(t,x)
and smooth in
x
) such that for all
x\in K
and
|t|\leq t_{0}
(for which all compositions are defined),

(B.8)

\Gamma_{t}^{I}(x)=x+t^{n}V_{I}(x)+t^{n+1}{\mathcal{E}}_{I}(t,x),\qquad n=|I|.

As preliminaries, we state and prove two auxiliary lemmas which play an important role in the proof of Lemma
B.1
.

Lemma B.2
.

Let
K\Subset U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, let
n\geq 1
, and suppose that

(B.9)

F_{t}(x)=x+t^{n}A(x)+t^{n+1}R(t,x),\qquad x\in U,

where
A\in C^{1}(U;{\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and

(B.10)

R\in C^{1}([-t_{0},t_{0}]\times U;{\mathbb{R}}^{d}),\qquad\sup_{|t|\leq t_{0}}\left\lVert R(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{C^{1}(U)}<\infty.

Then, after possibly shrinking
t_{0}>0
, the map
F_{t}
is a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism from a neighbourhood of
K
onto its image. Moreover, for every
y\in F_{t}(K)\subset U
, its inverse satisfies

(B.11)

F_{t}^{-1}(y)=y-t^{n}A(y)+t^{n+1}\widetilde{R}(t,y),

where
\widetilde{R}
is uniformly bounded for
|t|\leq t_{0}
and
y\in F_{t}(K)
.

Proof.

Upon shrinking
U
and picking
t_{0}
sufficiently small, the
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism claim follows via standard arguments from

(B.12)

D_{x}F_{t}(x)=I+t^{n}DA(x)+t^{n+1}D_{x}R(t,x).

It remains to prove the expansion for the inverse. Let
y\in F_{t}(K)
, and set
x:=F_{t}^{-1}(y)\in K
, so that

(B.13)

y=x+t^{n}A(x)+t^{n+1}R(t,x).

In particular, there holds
|x-y|\leq C|t|^{n}
for some universal
C>0
. We rewrite the above as

(B.14)

x-y+t^{n}A(y)=-t^{n}\bigl(A(x)-A(y)\bigr)-t^{n+1}R(t,x),

and, using the
C^{1}
-regularity of
A
, the estimate
|x-y|\leq C|t|^{n}
,
2n\geq n+1
, and the boundedness of
R
we obtain

(B.15)

x-y+t^{n}A(y)=O(t^{n+1})

uniformly for
y\in F_{t}(K)
.
Equivalently, there exists a uniformly bounded function
\widetilde{R}(t,y)
such that

(B.16)

F_{t}^{-1}(y)=y-t^{n}A(y)+t^{n+1}\widetilde{R}(t,y),

which proves (
B.11
).
∎

Lemma B.3
.

Fix
j
and let
\Phi_{t}^{j}
be the flow of
V_{j}
.
For any smooth vector field
W
there exists a bounded remainder
{\mathcal{R}}_{j,W}(t,\cdot)
on
K
such that for all
y\in K
,

(B.17)

D\Phi_{t}^{j}(\Phi_{-t}^{j}(y))W(\Phi_{-t}^{j}(y))=W(y)+t[V_{j},W](y)+t^{2}{\mathcal{R}}_{j,W}(t,y).

Proof of Lemma
B.3
.

Using (
B.5
) at
x=\Phi_{-t}^{j}(y)
and Taylor expanding
W(\Phi_{-t}^{j}(y))
in space around
y
(noting
\Phi_{-t}^{j}(y)=y-tV_{j}(y)+O(t^{2})
from (
B.4
)), we get

(B.18)

W(\Phi_{-t}^{j}(y))=W(y)-tDW(y)[V_{j}(y)]+t^{2}R_{1}(t,y),

and

(B.19)

D\Phi_{t}^{j}(\Phi_{-t}^{j}(y))={\mathbb{I}}+tDV_{j}(y)+t^{2}R_{2}(t,y),

with bounded
R_{1},R_{2}
on
[-t_{0},t_{0}]\times K
.
Multiplying the two expansions gives

(B.20)

D\Phi_{t}^{j}(\Phi_{-t}^{j}(y))W(\Phi_{-t}^{j}(y))=W(y)+t\big(DV_{j}(y)[W(y)]-DW(y)[V_{j}(y)]\big)+t^{2}{\mathcal{R}}_{j,W}(t,y),

and the coefficient of
t
is exactly
[V_{j},W](y)
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
B.1
.

Fix a compact set
K\Subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. We will work for
|t|\leq t_{0}
so small that all flows appearing in the induction are defined on the relevant compact neighbourhoods; since each
\Phi_{t}^{i}
depends smoothly on
(t,x)
, such a uniform
t_{0}>0
exists after possibly shrinking.
The proof is carried out via induction on
n=|I|
in three steps: the base case
n=1
, the first commutator case
n=2
, and the induction step from
n\geq 2
to
n+1
.

Base case
n=1
.

If
I=(i)
then
\Gamma_{t}^{I}=\Phi_{t}^{i}
, and (
B.8
) is exactly (
B.4
) with
V_{I}=V_{i}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{I}(t,x):=\frac{1}{2}DV_{i}(x)[V_{i}(x)]+t{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(t,x)
.
Thus (
B.8
) holds for
n=1
.

First commutator case
n=2
.

Let
I=(j,i)
, so that
V_{I}=[V_{j},V_{i}]
and

(B.21)

\Gamma_{t}^{I}=\Gamma_{t}^{(j)}\circ\Gamma_{t}^{(i)}\circ(\Gamma_{t}^{(j)})^{-1}\circ(\Gamma_{t}^{(i)})^{-1}=\Phi_{t}^{j}\circ\Phi_{t}^{i}\circ\Phi_{-t}^{j}\circ\Phi_{-t}^{i}.

Fix
x\in K
and set
y:=\Phi_{-t}^{i}(x)
so that, by (
B.4
),
y=x-tV_{i}(x)+O(t^{2})
.
Next,

(B.22)

\Phi_{-t}^{j}(y)=y-tV_{j}(y)+\frac{t^{2}}{2}DV_{j}(y)[V_{j}(y)]+O(t^{3}).

Using
y=x-tV_{i}(x)+O(t^{2})
and Taylor expanding
V_{j}(y)
around
x
, we get

(B.23)

\displaystyle\Phi_{-t}^{j}\circ\Phi_{-t}^{i}(x)

\displaystyle=x-tV_{i}(x)-tV_{j}(x)+t^{2}DV_{j}(x)[V_{i}(x)]+\frac{t^{2}}{2}DV_{i}(x)[V_{i}(x)]+\frac{t^{2}}{2}DV_{j}(x)[V_{j}(x)]+O(t^{3}).

Applying
\Phi_{t}^{i}
to this expression and using (
B.4
) again gives

(B.24)

\displaystyle\Phi_{t}^{i}\circ\Phi_{-t}^{j}\circ\Phi_{-t}^{i}(x)

\displaystyle=x-tV_{j}(x)+t^{2}\left(DV_{j}(x)[V_{i}(x)]-DV_{i}(x)[V_{j}(x)]+\frac{1}{2}DV_{j}(x)[V_{j}(x)]\right)+O(t^{3}).

Finally, applying
\Phi_{t}^{j}
gives

(B.25)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{t}^{I}(x)

\displaystyle=x+t^{2}\left(DV_{j}(x)[V_{i}(x)]-DV_{i}(x)[V_{j}(x)]\right)+O(t^{3})

(B.26)

\displaystyle=x+t^{2}[V_{j},V_{i}](x)+O(t^{3}).

Therefore, since
V_{I}=[V_{j},V_{i}]
,

(B.27)

\Gamma_{t}^{I}(x)=x+t^{2}V_{I}(x)+t^{3}{\mathcal{E}}_{I}(t,x),

for a bounded remainder
{\mathcal{E}}_{I}
on
[-t_{0},t_{0}]\times K
.
Thus (
B.8
) holds for all multi-indices of length
2
.

Inductive step.

Assume (
B.8
) holds for some
I
of length
n\geq 2
:

(B.28)

\Gamma_{t}^{I}(x)=x+t^{n}V_{I}(x)+t^{n+1}{\mathcal{E}}_{I}(t,x)\qquad(x\in K).

Fix
j\in\{1,\dots,m\}
and set
J:=(j,I)
so that
V_{J}=[V_{j},V_{I}]
and

(B.29)

\Gamma_{t}^{J}=\Gamma_{t}^{(j)}\circ\Gamma_{t}^{I}\circ(\Gamma_{t}^{(j)})^{-1}\circ(\Gamma_{t}^{I})^{-1}.

Write
y:=(\Gamma_{t}^{I})^{-1}(x)
. By Lemma
B.2
,

(B.30)

y=x-t^{n}V_{I}(x)+t^{n+1}\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{I}(t,x)

for some bounded
\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{I}
, and consider the conjugated map

(B.31)

C_{t}:=\Gamma_{t}^{(j)}\circ\Gamma_{t}^{I}\circ(\Gamma_{t}^{(j)})^{-1}.

Using the inductive hypothesis for
\Gamma_{t}^{I}(x)
at
(\Gamma_{t}^{(j)})^{-1}(y)
gives

(B.32)

\Gamma_{t}^{I}((\Gamma_{t}^{(j)})^{-1}(y))=(\Gamma_{t}^{(j)})^{-1}(y)+t^{n}V_{I}((\Gamma_{t}^{(j)})^{-1}(y))+t^{n+1}{\mathcal{E}}_{I}(t,(\Gamma_{t}^{(j)})^{-1}(y)).

Set
\delta:=t^{n}V_{I}((\Gamma_{t}^{(j)})^{-1}(y))+t^{n+1}{\mathcal{E}}_{I}(t,(\Gamma_{t}^{(j)})^{-1}(y))
, so
\delta=O(t^{n})
.
Now expand
\Gamma_{t}^{(j)}
in the space variable around
(\Gamma_{t}^{(j)})^{-1}(y)
:

(B.33)

\Gamma_{t}^{(j)}((\Gamma_{t}^{(j)})^{-1}(y)+\delta)=\Gamma_{t}^{(j)}((\Gamma_{t}^{(j)})^{-1}(y))+D\Gamma_{t}^{(j)}((\Gamma_{t}^{(j)})^{-1}(y))[\delta]+O(|\delta|^{2}).

Since
\Gamma_{t}^{(j)}((\Gamma_{t}^{(j)})^{-1}(y))=y
and
|\delta|^{2}=O(t^{2n})
, we get

(B.34)

C_{t}(y)=y+D\Gamma_{t}^{(j)}((\Gamma_{t}^{(j)})^{-1}(y))[\delta]+t^{2n}{\mathcal{R}}(t,y).

Since
n\geq 2
,
t^{2n}=O(t^{n+2})
and therefore this quadratic term is automatically beyond the order we need.
Thus,

(B.35)

\displaystyle C_{t}(y)

\displaystyle=y+t^{n}D\Gamma_{t}^{(j)}((\Gamma_{t}^{(j)})^{-1}(y))V_{I}((\Gamma_{t}^{(j)})^{-1}(y))+t^{n+1}D\Gamma_{t}^{(j)}((\Gamma_{t}^{(j)})^{-1}(y)){\mathcal{E}}_{I}(t,(\Gamma_{t}^{(j)})^{-1}(y))+t^{n+2}{\mathcal{R}}_{1}(t,y).

Apply Lemma
B.3
with
W=V_{I}
to the first term and use (
B.5
) to handle the second term (it contributes only
t^{n+1}{\mathcal{E}}_{I}(t,y)
up to
O(t^{n+2})
). We obtain

(B.36)

C_{t}(y)=y+t^{n}V_{I}(y)+t^{n+1}\big([V_{j},V_{I}](y)+{\mathcal{E}}_{I}(t,y)\big)+t^{n+2}{\mathcal{R}}_{2}(t,y).

Substituting
\Gamma_{t}^{I}(y)=y+t^{n}V_{I}(y)+t^{n+1}{\mathcal{E}}_{I}(t,y)
from the inductive hypothesis into (
B.36
) yields

(B.37)

C_{t}(y)=\Gamma_{t}^{I}(y)+t^{n+1}[V_{j},V_{I}](y)+t^{n+2}{\mathcal{R}}_{2}(t,y).

Finally,

(B.38)

\Gamma_{t}^{J}(x)=C_{t}\big((\Gamma_{t}^{I})^{-1}(x)\big)=x+t^{n+1}[V_{j},V_{I}]\big((\Gamma_{t}^{I})^{-1}(x)\big)+t^{n+2}{\mathcal{R}}_{3}(t,x).

Since
(\Gamma_{t}^{I})^{-1}(x)=x+O(t^{n})
by (
B.30
) and
[V_{j},V_{I}]
is smooth, we have

(B.39)

\displaystyle t^{n+1}[V_{j},V_{I}]\big((\Gamma_{t}^{I})^{-1}(x)\big)=t^{n+1}[V_{j},V_{I}](x)+O(t^{2n+1})=t^{n+1}[V_{j},V_{I}](x)+O(t^{n+2})

for
n\geq 2
. Thus,

(B.40)

\Gamma_{t}^{J}(x)=x+t^{n+1}[V_{j},V_{I}](x)+t^{n+2}{\mathcal{E}}_{J}(t,x),

for a bounded remainder
{\mathcal{E}}_{J}
on
[-t_{0},t_{0}]\times K
.
This is exactly (
B.8
) for the multi-index
J
of length
n+1
. The induction is complete.
∎
